## Stage 2 Abstract (≈ 400 words)

Eric R. Williams' *Screen Adaptation: Beyond the Basics* (2018) bridges screenwriting pedagogy, narrative theory, and professional practice to offer a unified model for transforming stories across media. The book positions adaptation not as replication but as creative translation—a process of re‑imagining ideas through the grammar of film and television.

Williams’ pedagogy unfolds across three progressive sections—Basic, Intermediate, and Advanced Practices. The first demystifies adaptation through comparative case studies (e.g., *Shrek!* from children’s literature to feature film; *American Splendor* from graphic novel to cinema; and *Memento* from short story to fractured narrative). These examples clarify that adaptation involves invention at every level—structure, tone, and theme. Legal and industrial frameworks (rights acquisition, intellectual property, public domain) contextualize adaptation as both art and enterprise.

The Intermediate section introduces Williams’ original frameworks: the Triangle of Knowledge (tension model balancing what the audience, author, and character know), the Character Spine (motivation → objective → intention → action), and the Primary/Secondary Emotion model distinguishing character emotion from audience emotion. Together, these systems transform intuitive craft into teachable methodology—adaptation as narrative engineering.

In the Advanced section, Williams expands his Screenwriters Taxonomy—eleven super‑genres and over two hundred micro‑genres—demonstrating how genre operates as an adaptive ecosystem. The Kortlander–Williams Matrix situates theme as a dynamic interplay between didactic and active expression, linking moral choice to story design. The book culminates in adaptation case studies from *Voices from the Heartland*, presenting original texts, screenplay transformations, and reflective commentary—showing how theory becomes professional decision‑making.

For screenwriters, producers, and educators, *Screen Adaptation: Beyond the Basics* offers a repeatable system for creative decision‑making grounded in experience rather than formula. Its balance of rigor and flexibility makes it essential to any adaptation toolkit, securing its place within #adaptation-theory, #screenwriting-pedagogy, and #narrative-engineering.

Screen Adaptation: Beyond
the Basics
Once you understand the basics of screenwriting, ideas for your next screenplay are everywhere. Whether it comes from a favorite children�s book, a summer novel you discover accidentally, a news story that catches your imagination, or a chapter from your own life�advanced screenwriting strategies should now guide you through your first adaptation. In Screen Adaptation: Beyond the Basics, award-winning screenwriter Eric R. Williams uses examples from award-winning screenplays to explain new storytelling techniques. His real-world examples illustrate a range of advanced approaches�including new ways to identify and craft tension, how to reimagine structure and character, and how to strengthen emotional depth in your characters and in the audience. This book teaches readers new ways to engage with source material in order to make successful adaptation decisions, regardless of the source material.
The book offers:
�
Three detailed examples of award-winning adaptations by the author, including the complete short story and final scripts used in the Voices from the Heartland project;

�
Breakout boxes highlighting modern and historical adaptations and providing examples for each concept discussed in the book;

�
More than fifty charts providing easy-to-use visual representations of complex concepts;

�
New screenwriting techniques developed by the author, including the Triangle of Knowledge, the Storyteller�s Parallax, and the idea of Super Genres as part of a Screenwriters Taxonomy.

Eric R. Williams� screenplays have received the Best New Work award from the Writers Guild of America, an Emmy Award from the National Academy of Television Arts and Sciences, and the Award for Individual Excellence in Screenwriting from the Ohio Arts Council. He has adapted novels by Luis Urrea (Across the Wire), Bill Littlefield (Prospect), and Guillermo Fadanelli (The Other Face of Rock Hudson). His latest projects include an adaptation of the anthology Voices from the Heartland into a thirteen-part television series. Eric received his MFA from Columbia University�s School of Film. He currently teaches at Ohio University, where he has received the University Professor Award for Excellence in Teaching.
You can learn more by visiting his website: www.WilliamsOnStory.com.

Preface

As a professor and a professional screenwriter, I am torn and strengthened by having my feet in both worlds. A good screenwriter�especially one undertaking an adaptation�needs to be able to collaborate with other writers, producers, and directors about story, structure, character, and thematic interpretations. Students can be as demanding as producers. A professor must teach these practices to young screenwriters. Often, students (and producers) believe that screenwriting is an act accomplished alone. It is not. Screenwriting is a dance. It can be rehearsed, discussed, presented, experienced, and revamped. Of course, you can dance by yourself (many do), but the most rewarding dances involve more than one person. When hired, a screenwriter needs to understand the expectations of the producer, of the audience, and of himself (and writing partner). The same is true for a student, with the teacher standing in as producer, mentor, and audience.
As a writer, I frequently isolate certain elements of the adaptation process so that the script can be examined under different lenses. I discuss these elements with my students too. The process is very similar to going to the ophthalmologist. Neophyte screenwriters expect to work in isolation until they turn in their screenplay for the professor to grade it�or the producer to judge it�in its entirety. They want to know that their work is �good,� and what if anything will make the screenplay �better.� They look at their work holistically, like a cake, as if the answer might be: �add more frosting.� This is an amateur approach.
Rather, think of the ophthalmologist: placing different optical variants in front of your eyes, asking �Do these make your vision better or worse?� You answer. And a new lens snaps into place: �Better or worse?� And so it goes as your doctor tests each variant in your eye until, eventually, the device is removed and you are prescribed a set of glasses. Each aspect of screenwriting is its own set of variants. When critiquing a script, the question should not be, �Is it any good?� but rather, �Will an adjustment to the dialogue make it better or worse?� Character: �Better or worse?�
Structural adjustment, thematic refinement, or a change to genre expectations: �Better or worse?� These are the questions I prefer to discuss with a producer � a colleague � myself, when I write.
Adaptations are a tough racket, but I believe in them. They are tough because, unless you personally own the rights to the underlying material, writing them requires doing work for hire. If the script isn�t produced, you can�t shop the material yourself. It�s a dead project (as far as the screenwriter is concerned). Yet, I believe in them because an adaptation gets to the heart of the collaborative process. The screenwriter really gets to roll up his or her sleeves and mull over an idea with producers, directors, and sometimes the original writer. I find this incredibly rewarding. Some of my best work has come through adaptation. Others, I believe, would say the same. In 2005, the Writers Guild of America surveyed its members, asking them to determine the 101 Best Screenplays of all time. More than half of the screenplays chosen for this honor are adaptations; including five of the top ten, three of the top five, and both of the top two.

Adaptation is an art: an art in and of itself. Each year, two Academy Awards are given to screenwriters: one to those who write original screenplays, and one to those who write adaptations. The skill and artistry that goes into the work of each is recognized by one of the highest filmmaking awards. Each year, thousands of people�if not tens of thousands�try to write the great American screenplay. I know that I do. You probably do too. Films that you may not expect are adaptations. Films like: Raging Bull, Vertigo, The Shawshank Redemption, No Country for Old Men and Shrek.
I write professionally. I have for more than twenty years. Some of what I write is in the �original screenplay� category. My script Crystal Messiah, which won one of twelve Best New Work awards from the Writers Guild of America in 2000, was an original piece. I had joined the WGA a year prior, after writing one of the last Columbo murder mysteries for Peter Falk at Universal Studios. Unfortunately, due to his health, it was never produced.
I have also been hired to write a number of adaptations. Academy Award-winning producer Alvaro Donado first hired me to adapt Bill Littlefield�s baseball novel Prospect, and then a series of newspaper articles by Luis Urrea published as Across the Wire: Life and Hard Times on the Mexican Border. After co-writing the script Legendary with director Alfredo Rodriguez de Villa (Nothing Like the Holidays, Fugly!), de Villa again hired me to adapt the Mexican crime novella La Otra Cara de Rock Hudson by Guillermo Fadanelli�a project in process as I write this book.
A well-kept secret is that many screenwriters make a good living from writing, even if their screenplays are not produced. Money is made, and money is paid, for the work a screenwriter writes. It is then the job of the producer to get the film made. Some of my best work, unfortunately, still sits in a dusty office drawer or stored on a hard drive�paid for, but never produced. Oh well. Keep writing, I say. As writers, we must.
Each script gets better. With each script we learn. Brave Run Home�a Sundance Screenplay semi-finalist�was a script that I co-wrote with Jennifer Aktins. In 2008, my students and I won an Emmy Award adapting reality TV shows into an interactive educational program. The next year, I received the Award of Individual Excellence in Screenwriting from the Ohio Arts Council for adapting a set of short stories into a spec television series called Voices from the Heartland. You will find examples of this project (the script and original material) at the end of each section in this book.
I share my successes and failures with you so that you know I have been through this process myself. The ideas in this book are not hypothetical. They are not theoretical. They are, to me, tried and true. This book is an explanation of my process, developed over a quarter century in the screenwriting trenches.
My hope is that you will use this book as a way to organize your thoughts when you are writing an adaptation�and also when you are writing an original script. I don�t believe in painting by numbers. I don�t believe that there is one �best� way to write. What I present to you in these pages is a culmination of twenty-five years of professional writing and teaching experience all rolled into one. It has served me well. I hope that it may it do the same for you.
Eric R. Williams Screenwriter and Professor Athens, Ohio summer, 2017

Beyond the Rules Adaptation

Adaptation is not transcription. This much I know. The process of adapting a screenplay is just as creative as writing a non-fiction novel based upon true events, painting a portrait based upon the person sitting before you, or directing a film based upon the screenplay. Adaptation is the process of reimagining ideas from one medium into the language of another. Adaptation is not transcription.
OceanofPDF.com

The Screenplay Format
This book is designed to move writers beyond the basics, so I presume that you are familiar with the fundamentals of writing a screenplay. If not, David Trottier�s book The Screenwriter�s Bible is an excellent resource. For the sake of discussion in this book, I�d like you to keep in mind three concepts that I believe create the core of the screenplay format. As you will note in the book�s many examples, some screenwriters would say that these concepts are merely suggestions.
1.
A script should be written so that one page of script translates to one minute of screen time. Therefore, a two-minute chase scene should utilize two pages of script; a 120-page screenplay will become a two-hour movie. To standardize this page-per-minute idea, all scripts should be written in twelve-point courier font according to industry standards for spacing.

2.
Every scene begins with a Scene Heading in ALL CAPS, after which the scene is basically a combination of text description and dialogue. A new scene deserves a new Scene Heading. Some screenwriters will indicate scenes within scenes by capitalizing areas within the scene. For instance, at a party that takes place INT. NY LOFT�NIGHT, it would not be unusual to jump around within the scene, moving from the KITCHEN, to FRONT ENTRANCE to BACK STAIRS�without reminding us that we are INT (interior) or that it is NIGHT.

3.
The screenplay should only describe what can be seen or heard by the audience. For example, a character�s backstory or beliefs should not appear in a screenplay. Rarely will you see: �John smiles because he remembers the joke from when he was a child and it makes him happy.� Instead, John would simply �smile� because that is all the information the audience would have when watching the film.

With those rules in mind, a page of script looks like this (in 12-pt. courier font):

TARHEAD � skinny, 16, with a big bunch of black hair crammed inside his hoodie � stands at a SQUEAKY comic book rack, twisting it around and around. SQUEAK SQUEAK SQUEAK.
While SETH BROWNING, � a bland band-geek with a wild mop of curly brown hair � sits behind the counter making French-to-English notecards from a dictionary. SQUEAK SQUEAK.
But he can�t focus because his eyes keep wandering over to Tarhead. And Tarhead isn�t looking at the comics�
His eyes keep wandering over to Seth. SQUEAK SQUEAK.
TARHEAD Hey, where�s Miss Kelly at? (Seth shrugs) Does that mean �you don�t know� or �you don�t
wanna tell me�? (Seth shrugs) Fine. Be that way.

Seth eyes a surveillance monitor behind the counter. CLICK. CLICK. He tracks Tarhead�s furtive movements.
Tarhead�s not really buying anything he�s just� traipsing.

Adaptation is one of the few art forms where the final product (the script) is rarely seen by the general audience. Instead, they see the film. This is similar to an architect whose blueprints are never seen although the building is widely enjoyed. Oddly though, with an adapted screenplay the audience has had the opportunity to enjoy the:

�

�
Stage Play.

As each medium has its own creative nuance and purpose, it is important for screenwriters to differentiate the media they are adapting from the screenplay they are trying to write.

Children�s Book�Expanding Simplicity
The original source material for the screenplay Shrek is a picture book with no more than twenty-six pages. Most children�s books include pictures, simple relationships, and a happy ending. The primary metamorphosis from children�s book to feature-length screenplay involves expanding this simple world from a bite-sized snack into a full meal.
William Steig�s children�s book Shrek! created a simple relationship between Shrek (a hideous ogre) and his new sidekick (a talking Donkey). This duo travels together on a quest to rescue a princess. The story moves quickly and does not spend much time on character motivations. When Shrek randomly meets the verbose Donkey�a meeting foretold by a Witch at the start of the book�they are quickly on their way together with very little character development. The scene from the book, in its entirety, reads:
�You jabbering jackass!� Shrek screamed. �Aren�t you supposed to take me somewhere?� �I am. To the nutty knight. Who guards the entrance. To the crazy castle. Where the repulsive princess. Waits.� �Then take!� Shrek shrieked, and he hopped onto the donkey�s back.
Ted Elliott, Terry Rossio, Joe Stillman, and Roger S. H. Schulman adapted William Steig�s children�s book Shrek! into the animated film Shrek. The screenplay won the Best Adapted Screenplay award from the British Academy of Film and Television Arts, and won an Academy Award for Best Animated Feature.
However, when adapting Shrek! the screenwriters needed to create a much richer world to captivate their audience for two hours. And, as a DreamWorks Animation film, the script needed to appeal to more than just children. A complex world with a wide variety of entertaining characters was subsequently developed. To engage adults, they satirized Disney characters and used them to populate their world. These characters, in turn, illustrate Shrek�s motives for undertaking his trek and establish his relationship with Donkey. In this scene, Steig�s single page has been expanded into three minutes of screenplay.

Graphic Novel/Comic Book�Adding Movement
Screenwriters Shari Springer Berman and Robert Pulcini had different issues to address when adapting American Splendor. Instead of expanding the story, they needed to whittle back years of comic book scenarios into one concise script. They also had to consider how to add movement to the story. Illustrations are still images, but they imply movement�both physical and emotional. It is the screenwriter�s challenge to translate the implied movement from the page into active movement on the screen. With a superhero story, the movement is easy to translate. But in some graphic novels, even the smallest movements, like reading a book, must be dramatized into a more cinematic language.
American Splendor was nominated for an Academy Award for Best Adapted Screenplay, and won the Best Adapted Screenplay award from the Writers Guild of America. Shari Springer Berman and Robert Pulcini wrote the script, based on Harvey Pekar�s comic book series American Splendor and the graphic novel Our Cancer Year written by Harvey Pekar and Joyce Brabner, illustrated by Frank Stack.
Take these four sequential frames explaining how Harvey Pekar met Robert Crumb and became interested in his work as an example. With very little visual action, the audience understands that Harvey can�t pry himself away from Crumb�s work.
A man reading a book in silence doesn�t play well on the big screen for very long. So, Berman and Pulcini injected verbal and physical movement into the scene. They animated their characters and filled in the metaphorical spaces between frames. They also verbalized the non-verbal aspects of the comic. This scene similarly captures Harvey being introduced to Crumb and his work�but this time with much more physical, visual, and emotional movement.
Figure 1.1 From American Splendor: The Life and Times of Harvey Pekar Source: Stories by Harvey Pekar, Art by R. Crumb; Ballantine Books, 2003. With permission from Joyce Brabner

HARVEY: It�s more than an exercise. It�s breaking ground, man. There�s some wild shit in here.
Crumb is immune to Harvey�s enthusiasm.
CRUMB: You�re spitting on me, Harvey.

Short Story�Interpreting Literary Techniques
Similar to adapting a picture book, the short story often requires expansion. However, the short story has additional challenges. Short stories entertain the audience with literary techniques that need to be translated into film language if they are to retain their impact.
Jonathan Nolan�s short story �Memento Mori� begins by addressing the reader in second person, placing them into the shoes of someone with a dead wife and a memory problem. It�s an intriguing technique and immediately pulls the reader into the story.
Your wife always used to say you�d be late for your own funeral. Remember that? Her little joke because you were such a slob�always late, always forgetting stuff, even before the incident.
Right about now you�re probably wondering if you were late for hers.
You were there, you can be sure of that. That�s what the picture�s for�the one tacked to the wall by the door. It�s not customary to take pictures at a funeral, but somebody, your doctors, I guess, knew you wouldn�t remember. They had it blown up nice and big and stuck it right there, next to the door, so you couldn�t help but see it every time you got up to find out where she was.
The guy in the picture, the one with the flowers? That�s you. And what are you doing? You�re reading the headstone, trying to figure out whose funeral you�re at, same as you�re reading it now, trying to figure why someone stuck that picture next to your door. But why bother reading something that you won�t remember?
Christopher Nolan used a similar approach in his screenplay Memento. Using the protagonist�s second person narration, the character (Leonard) speaks to both the audience and to himself. Similarly, Nolan designed his story in a way that forces the audience inside the mind of a man with a memory disorder.

The Sundance Film Festival awarded both Christopher and Jonathan Nolan with the Waldo Salt Screenwriting Award in 2000. Nolan won Best Adapted Screenplay that same year from the Southeastern Film Critics Association.

Novel/Novella�Condensing and Externalizing Complexity
The story in a novel is usually much larger than can fit into a two-hour screenplay. Characters need to be condensed. Story elements need to be shortened or removed. And the internal world of the characters needs to be externalized, making it critical to examine the point of view of the novel. In Ken Kesey�s classic novel One Flew Over the Cuckoo�s Nest, the story is told through the eyes of an inmate in a mental hospital�Chief Bromdon. From the very start of the novel, we see what the Chief sees.
They�re out there. Black boys in white suits up before me to commit sex acts in the hall and get it mopped up before I can catch them.
They�re mopping when I come out the dorm, all three of them sulky and hating everything, the time of day, the place they�re at here, the people they got to work around. When they hate like this, better if they don�t see me. I creep along the wall quiet as dust in my canvas shoes, but they got special sensitive equipment detects my fear and they all look up, all three at once, eyes glittering out of the black faces like the hard glitter of radio tubes out of the back of an old radio.
�Here�s the Chief. The soo-pah Chief, fellas. Ol� Chief Broom. Here you go, Chief Broom �� Stick a mop in my hand and motion to the spot they aim for me to clean today, and I go. One swats the backs of my legs with a broom handle to hurry me past. �Haw, you look at �im shag it? Big enough to eat apples off my head an� he mine me like a baby.�
They laugh and then I hear them mumbling behind me, heads close together. Hum of black machinery, humming hate and death and other hospital secrets. They don�t bother not talking out loud about their hate secrets when I�m nearby because they think I�m deaf and dumb. Everybody thinks so. I�m cagey enough to fool them that much. If my being half Indian ever helped me in any way in this dirty life, it helped me being cagey, helped me all these years.
I�m mopping near the ward door when a key hits it from the other side and I know it�s the Big Nurse by the way the lockworks cleave to the key, soft and swift and familiar she been around locks so long. She slides through the door with a gust of cold and locks the door behind her and I see her fingers trail across the polished steel�tip of each finger the same color as her lips. Funny orange. Like the tip of a soldering iron. Color so hot or so cold if she touches you with it you can�t tell which.
Lawrence Hauben and Bo Goldman, when they adapted Kesey�s novel, included the Chief in the story but moved the point of view to one that was more omniscient. The Chief still plays a key role in the film. If you read the novel, you can imagine that he is still our guide through this tale, but to an audience new to the story the Chief would seem to be just another ward of
the state until a reveal in act two. From the 1975 screenplay, you�ll notice how the ideas from Kesey�s
novel are kept intact, but the point of view has shifted:
INT. MEN�S DORM � OREGON STATE HOSPITAL � DAWN

The third wall is a heavy gauge steel grill, with a door that opens on to the day room. The door is open. On the far side of the day room, a long hallway with other doors opening into rooms: the latrine, washroom, tub room, mess hall, seclusion room, psychiatrist�s office, visitors� room, etc.
Across the day room, a glass enclosed nurses� station where TURKLE,
a Negro night attendant, is seen preparing to go off duty.
The CAMERA PANS the beds in the men�s dorm. One man turns,
another twists, a third lies as if dead.
CAMERA PAN ENDS on BROMDEN, who lies still, eyes wide open, very alert. He reaches down, plucks a stale piece of gum from under the bed frame, puts it in his mouth and starts chewing.
A beat, then Bromden carefully undoes the leather strap which binds him to the bed. He slips out of bed and quietly makes his way down the aisle, paying no attention to the other patients, some of who are beginning to stir awake.
Ahead, at the end of the hallway, the door opens and three Negro day attendants, WASHINGTON, WARREN and MILLER, dressed in white uniforms, enter and move down the hallway and disappear into a side room.
Bromden continues his silent journey towards the day room as Turkle emerges from the side door to the nurses� lounge, goes up the hallway as MISS PILBOW, the day nurse, comes in, passing Turkle on the way out. She crosses to the nurses� station and enters as Bromden reaches the day room.

As Bromden makes his way across the day room, past the nurses� station, unnoticed by Miss Pilbow who is busy preparing the day�s medication.
Bromden is sliding along the hallway wall, when he is suddenly cut off by a mop which THUDS against one side of his neck. A second mop yokes him on the other side. Bromden freezes. Terrified.
MILLER: Where you goin�, Chief?
WARREN: You goin� t�see the Big White Fathuh?
WASHINGTON: He goin� t�see the Big White Muthuh�
They cackle, legs jiving, as the ammonia stings Bromden�s eyes and burns his nose. He tries to twist his head, but Washington jams the mop harder. Bromden freezes, panic spreading across his face.
WASHINGTON: Haw, look at �im, big �nough to eat apples off my head an� he mine me like a baby�
A KEY is HEARD hitting the lock in the main door. Washington very adroitly releases Bromden, hands him the mop, and turns, along with Warren and Miller, to their duties, as BIG NURSE enters the ward.

Hauben and Goldman adapted the Chief�s paranoia by externalizing it into actions and ambiance. Sounds, props, movement, and color are used to adapt Bromdon�s first person point of view from the novel.
The screenplay for One Flew Over the Cuckoo�s Nest was written by Lawrence Hauben and Bo Goldman, based on Ken Kesey�s novel of the same name. It won the BAFTA and Academy Award for Best Adapted Screenplay, and the Golden Globe Award for Best Screenplay in 1975.

Non-fiction/Journalism�Fictionalizing Fact
Non-fiction work can sprawl as much as a novel, or be as pithy as a short story. They tell stories just as intriguing as their fictional counterparts, yet delve deeply into the facts. Pages may be dedicated to historical detail, legal arguments, philosophical theories, or simply the chronology of the main characters. Adapting non-fiction is a careful balance of eliminating and illuminating the dramatic elements of the source material.
Always keep in mind that a film adapted from reality is still fiction. You are not writing a documentary script�that is a different process entirely. Our process involves the use of non-fiction source material to craft an entertaining piece of fiction. It may be important to retain the journalistic integrity of the original story, but often, creative decisions will trump the facts.
For example, the film Argo is based upon the true story of CIA agent Tony Mendez�s extraction of six American diplomats from Iran during the Iranian hostage crisis in 1979. The screenplay was adapted from two sources: a gripping magazine article and a tension-filled biographical book chapter. The story makes our hearts race with simple facts. Yet, facts are not always the fodder needed for screenplays. Take this paragraph as an example (from Joshuah Bearman�s May 2007 article in Wired magazine, upon which the screenplay was based):
Everyone breathed easier when check-in at the Swissair counter and customs went smoothly. The group made small talk as Schatz approached immigration, presented his passport, and got his stamp. The Americans were momentarily terrified when the officer disappeared with the rest of the crew�s passports. But then he absentmindedly wandered back to the counter with some tea and waved the group on to the departure lounge without bothering to match the yellow and white forms.
In truth, there was very little drama at the airport. But screenwriter Chris Terrio understood that tension must be delivered through to the climax� especially in a Thriller about a CIA agent trying to rescue diplomats from a dangerous situation. Terrio knew that the climax should probably be their moment of departure from Iran, and so he wrote a tension-filled scene where the Iranian guard chased Terrio�s plane down the tarmac trying to stop him and the diplomats from leaving the country.

In the end, the essence of the story remained, but Terrio took liberties in order to craft tension at the climax of the film. Few of the tension-raising elements of the film�s third act ever happened, but it made for award-winning storytelling.
Chris Terrio�s screenplay for Argo won the Academy Award for Best Adapted Screenplay. It was based on the May 2007 Wired magazine article �The Great Escape� by Joshuah Bearman, and Chapter 9
of Antonio Mendez�s book The Master of Disguise.

Biography/Memoir�Broadening the Perspective
A biography or memoir is also non-fiction, but with a more personal point of view, often told within a political or historical context. A screenwriter must strongly consider how the voice of the memoir will be reconstructed for the new medium. Sometimes, a first person point of view can be utilized to give the film a more personal touch, while at other times the point of view is broadened to make the story more universal in nature. Notice how the first person voice of Solomon Northup creates great intimacy and personal connection from the early pages of his memoir,
Twelve Years a Slave:
Having been born a freeman, and for more than thirty years enjoyed the blessings of liberty in a free State�and having at the end of that time been kidnapped and sold into Slavery, where I remained, until happily rescued in the month of January, 1853, after a bondage of twelve years�it has been suggested that an account of my life and fortunes would not be uninteresting to the public.
Since my return to liberty, I have not failed to perceive the increasing interest throughout the Northern States, in regard to the subject of Slavery. Works of fiction, professing to portray its features in their more pleasing as well as more repugnant aspects, have been circulated to an extent unprecedented, and, as I understand, have created a fruitful topic of comment and discussion.
I can speak of Slavery only so far as it came under my own observation�only so far as I have known and experienced it in my own person. My object is, to give a candid and truthful statement of facts: to repeat the story of my life, without exaggeration, leaving it for others to determine, whether even the pages of fiction present a picture of more cruel wrong or a severer bondage.
John Ridley could easily have used Northup�s narration in voice-over when penning his script 12 Years a Slave. Instead he chose a more distant point of view that introduces the audience to a wider African-American experience of the 1800s. By not including a personalized voice-over, the story is no longer simply Northup�s story�it is our story. By scene four in the final shooting script, Ridley has already established the livelihood of Solomon Northup and his family in the year 1841:
EXT. STREET � DAY
Solomon and his family are out walking along the streets and groves of Saratoga.
The streets are well populated this morning with many people out strolling. Most are WHITE, but there are BLACKS as well. They are FREED BLACKS who mingle fairly easily � though not always completely � with the whites. We see, too, a few BLACK SLAVES who travel with their WHITE MASTERS. These pairings are largely from the south and � despite the fact the blacks are slaves � they are not physically downtrodden, not field hands. They are well dressed and �leading apparently an easy life� � comparatively speaking � as they trail their masters.
As they walk, Solomon and his family arrive to an intersection well-worn and muddied from horse and cart traffic. Solomon and his children easily jump across the muck. Anne stands at the lip of the puddle, calls for Solomon to help her across.
ANNE: Solomon�
Solomon, turning back to his wife with a broad smile waving her forward:
SOLOMON: Come, Anne. Jump.
The children, now smiling as well, egg their mother on.
ALONZO:  Jump. You can make it.
MARGARET:  I�ve done it. You can make it.
ANNE:  I will not ruin my dress. Catch me!

Solomon moves close, holds out his arms. Yet, there�s still just a bit of mischievousness in his eyes. Anne gives her husband a lightly stern look to which Solomon replies.
SOLOMON: I will catch you, Anne. (beat) I will. Again, lightly stern: ANNE: You will.
And with that Anne takes the leap. Solomon catches her, swings her around grandly and sets her down lightly to the delighted applause of the children. That done, Solomon takes Anne�s hand and leads her on.
As Solomon and his family make their way, among the slaves on the street, we see one in particular; JASPER. As he trails his MASTER he can�t help but note Solomon and his family as they enter A STORE. His intrigue of this most handsome and harmonious group should be obvious.
With his Master occupied, Jasper moves slyly toward the STORE. Frozen on the spot, Jasper looks on admiringly. Suddenly a voice barks out-
A VOICE: (O.S.) Jasper! Come on!
By opening up the point of view, Ridley has expanded the story beyond
Solomon�s perceptions. We now must consider the plight of Jasper and
others in the community as a central part of the story Ridley is telling.
Screenwriter John Ridley wrote 12 Years a Slave (2013) based upon Solomon Northup�s memoir Twelve Years a Slave. His script won the Academy Award for Best Adapted Screenplay, the Best Screenplay award from the African-American Film Critics Association, Best Adapted Screenplay from the Broadcast Film Critics Association, and was recognized as Best Screenplay at the Independent Spirit Awards.

Stage Play�Visualizing the Verbal
In a stage play, not only must dialogue be used to create character, but also stage plays are more limited in their ability to change quickly from scene to scene. In a screenplay, the story may jump in time or location at the writer�s whim.
The screenplay for the classic film Casablanca (1942) was written by Julius J. Epstein, Philip G. Epstein, and Howard Koch, based upon the little-known play Everybody Comes to Rick�s by Murray Burnett and Joan Alison. It won the Academy Award for Best Adapted Screenplay and, in 2006, the Writers Guild of America hailed it as the best screenplay ever written.
Compare Murray Burnett and Joan Alison�s stage play Everybody Comes to Rick�s to the screenplay adaptation Casablanca. Note how the scenery and most of the character movements are front-ended in the play:
Scene: the bar of Rick�s Caf�, Casablanca, French Morocco, 1941. About eleven o�clock of a summer�s evening. This is the part of an expensive and chic nightclub which definitely possesses an impalpable air of sophistication and intrigue. There is a hectic excitement about the people and entertainers that manifests itself in speech and manner. At Rear Centre, are double swinging doors, covered with emerald green tuft leather, leading to the street. Running from the rear wall to
L. side wall is a semi-circular bar, and green tufted leather. Walls behind bar are crystal shelved for bottles. In front of are bar stools, leather topped. Behind the bar, along L. wall, is a flight of steps. This can be reached only by going behind the bar. Along L. wall centre, is a door leading to the gambling and dance rooms, from which, at intervals bursts of music and voices can be heard. These increase in volume whenever the door is open. At L. front, is a table at which a man in tuxedo with white summer jacket is seated. Further to the front is another table marked �Reserved�. Along R. wall, set into it, are three tables and a number of seats. At extreme R. corner, is a small piano on wheels, salmon colored, ornate, and eye filling. There is a NEGRO on a stool before it. The walls are decorated with rare tropical birds, flamingos, etc. and huge palm fronds. The place is full of people, at bar and tables, in evening dress and uniforms. People entering head for the bar, some seek admittance to the gaming rooms, but only after they have been silently approved by the MAN sitting at the table nearest the
L. door. He is RICK, an American of indeterminate age. There is a drink before him, untouched. The NEGRO, in bright blue slacks and sports shirt, open at the throat, touches the keys of the piano softly, playing Stardust, taking liberties with it. There is a hum of voices, chatter, laughter.
The NEGRO, whose sleeves are short, has one arm completely covered by bracelets extending from wrist to elbow. The door opens, and A MAN in a beautifully cut, tropical evening suit with a cummerband, enters. He is about forty, distinguished, with a comforting aura of solidarity and dignity about him. The NEGRO suddenly bursts into wild swing. The MAN looks around, nods to several people. He then comes directly to the RESERVED table, looks questioningly at RICK, who nods. He sits, and immediately a WAITER brings a magnum of champagne in a cooler. He speaks to the WAITER, who nods and lays two other places. The NEGRO is playing just loud enough for the audience to hear. His playing softens, and he starts to improvise. The MAN leans towards Rick.
UGARTE: Join me, Senor?
RICK: No, thanks, Ugarte. You know my rules.

Another difference is in the use of dialogue. In a play, information is often delivered verbally�but in a screenplay, the screenwriter can use visuals just as effectively.

Kinds of Adaptations
Guy Gallo, who wrote the adaptations Under the Volcano and Adventures of Huckleberry Finn, was a professor of mine at Columbia University. In his excellent book, Screenwriter�s Compass: Character as True North, Gallo identifies three kinds of adaptations:
1.
Prestige;

2.
Straight;

3.
Reimagined.

Each category, according to Gallo, is based upon two interrelated concepts:
(A) How popular is the source material; and (B) How closely does the screenwriter plan to adhere to the source material. Gallo never presented his ideas graphically but, if he did, they might look something like this:
Figure 1.2 Gallo Adaptation Graph (inspired by the ideas of Guy Gallo)
Gallo identified the source material along a continuum of being Popular or Obscure. Popularity was based upon how many people had actually read/watched the source material�as opposed to how many people simply knew about it. Wildly popular source material that has been read by millions of people (e.g., Harry Potter) is much more �Popular� than a book that people know about, but have not read (e.g., Homer�s Odyssey). An obscure book would be even less widely read and less well known. When Gallo was adapting Adventures of Huckleberry Finn, he knew that millions of people were familiar with the story, and were expecting a faithful adaptation. This is what he refers to as a �Prestige� adaptation. Prestige adaptations are highly faithful to the source material, specifically because expectations from the public-at-large are so widespread. On the other hand, when the Coen brothers adapted Homer�s Odyssey into O Brother, Where Art Thou? they took far greater adaptive liberties: reimagining characters, locations, the time period, and the story in general while still maintaining the essence of Homer�s Sirens, the Cyclops, and the attacks of Scylla and Charybdis.
Regardless of the source material�s popularity, a �Straight� adaptation is a faithful adaptation of either a popular or an obscure source. If the screenwriter is less than faithful when adhering to the material, the screenplay is considered �Reimagined.� Just �how� faithful the straight adaptation will be can vary. When Calder Willingham and Buck Henry adapted Charles Webb�s novel into their screenplay The Graduate, they kept most of the story, but changed the main character (Benjamin) from a dour, budding-alcoholic jerk into a lost but lovable young man. The adaptation, while remaining faithful to the story of the novel, was more inventive when dealing with the protagonist.
Figure 1.3 Gallo Adaptation Graph�detailed
Figure 1.4 Gallo Adaptation Graph with examples
By taking Gallo�s definitions and placing them on a graph, we can visualize the relationship of certain kinds of adaptations. The Shakespearean love story Romeo and Juliet is widely known, but Romeo + Juliet reimagined the story in the midst of a modern-day mafia landscape. Apocalypse Now completely reconfigured Conrad�s semi-popular novella Heart of Darkness by placing it inside the Vietnam War. The short story from which Memento was based had not been published when the adaptation was written, so only a handful of people may have read it. And Oliver Stone wrote a fairly straight rendition of William Hayes� trial and tribulations in Turkey when penning Midnight Express, with only the details of Hayes� escape attempts altered. A film like Argo fits in this model in an unusual way, in that the context of the Iranian hostage crisis was widely known, but the details of this individual story were not. And, despite the popularity of the magazine article and book chapter upon which the screenplay was based, it was no The Lord of the Rings.

Remake, Reboot, and Reimagine
In the last fifteen years or so, Hollywood studios have introduced new verbiage into the adaptation lexicon: to remake, to reboot, and to reimagine. To parse these ideas we first need to define three legal concepts:
�
The Production;

�
The Property;

�
The Underlying Work.

For these and other legal issues associated with adaptations, you should consult The Guide to the Acquisition of Rights, a handbook written by the Association of the Bar of New York City, but for the purpose of our discussion, this rough overview will suffice:
The Production is the media that is consumed by the public. A film, a web series, television series, video game, or graphic novel can all be identified as �Productions.�
The Property is any material upon which the Production is directly based. For instance: a script is the Property upon which a film Production is based; a stage play and music/lyrics are the Property upon which a musical Production is based. When the Property is an adaptation, then there is yet another layer of description.
The Underlying Work is the material upon which the Property was based. The Underlying Property could be a person�s life, a comic book, novel, play, etc.
Each element (the Underlying Work, the Property, and the Production) may receive a copyright, as each element is a unique, creative work. However, the rights (and ownership) of each layer must be based upon the copyright acquisition of the previous work. This means that in order for a producer to make a film (The Production), that producer must own the copyright of the screenplay (The Property). Once the film is made, it can have its own copyright, but the filmmakers must obtain the copyright of the screenplay first. Similarly, a screenwriter must obtain the copyright of the short story (The Underlying Work) before s/he would be able to sell the screenplay (The Property) to be made into a film (The Production).
This is how creative people earn a living: by selling the rights to their creative work. American copyright law typically allows the author/family/estate to hold the copyright of a creative work for seventy-five years beyond the lifetime of its creator. Afterwards, the work falls into Public Domain and may be used without receiving permission from the copyright owner. It is always wise to check the copyright ownership before you adapt any work.
Example of authors whose work is in the public domain

�

�

�

As far as films and TV shows go, the verbs to Remake, to Reimagine, or to Reboot exist primarily in relationship to a Production, rather than a Property. Studios remake, reimagine, and reboot a Property they own for new audiences.

Remake
A �Remake� is generally used to describe a movie that uses an earlier Production as the Underlying Work. Gus Van Sant remade Alfred Hitchcock�s classic film Psycho by simply recreating it shot by shot. Joseph Stefano was credited as screenwriter for both films, although he only wrote one script. This would be the strictest example of a remake. The actors and the color film were the only creative decisions that changed. The story, shots, and editing were exactly the same. Most Remakes are not this strict.
As was detailed in Fran�ois Truffaut�s book, Hitchcock, Hitchcock did a remake of one of his own films, The Man Who Knew Too Much, in 1956. The remake was a color version of his 1934 black and white original, and contained a variety of differences in story, location, and mood. The script for the 1956 version was written by John Michael Hayes, who supposedly never saw the earlier film nor read the script. He simply relied on notes from Hitchcock to write the new screenplay. Hitchcock, naturally, was familiar with the film he made more than twenty years previously, and crafted what many consider a much improved remake of an original idea.
Similarly, Tim Burton directed Charlie and the Chocolate Factory, written by John August, which was a remake of Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory written by Roald Dahl and directed by Mel Stuart. Both films were based upon Roald Dahl�s novel Charlie and the Chocolate Factory.
OceanofPDF.com

Reimagine
In 2001, Tim Burton was said to have reimagined Planet of the Apes. A �Reimagined� film is when the storytellers remake a film using the original Underlying Material as inspiration, but depart from the story found in the first film at one or more critical points. I consider the process similar to �re-adapting� the film. If you followed the key decisions of the first adaptation, then you are remaking the film; if you adapt the underlying material differently than the first adaptation, then you are reimagining the film. The 2001 Planet of the Apes starts with the same idea from Pierre Boulle�s novel (the Underlying Work): in a distant universe, astronauts find a planet that is similar to Earth but ruled by apes instead of humans. This is the same premise as the 1968 version of Planet of the Apes. However, the 2001 version came out markedly different from the 1968 version in tone, theme, story, character, and ending. The film had been Reimagined.
OceanofPDF.com

Reboot
A Reboot is something different from a Remake or a Reimagined film. A Reboot has more to do with a franchise, and the world created by that franchise. When a screenplay is written and a film is made, there is a distinct tone to the world in which that franchise lives. This tone is created through a combination of characters, language, actions, visuals, and sound. Consider the Batman franchise of the 1980s and 1990s. Using Sam Hamm and Warren Skaaren�s script (The Property) Tim Burton creates a fun and exciting Gotham City where Danny Elfman scores scenes that are not too violent, and not too edgy; where Michael Keaton plays Batman and Jack Nicholson plays the Joker. The colors are bright and the costumes are fun. This series lasts for four films (the first two directed by Burton, the latter two directed by Joel Schumacher).
Then, in 2005, just as you might reboot your computer, Warner Brothers Pictures decided to �Reboot� Batman. This time, the film was written and directed by Christopher Nolan and company. Nolan�s scripts created more of an edge to Gotham City. The stories are more violent, the crimes more vicious, the stakes higher for everyone. The city (both in the script and on the screen) was darker. Colors were muted; Danny Elfman no longer scored the soundtrack. An edgy Heath Ledger played the Joker instead of the eccentric Jack Nicholson, and Christian Bale replaced Michael Keaton.
The same characters, city, and stories still exist in the new version�but the world is inherently different. Notice how, in Hamm and Skaaren�s Batman, more attention is paid to the clothing, the way people walk, and dramatic reveals. This scene introduces the Joker in an amusing way. We almost root for him.

Inspired By and Based Upon
The difference between �Inspired by� and �Adapted from� has both legal and creative ramifications. �Inspired by� is typically used when an idea has been influenced by the work of someone, or something, else�yet the idea is, in itself, unique. The screenplay Apocalypse Now� written by Francis Ford Coppola and John Milius�was �inspired by� Joseph Conrad�s 1899 novella Heart of Darkness. Details of this are explained in Steven Travers� book Coppola�s Monster Film: The Making of Apocalypse Now. When compared, there are vast differences between the Property and the Underlying work. Most notably, Conrad�s story takes place in the African Congo and tells the story of a timber merchant�s search for wealth in the late 1800s, while Apocalypse Now takes place in Southeast Asia in the 1970s during the Vietnam conflict, and follows the story of a soldier assassin. Very different characters in very different settings�even though both stories have a demagogue named Kurtz who mutters �the horror � the horror �� in the final act.
Milius recognized the allegorical elements in Conrad�s work, and chose to use them in his own work. Both stories explore the dark heart of a foreign land (and a man�s soul). Both stories peel back the layers of a man�s humanity as he distances himself further and further from society. But themes and allegorical elements are not copyrightable. Milius and Coppola created original characters, placed them in original scenarios and told a different story. The naming of a Kurtz character and the echoing of Conrad�s lines are more of an homage to the nineteenth-century author than an adaptation. Nevertheless, Apocalypse Now was nominated for an Academy Award for Best Adapted Screenplay.
Note: To see a true adaptation of Heart of Darkness, you can watch
Tim Roth and John Malkovich starring in the 1993 made-for-TV
movie of the same name, directed by Nicolas Roeg.
Similarly, you may run across the film credit �Based Upon.� Of course, all adaptations are based upon something else�the Underlying Work. But this specific term, when seen in credits, has a particular meaning. Historical or newsworthy events will frequently capture the imagination of storytellers. However, unless the screenwriter is basing his writing on a specific Underlying Work, then he is not adapting another artist�s material and the screenplay may not be an official �adaptation��even if the other media was created around the same historical events.
For instance, a number of years ago, I wrote a screenplay called I.Vermeer, which was based upon the life of the famous art forger Han van Meegeren. In the 1940s, his was The Trial of the Century. After reading a number of articles, a few books, various newspaper clippings, and translated court transcripts, I wrote a screenplay from my own imagination of what Mr. van Meegeren�s life of forgery might have been like.
My script was not �an adaptation� because there is no Underlying Work. Or rather, the Underlying Work was simply the facts of van Meegeren�s life (where he lived, the forgeries he sold, the scientific process he followed, etc.). As a writer, I wasn�t creating the story out of whole cloth. It was, in fact, �based upon� some sort of recognizable material�material that is not copyrightable, per se, but is identifiable by the general public.
Even though I had read a good amount of material to understand van Meegeren�s life, facts by themselves are not copyrightable. Therefore, the facts found within an Underlying Work are not copyrightable. Only the manner in which the facts are expressed are copyrightable. I took the facts from van Meegeren�s life and expressed them in a unique, fictional manner. Similarly, a number of books have been written about Han van Meegeren. They are not adaptations of each other. They are original works based upon researched and originally organized facts. In 2016, the Dutch film A Real Vermeer, screenplay by Jan Eilander & Rudolf van den Berg, premiered in the Netherlands Film Festival. The screenwriting credits imply that this too is an original work�not an adaptation.
Adaptation is not transcription.

Fundamentals of Adaptation

By example, we can now see how writing an adaptation becomes a process of examining dialogue, visuals, story structure, character, point of view, theme, story, and audience expectations. Also known as: the fundamentals of screenwriting�or, in this case, the fundamentals of adaptation. There are dozens of great books explaining these fundamentals. Each is unique, but they all outline the same basic concepts of how to organize a story, how to imagine interesting characters, and how to write smart dialogue.
Students often ask me to recommend �a screenwriting book.� There is no one single book (or blog, or magazine, or lecture series) that I believe is best. Rather, if you are a screenwriter, you need to gather different approaches and synthesize them in a way that is meaningful to you. Aspiring screenwriters can find the basics in a classroom or from books, as well as videos, websites, and seminars at film festivals. Do what is right for you�but don�t forget the most reliable way to learn about screenwriting: by reading actual screenplays and by writing your own.
For me, I find a mix of all of these is best.
To move beyond the basics, we must first define the basics. Personally, I believe that the essence of good screenwriting lives somewhere in these ten books:
1.
Alternative Scriptwriting: Writing beyond the rules by Ken Dancyger and Jeff Rush

2.
Creating Unforgettable Characters: A practical guide to character development in films, TV series, advertisements, novels and short stories by Linda Seger

3.
Good Scripts Bad Scripts: Learning the craft of screenwriting through 25 of the best and worst films in history by Thomas Pope

4.
The Hero with a Thousand Faces by Joseph Campbell

5.
Poetics by Aristotle

6.
Save the Cat! by Blake Snyder

7.
Screenplay�the Foundations of Screenwriting: A step-by-step guide from concept to finished script by Syd Field

8.
Story: Substance, structure, style and the principles of screenwriting by Robert McKee

9.
The Writer�s Journey: Mythic structure for storytellers and screenwriters by Christopher Vogler

10.
Writing Movies: The practical guide to creating stellar screenplays edited by Alexander Steele, from the Gotham Writers� Workshop

That is not to say that other screenwriting books are �bad.� There are some fantastic books about screenwriting (Guy Gallo�s Screenwriter�s Compass, Sherri Sheridan�s Developing Digital Short Films, and Claudia Johnson�s Crafting Short Screenplays that Connect, to name a few)�not to mention the thousands of screenwriting blogs, magazine articles, instructional videos, seminars, classes, and websites available today. But these ten books capture what I consider to be �the essentials� needed to start writing well.

Character and Dialogue
Syd Field informs us that characters are essentially a point of view. In order to establish this point of view, a screenwriter needs to create a context for the character. This context establishes in the mind of the audience a perception of the protagonist and the world in which he lives. During the course of the screenplay, the audience learns more about the character�about his attitudes, his personality, and his behaviors�and learns to identify with the character.
OceanofPDF.com

Defining a Character
The essence of any character is their behavior�their actions. In fact, there are three simple ways that the audience learns about a character:
�
What the character does;

�
What the character says;

�
What others say about the character.

From these three pieces of information, the audience creates both an Internal and an External impression of the character.
The internal information about a character includes items such as their intelligence, education level, upbringing, religious/moral beliefs, and their past and present profession. Basically, the internal information is a sort of biography of the character.
Externally, we are more interested in the character�s needs, and the actions they take to meet those needs. The audience�consciously or subconsciously�notices what characters talk about (and what they avoid talking about). They also notice how characters say things, and whether the characters� dialogue changes during the course of the film (do they speak differently about things as time passes).
The audience also watches how a character behaves. How does the protagonist interact with other characters, and how does the protagonist interact with himself as he tries to achieve the story�s dramatic need? Linda Seger points out that behavioral details are what make characters special. If you think back about the life of your characters�before the story that is written in the screenplay�a writer should be able to develop a history for their character, upon which the writer can build internal consistencies for the character�s behavior. The character�s values, attitudes, and emotions must maintain this internal consistency, even if (and especially when) the story at hand reveals a paradox between the character�s personal point of view and the decisions she is asked to make.
OceanofPDF.com

Primary and Secondary Characters
Key to the protagonist�s struggle is the set of secondary characters�each of whom play a role in the story. Supporting characters should help build the theme of the story, and add texture and depth to the world. Supporting characters should have specific similarities and differences with the protagonist, which help to further define our hero. By creating characters that are similar to the hero, the screenwriter creates depth to the protagonist�s belief system or ideology. By creating secondary characters that disagree with the hero, the screenwriter designs choices for the protagonist. The same concept applies to the antagonist, and the secondary characters that surround her. The secondary characters usually provide encouragement and/or uncertainty, testing the hero and forcing her to make choices. These secondary characters also reflect the hero�s choices by giving the audience a gauge of how likely her future success will be. Characters can be broken up into three fundamental states of being:
�  Active;
�  Passive;
�  Reactive.

Active characters tend to make choices, while passive characters tend to follow the lead of other people. Reactive characters typically only make choices when they are forced to�they react, they do not initiate.
Your decision to have an active, passive, or reactive protagonist will most likely be illustrated through the secondary characters. For instance, secondary characters will demonstrate how the protagonist reacts in situations compared to other characters. The audience sees the protagonist in context by comparing the hero to her peers (who orders lunch first, who makes the plan, who volunteers for the mission, for example).
Secondary characters also help the audience understand the protagonist when scenes are written from the secondary characters� point of view. The protagonist may very well �make the plan� but if the secondary characters do not trust the plan and make plans of their own, then the hero may not be as active as we suspect.
OceanofPDF.com

Trusting a Character
As Field points out, there is a constantly shifting level of trust in storytelling. What the audience sees and hears when a character is out in public or working in their profession is the least trusted type of information. The audience understands that characters are not always honest or forthright in these situations. However, when a character is with family and friends, or with a trusted confidant like a doctor or a priest, these personal moments are more trustworthy and the audience tends to believe the actions and words of the characters more. Information is considered most reliable when a character is by themselves. Words prayed, or written in a private journal, are rarely questioned by the audience as untrue. Similarly, what a character does when they believe that no one is looking can be considered most trusted actions by the audience�this is important information for a storyteller to have.
�
Public actions = least trusted

�
Personal actions = more trusted

�
Private actions = most trusted

Dialogue and Character
Regardless of whether the audience believes what is being said, good dialogue serves three purposes. Good dialogue informs, enlightens, and entertains the audience. Ideally, dialogue can accomplish all three goals simultaneously by:
A. Providing emotional and meaningful backstory;
B. Foreshadowing and explaining what the characters anticipate will happen; and
C. By involving the audience emotionally with what is happening in each scene.
Akiva Goldsman�s Academy Award-winning screenplay A Beautiful Mind unifies Field�s and Seger�s concepts in its second scene. The protagonist, John Nash, has recently arrived at Princeton for graduate school in 1947. Notice how the dialogue simultaneously informs, enlightens, and entertains, while the secondary characters act to influence our understanding of Nash, and to enrich the world.
EXT. PRINCETON RESIDENCE RECEPTION
Sunlight dapples the flickering autumn leaves. Students in formal dress mill. FIND two STUDENTS standing at a bar, sipping martinis.
ZWEIFEL is tall and wiry, like a scarecrow made of skin. SHAPELY is older, maybe 26, and handsome. Both watch someone in the crowd.
SHAPELY: Milnor�s going to get the brass ring if it kills him.
ZWEIFEL: He�s used to pretty metal. (taps his teeth) Silver spoon.
REVERSE on the subject of their musings. A student with shaggy hair and fiercely intelligent eyes is pumping hands. MARTIN MILNOR.
ZWEIFEL: It�s not enough he won the Carnegie scholarship.
SHAPELY: Has to have it all for himself.
CAMERA DRIFTS to a third figure standing near them at the bar. John Nash can�t help but overhear their conversation. Zweifel notices.
ZWEIFEL: First time the Carnegie prize has been split. Now Milnor�s all bent.
Nash nods, looks again at the young man working the crowd. SHAPELY: He�s set his sights on the new military think tank at RAND. ZWEIFEL: They only recruit the best brain from each class. SHAPELY: Milnor�s used to being picked first. (extends his hand) Shapely. Symbol
Cryptography. ZWEIFEL: Shapes broke a Jap code. Helped rid the world of Fascism. Least that�s what he
tells the girls. (extends his hand) Zweifel. Atomic physics. And you are�
Just then a third FELLOW runs up. Breathless, clever face that resembles his name. FOX.
FOX: Am I late?
Zweifel and Shapely exchange a look. He�s always late.
SHAPELY & ZWEIFEL: Yes.
FOX: (to Nash) Hi. I�m Fox.
That�s when Milnor emerges from the crowd to join his friends.
MILNOR: Ah, the burden of genius. So many supplicants, so little time.
Milnor�s eyes light on Nash. The beat of recognition is so slight it�s almost imperceptible. Then he
smiles. MILNOR: I�ll take a white wine. NASH: Excuse me. MILNOR: A thousand pardons. I simply assumed you were the waiter. FOX: Play nice, Milnor. SHAPELY: Nice is not Milnor�s strong suit. MILNOR: An honest mistake. What with those war ration shoes� Nash�s outfit does look off the rack compared to Milnor�s couture. NASH: It�s not your first mistake. I read your brief on Nazi ciphers. With that, Nash offers a slight nod, turns and walks off. FOX: Who was that masked man? MILNOR: Gentlemen meet John Nash. The mysterious West Virginia genius. The other

Avoiding Stereotypes
Seger also instructs screenwriters on how to avoid stereotyping. The more characters that a writer creates that show different sides of a group of people, the less stereotypical the group becomes. In the previous example, we see the behavior of five different Princeton Ph.D. students, but each is portrayed as coming from a different emotional, intellectual, and social background. Milnor therefore becomes a character �type��the arrogant genius�but Goldsman does not paint all Princeton graduate students with the same brush. Some are forgetful, some polite, and others mysterious in contrast. Working with type, and being aware of the pitfalls of stereotyping, will keep screenwriters from portraying groups of people in a narrow, dehumanizing way.
OceanofPDF.com

Archetypes, Myth, and the Hero�s Journey
Christopher Vogler adapted mythologist Joseph Campbell�s ideas of archetypes and the Hero�s Journey into a screenwriting manual. Campbell�s central concepts stemmed from the hypotheses of psychologist Carl Jung (and others). Jung believed that the deep-seated mythological stories we tell (and enjoy) as a human race are nothing more than a �mirror for the collective unconscious.� It was this collective unconscious that interested Campbell, who studied stories around the world to find similarities between all the world�s stories. After decades of research, Campbell claims to have discovered a commonality amongst all of the world�s great stories. He has discovered what he called the �Monomyth.�
OceanofPDF.com

The Monomyth�The Hero�s Journey
The Monomyth is a single storytelling structure shared by popular stories from around the world regardless of where or when they came into being. It doesn�t matter if it was thousands of years ago or last year�s best seller�whether it is shared across a continent on television or told by a tribe in isolation�the monomyth remains relatively the same.
Campbell wrote about the Monomyth in his 1949 book The Hero with a Thousand Faces, and called the structure that he discovered: The Hero�s Journey. In this journey, Campbell explains, storytellers use a similar structure every time�but that the details of the plot and location and characters make each story seem unique.
Forty years after Campbell first published The Hero with a Thousand Faces, Hollywood story consultant Christopher Vogler adapted Campbell�s ideas of the Hero�s Journey into a film paradigm in the 1990s in his book The Writer�s Journey. Since then, various books and blogs have used a sort of Campbell/Vogler hybrid structure to conceptualize Campbell�s discovery, including Sherri Sheridan�s book Developing Digital Short Films. In brief, the Hero�s Journey looks like this:
The film starts with a hero in an �Ordinary World��not necessarily what the audience would consider to be ordinary, but a world that is ordinary for the hero. From this ordinary world, the hero is called to an adventure and given an opportunity to do something special.
Figure 2.1 First quarter of The Hero�s Journey
At first, the hero refuses the call because she is not ready, or is required to do something else instead. But then a mentor character offers assistance and changes the hero�s mind; either that, or some supernatural event occurs. It is this mentorship or supernatural event that inspires (or forces) the hero to leave on an adventure.
All of this takes place in the first quarter of the story.
The second quarter of the story begins as our hero passes through a literal or metaphorical threshold where she learns the rules of the new world she is entering. Soon thereafter, our hero encounters a variety of tests and challenges as she approaches some sort of metaphorical cave, representing the essence of her quest.
Figure 2.2 Second quarter of The Hero�s Journey
Inside the �cave� the hero is confronted with a supreme ordeal. This confrontation can be physical, mental, or both. It could be an external confrontation or a personal struggle. Regardless, this ordeal leaves the hero at her lowest point (physically, mentally, and/or emotionally).
In the third quarter of the story, our hero rejoins the action and is tested yet again. This time, however, she fares far better since she has been strengthened by surviving the supreme ordeal. Our hero now meets these new tests with a better understanding of the world she lives in, and with experience on her side.
This new knowledge/strength/courage helps our hero to succeed this time, giving her a chance to successfully retrieve the goal she�s been seeking. After �the object� (e.g., a sword, knowledge, love, treasure) has been retrieved, our hero escapes back across a second threshold�quite often being chased by her enemies�returning to her normal world. Despite crossing the second threshold and returning to her normal world, the story is not over yet. The hero is far from safe.
Figure 2.3 Third quarter of The Hero�s Journey
In the final quarter of the story, the hero must confront her enemy in a battle to the death.
Figure 2.4 Final quarter of The Hero�s Journey
During the battle, it seems that our hero is going to die, only to be physically, emotionally, or metaphorically �resurrected,� and ultimately triumphing over the enemy. In the end, our hero returns to friends and family where she is received with a reward and ceremony, and quite often shares the lessons she learned on her journey.
As a whole, the Hero�s Journey is typically drawn as a circle, and looks like this:
Figure 2.5 The Hero�s Journey
OceanofPDF.com

Archetypal Characters
Within this model, Campbell and Vogler also introduce the idea of Archetypes. Archetypes are subconscious elements needed for a story that frequently manifest themselves as characters. For instance, in most stories the hero receives some sort of mentorship. Someone (or something) provides wisdom to the main character. Made human, this mentorship comes from a teacher, a coach, or a best friend and becomes a character in the story. But sometimes the mentorship can come from a memory, a metaphor, or a song. To Campbell (and Jung) the shape of the mentorship did not matter. What mattered is that the archetypes existed to help tell the story. Vogler tends to personify the Archetypes, and identifies nine of the most common:
Table 2.1 Nine Common Archetypes according to Christopher Vogler
Archetype Description
Hero The self-sacrificial character that searches for identity and wholeness, and finds herself at the center of the story
Higher Self A character that embodies the hero�s vision of utmost personal improvement or perfection
Mentor A character that represents the voice of divine knowledge, often invoking wisdom that lies dormant within the hero
Shadow A character that represents a psychosis that is destroying the hero
Shape A seemingly two-faced character whose mercurial nature constantly challenges the Shifter hero for good or bad
Threshold A character representing the boundaries of the hero�s neuroses. These characters are Guardian often (but not always) an extension of the villain
Trickster A character embodying mischievous energies that elicit change in the hero, often by leveling the playing field so egos can be examined as they truly are
Herald A character (or media) that announces the need for change within the hero and/or the story; often motivating the hero to move forward
Ally A character who aids the hero on her journey
Table 2.2 Common Archetypes that appear in The Wizard of Oz
Archetype As Portrayed in The Wizard of Oz
Hero  Dorothy
Higher Self  Initially the Professor, then Aunt Em
Mentor  Glinda the Good Witch
Shadow  Wicked Witch of the West
Shape Shifter  The Wizard of Oz
Threshold Guardian  Apple Trees and the Guardian of Oz
Trickster  Toto
Herald  Munchkins
Ally  Scarecrow, Tin Man and Cowardly Lion

Figure 2.6 The Hero�s Journey applied to The Wizard of Oz
When examined through Vogler�s lens of Hollywood screenwriting, the paradigm can be applied to any number of modern and classic films. For instance, the 1939 version of The Wizard of Oz fits both the archetype model and the Hero�s Journey quite nicely.

Archetypal Relationships
Jung not only theorized that we think in archetypal characters, he also believed that we think about archetypal relationships and archetypal events. For instance, if I said: �They were husband and wife, until they realized that they were also mother and son,� you have a strong visceral reaction, even though I didn�t provide you with any details.
Jung believed that these reactions are similar across all people because archetypal relationships carry with them certain deeply engrained expectations. Familial archetypal relationships include:

Those relationships mean something to us as human beings, regardless of the culture or the time.
There are other archetypal relationships beyond the familial. Since they are supposedly found within our psyches, simply naming a few should allow you to extrapolate others:

As you can imagine, these relationships can be used as shorthand for the storyteller. Since human beings collectively understand what these relationships mean, we as screenwriters don�t need to spend time explaining the coach�s relationship to the athlete. Instead, we can spend time illustrating what makes the relationship unique.
OceanofPDF.com

Archetypal Events
Jung also believed that just as we had these characters and relationships in our collective unconscious, the same is true with archetypal situations. We all, subconsciously, understand the implications of being in control of a powerful weapon. You don�t need to go into detail in your script explaining that the character will fight an internal battle over how to use this weapon. We inherently understand this, and�in fact�it becomes tedious and annoying if we do explain it to the audience. Gollum is a great character from The Hobbit: An Unexpected Journey, but we don�t need to spend time explaining his obsession with the ring. We understand the archetype, and therefore understand both the ring and the character, and can move on with the greater story.
These sort of archetypal shortcuts allow us to communicate stories efficiently and effectively to the audience. A very short list of examples include:

Apocalypse;

�

�

�
Healing;

�

�

�

�

�

At the archetypal level, the ideas are clear, but their application is vague. However, if we add genre expectations to our archetype expectations, then we add more specificity.
