Genre and (the Three-Act) Structure
Genre and the Three-Act Structure have long been pet peeves of mine as far as screenwriting concepts go, but for two very different reasons. The working definitions of Genre are too vague and contradictory, while the common models for the Three-Act Structure are too strict. In both instances, these ideas�in their current state�are far less helpful to screenwriters than they should be.
Structure and Genre should fit hand-in-glove. But they do not� especially on the fundamental level. I will touch briefly on this professional disconnect in Chapter 4, but I encourage you to read my book on the subject (The Screenwriters Taxonomy: A New Creative Process published in 2017 by Routledge Focus) where I address the problem in detail. The Screenwriters Taxonomy suggests a new approach to discussing and imaging the creative process of screenwriting. However, to recognize that there is a problem, let�s first examine a few of the current models.
Robert McKee, Ken Dancyger, and Jeff Rush all talk about the importance of Genre. They generally define genre as a set of expected conventions that have been created by the film-going audience over time. The idea is that there is an unspoken relationship between the audience and the storytellers, one that establishes settings, character roles, key events, and conscious or subconscious values within different kinds of films. These different films, over time, can be recognized as similar and given a name, or a genre. The Western Genre, for instance, takes place in the American Southwest (setting), contains cowboys, Indians, sheriffs, outlaws (character roles), who participate in shoot-outs, horse riding, card play, and epic battles involving expert marksmanship (key events), all under the thematic umbrella of good vs. bad, or chaos/primitivism vs. order/society (values).
OceanofPDF.com

Character Types in Genre
Furthermore, when archetypes are introduced to genre, Character Types are created. As an example, in the Western Genre, the mentor might quite often be the grizzled lawman giving sage advice to the naive sheriff. Others include:
�  The shadow is typically going to be the outlaw dressed in a black
hat.
�  The allies are going to be the deputy and key townsfolk.
�  Some common heralds might be the stagecoach driver, or a traveling
snake-oil salesman, bringing in fresh news from the surrounding
towns.
�  The threshold guardians might be the hermit on the edge of town
warning our sheriff of the dangers of the hills, or the bartender on
our hero�s return, explaining what happened to the town while he
was away.
�  The trickster might be  a  drunken, sharpshooting cowboy who
behaves badly, but through his bad behavior illustrates both good
and bad choices our hero will make.
�  The shape shifter might be the hooker-with-a-heart-of-gold who
betrays the hero when the villain promises her a new and better life
if she just reveals the hero�s secrets.
OceanofPDF.com

Different Approaches to Genre
Dancyger and Rush, and McKee embrace this idea of Story Types, but for different reasons. McKee believes that �Genres impose creative limitations on the storyteller, allowing (and challenging) them to create original stories within convention,� while Dancyger and Rush remind us that �Genre is more than formula,� taking a Jungian approach. In Alternative Scriptwriting, they explore genre themes and motifs as representations of dreams and nightmares of the audience. Regardless, all three believe that, as social attitudes change, genres should reflect those changes through an organic, evolutionary process�each suggesting that a creative merging of genres and audience expectations can lead to new and exciting stories. With this, I do not disagree.
What I disagree with is how broadly these Genre categories seem to extend. Without specificity, a category is meaningless. For instance, Alternative Screenwriting and Story both seem to agree on what the word �genre� means, yet here is a list of their Genres:
Table 2.3 Genres according to Dancyger and Rush, and McKee
Alternative Screenwriting  Story
Adventure  Action/Adventure  Love Story
Biographical  Animation  Maturation Plot
Epic  Art Film  Mockumentary
Film Noir  Biography  Modern Epic
Gangster  Comedy  Musical
Horror  Crime  Punitive Plot
Melodrama  Disillusionment  Redemption Plot
Plot
Satire  Docu-drama  Science Fiction
Science Fiction  Education Plot  Social Drama
Screwball Comedy  Fantasy  Sports
Situation Comedy  Historical Drama  Testing Plot
Sports  Horror Film  War Western
War

Western
To begin with, if a genre is supposed to work with established settings, character roles, key events, and values systems, what are those touch points in an Animated film? Or Satire? Or Art Film, Epic, Melodrama, Mockumentary, Biography, or Social Drama? These may be important descriptors�but they do not define settings, characters, events, or values. Therefore, perhaps these eight descriptors are not �Genres� as previously defined�but instead something else.
Second, if genres are conventions that have been created by the film-going audience over time, then certainly we could establish a uniform list of agreed-upon genres. Yet, one list only has fourteen genres, and the other has twenty-four (more, if you include McKee�s subgenres). McKee identifies thirty-three additional subgenres. Oddly though, not all genres have subgenres, and the subcategories don�t seem to have a method of categorization. For instance, the two subgenres for War are �pro-war� and �anti-war,� while he identifies eleven subgenres for Crime including: Caper, Revenge, Thriller, Detective, and Prison Drama. McKee also includes two subgenres that Dancyger and Rush consider full-blown genres: Film Noir and Gangster. Further, there are no subgenres for Fantasy, Science Fiction, Animation, Art Film, or twelve other genres.
Dancyger and Rush state that �Whatever story you choose to write, you are probably working with genres.� So perhaps their list of fourteen genres was illustrative rather than complete. Regardless, it is important for screenwriters to understand that audiences have preconceived (genre) expectations that they carry with them when they experience a story, and it behooves the screenwriters to keep those expectations in mind when writing.
I suggest that the creative community should agree upon a definition of Genre so that it can be used in collaborative conversations. How will students of Dancyger/Rush and McKee collaborate if they can�t even agree on whether or not their �genres� are Genres? If we could agree on a definition of Genre, then perhaps we can begin to identify and articulate other markers in the creative process that can help storytellers discuss and conceptualize their ideas. Personally, the more specific my words and pictures, the sharper my ideas�and the easier they are to share and adjust.
OceanofPDF.com

Structure
McKee and Greek philosopher Aristotle both agree that story is organized through structure. Aristotle explains that the events in a person�s life�even the life of an imaginary character�must be sorted and organized in order to tell their story. McKee recommends organizing in terms of conflict and change within the character. Each event (or series of events) can be considered in order of smallest to largest: Beat�Scene� Sequence�Act.
Through the selection and sequencing of these events, the storyteller is able to guide the audience through what Aristotle would call a consistent and unified whole. For him, to test the �wholeness� of the story, the author must simply ask: Can any part of the story be removed without changing and distorting the meaning of the story? If something can be removed without altering the story, then that element of the story should be removed because, according to Aristotle, it is superfluous.
The structure of a story should be designed to elicit an intellectual and/or emotional response from the audience. McKee explains that the screenplay�s structure should build the events that change the protagonist and that convey this change (to the audience) through a clear shift of values within the story. He likens this to movement along a continuum that could be labeled as love/hate or chaos/order, for instance, depending upon the genre of the story.
This idea is tied closely to Aristotle�s idea that each story has a beginning, a middle, and an end�which is the fundamental basis of Field�s Three-Act Structure for screenwriting.
In the last twenty-five years, there has been much debate about the usefulness of Field�s model of the Three-Act Structure. Field lays out a model suggesting specific page numbers for specific storytelling moments (the Inciting Incident should occur on page 26, for example). The rigidity of this approach can seem claustrophobic and perhaps �uncreative.� However, if put in context and conceptualized broadly, a more pliable Three-Act Structure could be quite helpful and creative.
Unlike Field, who considers the Act Structure in and of itself, Aristotle considered structure as part of the story. Only by examining the kind of story (or, in other words: the genre), could one conceptualize its structure.
OceanofPDF.com

Aristotle�s Structure
Aristotle believed that all good stories need to be �whole,� and to be considered �whole,� each story needs to contain a beginning, a middle, and an end. As an example, Aristotle provides the story of Oedipus the King, which is a mystery (the genre). Oedipus is trying to solve the murder of his father. So the Beginning of this mystery is: the unsolved crime.
As the middle of a mystery tends to be the investigation, this is also true of Oedipus� story. He spends the middle of the play questioning suspects and trying to solve the murder. Then, as the audience expects, the ending of the mystery occurs after the detective solves the crime, which is just what Oedipus does.
�
Beginning: Unsolved mystery

�
Middle: Investigation

�
End: Mystery Solved

Aristotle also believed that good stories contain three specific elements that work together to create change in the main character. The first element would be a series of complications that create a �knot� of problems (in Aristotle speak: the Desis). The second element would be the culmination of this knot that somehow causes a Major Change in the main character (in Aristotle speak: the Metabasis). Following this character change, some sort of fallout will occur from the knot being untied, (in Aristotle speak: the Lusis).
To Aristotle, it did not matter where in the story the Major Change/Metabasis occurred. For instance, Aristotle theorized that the Tying of the Knot/Desis could even occur before the play started (meaning: earlier in the character�s life) and that the Major Change/Metabasis could occur in the Beginning of the story�with the Middle and the End of the story explaining how the Knot gets Untied/Lusis.
Figure 2.7 Aristotelian model with Desis occurring prior to the play opening
Or the Knot could get tied in the Beginning, Change the character in the Middle, and get Untied at the end.
Figure 2.8 Aristotelian model with the Metabasis occurring in the middle of the play
Or the Knot could get tied incrementally in the Beginning and Middle, only to Change the main character in the End just before the Knot unravels.
Figure 2.9 Aristotelian model with the Metabasis occurring toward the end of the play
Oedipus the King follows this third example. Our detective, through a series of connected complications, slowly tightens the knot until the end when Oedipus discovers that he is his father�s murderer.
This is a major Turning Point in the story (in Aristotle speak: the Peripeteia). Ironically, the detective�s attempt to prove his innocence actually leads to the discovery of his own guilt. Aristotle believed that all good stories contained at least one Turning Point. In the case of Oedipus the King, it occurs at the end when the detective is revealed to also be the criminal.
Figure 2.10 Three-Act Structure with the Anagnorisis included
Once Oedipus discovers that he is a murderer, the Knot starts loosening. He subsequently realizes that he also unwittingly married his own mother, and that this incestuous act is the cause of the plague that is destroying his kingdom. It is this moment of clarity (in Aristotle speak: the Anagnorisis) that finally allows the hero�and the audience�to see things as they truly are. Often, the Anagnorisis connects the ending back to the beginning, allowing the audience to reconsider the entirety of the story in a new light, and provides the audience with a theme to ponder.
In Oedipus the King the Anagnorisis would be: can human beings escape the predestination of life and what, if anything, does the answer imply about the nature of humanity?�a common Anagnorisis in the crime genre.
Figure 2.11 Aristotelian model with the Anagnorisis included
While Aristotle would point out that the structure of Oedipus the King was designed by Sophocles in service to this theme (and genre), the Turning Point does not always have to come at the end of the story. Nor does it have to coincide with the Major Change in the protagonist. Aristotle believed that there were many ways to structure an effective story. For instance, the Metabasis could be found anywhere in the story. In an Action Movie like The Bourne Identity, the story begins en media res, with the major change/realization of Jason Bourne�s identity coming early in the film. It is only at the end of the film, when Bourne himself is able to put the pieces of his mind back together, do we have a realization about what happened before the start of the film. Such a structure might look like this.
Figure 2.12 Aristotelian model applied to The Bourne Identity
Or, in a love story like Brokeback Mountain, tough guy Ennis breaks down in the middle of the film and has the opportunity to connect on an emotional level with the one man he truly loves: Jack. He chooses to fight against his emotions however and, by the end, the audience reflects back that this central moment in the film was Ennis� tragic mistake.
Figure 2.13 Aristotelian model applied to Brokeback Mountain
OceanofPDF.com

Freytag�s Structure
A similar analysis of Shakespearean Dramas occurred in the nineteenth century when German playwright and critic Gustav Freytag wrote the book Techniques of Drama. In it, he explains what is now known as Freytag�s Pyramid.
Figure 2.14 Freytag�s Pyramid
Freytag believed that �good� plays all share the same structure, which was best exemplified in five �Acts�:
Act I�Exposition: Each play starts with basic information about the characters and their situation.
Act II�Rising Action: A series of events occur, where each event gets more and more exciting for the audience.
Act III�Climax: Actions rise until one of two things happen: either
(1)
the Protagonist and the Antagonist meet face to face and the Protagonist finds that s/he has a hidden weakness, or

(2)
there is a Turning Point where the story reverses expectations (sound familiar?).

Act IV�Falling Action: Regardless of which climactic event occurs, the story begins to unravel (kind of like a knot?) and the final outcome of the story is thrown into question.
Act V�Denouement: The story ends with the tale unraveled and the audience reflecting on the meaning of it all. Freytag called this the Denouement�based on a French word that means �to untie a knot� (interesting).
Using this paradigm to analyze the celebrated play Romeo and Juliet:
Act I�Exposition: Romeo is in a relationship with a woman who will never marry him. This troubles Romeo, until he meets Juliet.
Act II�Rising Action: Romeo falls for Juliet�and she for him. But their future is in question because their families are at war, and they will never be allowed to marry each other.
Act III�Climax: Romeo murders Juliet�s cousin and is banished from town. Juliet keeps her love for Romeo a secret and her family arranges for her to marry Paris.
Act IV�Falling Action: Juliet decides to fake her own death instead of marrying Paris. Romeo subsequently believes that Juliet is dead, and kills himself. When Juliet awakes from her ruse, she finds that Romeo is dead and, crestfallen, kills herself.
Act V�Denouement: After the tragedy, both Romeo and Juliet�s families realize that their age-old feud was the reason for their children�s deaths, and agree to end the feud.
Comparing Freytag�s triangle to one of Aristotle�s theoretical constructs, you�ll notice that they are not too dissimilar.
Figure 2.15 Freytag�s Pyramid compared to Aristotelian model
OceanofPDF.com

Field�s Structure
Just as Aristotle analyzed Sophocles and Freytag analyzed Shakespeare, Syd Field made his mark by analyzing successful Hollywood films in the 1970s. He published his analysis the seminal book, Screenplay, which identified a basic structural paradigm that, he claims, all successful screenplays follow.
Field believes that most successful screenplays have three parts (in Field speak: Acts), and that each Act contains a specific percentage of the overall length of the film.
 �
Act I The Set up approximately 25 percent of the total length of the film

 �
Act II The Confrontation approximately 50 percent of the total length of the film

 �
Act III The Resolution approximately 25 percent of the total length of the film

Field also theorized that the main objective of each act is to constantly increase the overall tension of the story, until it reached its highest point of tension (in Field speak: the Climax), followed by a sharp decrease in tension at the very end (in Field/Freytag speak: the Denouement). In broad terms, Field�s act structure looks like this:
Figure 2.16 Three-Act Structure according to Syd Field
Look familiar?
Field also added some unique identifiers. Near the end of Act I, a plot point incites the hero into an adventure (in Field speak: the Inciting Incident). About midway through the film, a turn of events causes everything in the story to change (in Field speak: the Midpoint). He also points out that there are various plot points that occur between the Inciting Incident and the Midpoint, as well as between the Midpoint and the Climax. In essence, Field�s paradigm ends up looking something like this�which has become what most people refer to as the Three-Act Structure for screenwriting.
Figure 2.17 Three-Act Structure with additional story elements
For Field, it is all about proportionality. This proportionality is one of the biggest criticisms of Field. Some say that he encourages people to �paint by the numbers.� But if you remove proportionality, Field�s observations become quite similar to Aristotle and Freytag. In fact, I would argue that Aristotle, Freytag and Field are all saying basically the same thing, only using different descriptors.
OceanofPDF.com

The Aristotle�Freytag�Field Amalgam
Both Aristotle and Freytag identify a point in the middle of the story where the character makes a major change. Aristotle calls it the Metabasis and Freytag calls it the Climax. But Freytag is not identifying the Climax as the height of tension (as Field does). Instead, Freytag is talking about the Climax being the pivotal moment in the growth of the protagonist. In some instances, this could actually be a �low point� for the protagonist� as when Romeo is banished from town for killing Tybalt�and so is not �the climax� as we know the word today.
If, for clarity�s sake, we agree to identify Aristotle�s �Metabasis,� Freytag�s �Climax,� and Field�s �Midpoint� simply as �The Turning Point,� we can then recognize that the modern-day Three-Act Structure is simply an amalgam of Aristotle, Freytag, and Field.
Both Freytag and Aristotle identify the story points leading up to and falling away from the Turning Point. What Freytag calls Rising Action, Aristotle calls Lusis; and what Aristotle calls Desis, Freytag calls Falling Action. Remember, Freytag�s descriptions of �Rising� and �Falling� do not pertain to the rise and fall of Tension, but rather the causes and consequences of the Turning Point in the story.
Just for kicks, let�s imagine combining all three concepts into one model. If we did, this unified theory would hypothesize that a successful screenplay should contain three acts (beginning, middle, end), and that each Act should incorporate elements that allow storytellers to manipulate tension in the following way:
OceanofPDF.com

Act I
� �  The story begins with a low amount of tension while the story is Set up. An Inciting Incident occurs that propels us into the second act and raises tension.
OceanofPDF.com

Act II
� � �  Tension rises continuously over Time. A series of events (Desis) lead to a change (Turning Point) in the protagonist and/or the audience. The Turning Point subsequently causes its own series of events (Lusis) that continues to increase tension.
OceanofPDF.com

Act III
�
Rising tension culminates in the Climax of the story.

�
A Denouement releases tension back towards its original level of Act I.

�
The story ends with the protagonist and/or audience having an Anagnorisis.

Figure 2.18 The A-F-F Amalgamation
From my perspective, these story elements are relatively fluid and are not bound to any specific page count. Similarly, the frequency of their occurrence can also change from film to film (i.e., there can be singular or multiple Turning Points, the Desis and Lusis can be longer or shorter, a story can have multiple climaxes, etc.), but the general structure is one that a screenwriter should consider. Granted, this is not the only way to tell a story, but a few thousand years� worth of examples have proven that this is a working model for writing successful plays and screenplays.
OceanofPDF.com

Story Spine
A common starting point for storytellers is the idea of developing a Spine for the story. Blake Snyder, in his screenwriting manual Save the Cat!, defines the spine as �what happens to your hero� as the screenwriter charts his transformation from beginning to end. This transformation is initially conceptualized in broad strokes, including the thematic message of the story, the problem that the protagonist will face, and the literal, metaphorical, and spiritual changes that the hero will go through from the beginning of the film until the end. Others refer to these ideas as the �Character Arc� and suggest that all of the central ideas can be boiled down to two or three sentences that can be delivered in thirty seconds or less�or, what some call An Elevator Pitch.
This sounds simple, but the core element of a screenplay�s spine is a unifying connection between story, character, theme, and action. Basically: What is the story about? And, as Aristotle suggested, how can every scene play a part in what this story is all about?
Take these two examples. Both films take place during World War II, and both center around the lives of prisoners of war. But each film has a very different spine.

Example A
The Bridge on the River Kwai illustrates the insanity of war through the eyes of an American Navy man named Shears, held prisoner by the Japanese during World War II. Shears watches fellow prisoner, a British Colonel named Nicholson, stand up to the paranoid punishments of the Japanese camp commandant, Colonel Saito. At first heroic, Nicholson�s dogged reliance on systematic order slowly transforms into a crazed obsession�but not before Shears escapes from the prison. Unfortunately for Shears, he is coerced by yet a third madman�Major Warden, an American�driven beyond the point of reason by an unrelenting sense of military duty. Unable to escape the lunacy of these three characters, Shears inevitably brings them together for a final climactic battle at the bridge on the River Kwai.

Example B
The Great Escape tells the fantastic story of human ingenuity and creativity in the face of great danger. Set in a German prisoner of war camp during World War II, American Air Force Captain Virgil Hilts is one of more than a dozen prisoners who are hell-bent on breaking out to disrupt the Nazi war machine. Under constant scrutiny, each man uses his own specialty in collaboration with the others. Some men tunnel, some build disguises, others scrounge for materials or forge identity papers. Despite its tragic ending, the men succeed in pulling off one of the greatest escapes in military history, and the indomitable spirit lives on in the recaptured Hilts, who hunkers down to plan his next escape attempt.
If you were the screenwriter working from these specific spines, as you adapted The Bridge on the River Kwai you would focus each character and each scene through the thematic lens of �the insanity of war.� If you were adapting The Great Escape, you would highlight �ingenuity and creativity� in each scenario and each character. Each character�s goals, insights, and reactions would reinforce these themes. If a scene did not illuminate the theme in some way, it would be removed. Both screenplays, well over 150 pages each, are finely tuned stories that rigorously adhere to the spine of the film�even though both stories take place over the course of many months, involve dozens upon dozens of speaking roles, and tell intricate stories about many of them.
OceanofPDF.com

Story Shape
Story Shape is an approach to organizing a screenplay that is fundamentally different from the Three-Act Structure and the Hero�s Journey. This approach is discussed less in screenwriting texts, although I suspect screenwriters embrace the idea subconsciously. Personally, I have doodled out story shapes for years but never saw the idea written down until 2001 when Tom Pope wrote his excellent book Good Scripts Bad Scripts.
Story Shape works like this: When a story is told, each scene �belongs� to a specific character (or set of characters). The scene �belongs� to them because they are either the center of the story for that scene, or the elements in that scene have a profound effect on the character. Some stories follow just one character for the entire film, and so the structure would be a simple line.
Figure 2.19 Simple story shape of one character in every scene
OceanofPDF.com

Story Shape for Romance Movies
But, image if you are going to write a love story. Our first scene or two might have the girl as the central character. Then the next scene might introduce us to the boy and give a scene or two to him. Then we might have another scene with the girl; and then the boy.
Figure 2.20 Beginning Story Shape for a Romance
And for each scene or sequence, we�ll just put a little mark, and we�ll move the marks closer and closer to each other because the two characters are getting closer and closer to meeting. Until finally they do. They share a scene � in this example: Scene 7.
If their attraction is short-lived, then the boy goes off in one direction and the girl goes off in another. Each time we mark a scene, we�re �giving that scene� to that character. Our two characters spend time apart on screen but, by crosscutting between the characters, the audience realizes that the characters are longing for each other.
As the story continues, the Girl and Boy get back together again and this time fall in love. But then something goes wrong and they break up again. During the breakup, the Girl has a few scenes of her own and the Boy has a few scenes of his own. Finally they realize that they were made for each other and get back together again.
They say that most romances are the story of Boy Meets Girl, Boy Loses Girl, Boy Gets Girl Back, Boy Loses Girl and Boy Gets Girl Back Again. You can see that this pattern fits that description�with two options for an ending. Most romances end with the lovers together. With a tragic ending, they are torn apart.
Figure 2.21 Common Story Shape for a Romance
Figure 2.22 Common Story Shape for a Tragic Romance
Keep in mind that there are no set rules for Story Shape. Each screenwriter, or screenwriting team, should design the shape that best meets the demands of the story they are telling. Yet, it is worth recognizing that general shapes already exist. Many genres have generic shapes that might be useful when initially sketching out your story.
OceanofPDF.com

Story Shape for Sports and Crime Movies
Many Sports Movies are about two teams (or two contenders), each preparing for the Big Game, or the Big Fight, or the Big Match. Subsequently, the shape of these stories typically bounces back and forth between the two opponents until the big event when we get to watch them duke it out.
Figure 2.23 Common Story Shape for a Sports Movie
Figure 2.24 Common Story Shape for a Crime Film
This is also a common story shape for Crime Films. Only instead of two teams, you have the detective and the criminal, the cop and the robber, or the prosecution and the defense. And instead of the Big Game, there is an attempt to deliver justice.
Often, it is the shape of the story that builds tension�not always the scenes themselves. Watching lawyers discuss a case does not, in and of itself, create tension. But watching lawyers discuss a case when we know (through editing) that they are doomed for failure, does. And�as we know from the Three-Act Structure�Tension is the name of the game.
OceanofPDF.com

Story Shape for Horror and War Movies
Horror Films and War Movies also tend to share a similar story shape; one in which a group of characters systematically die during the course of the film. In Horror, a handful of characters (in this example, let�s say six characters) set off on an adventure. Genre expectations suggest that one by one, these characters are going to die at the hands of a deranged killer. By doodling out the order and proximity of the murders, the screenwriters will be designing a rough roadmap for their story�one that can be shared, discussed, and rearranged until everyone agrees to its general shape. I find that this tool helps save hundreds of hours in the actual writing process, because I am no longer �writing blind.� This story structure can also be reimagined for the War genre�except, instead of the Killer killing teenagers, the story would involve the Enemy killing soldiers.
Figure 2.25 Common Story Shape for both War and Horror Genres
I realize that some writers enjoy �letting the characters speak to them� as they write. I am the same way. But personally it helps if I know that the innocent babysitter is the one who is going to survive until the end, and that the football player, who seems to be the guardian of the group, is going to die first. It also gives my producer a sense of reassurance because she is able to �see� the story painted in broad strokes before I go off and write. With an adaptation, you probably already have a good idea of who is going to die, and in which order. But what happens if you need to condense characters, eliminate scenes, or reorder elements of the story? Visualizing the story through rough sketches and discussion often helps everyone get on the same page when making these decisions.
OceanofPDF.com

Kurt Vonnegut�s Story Graph
Yet another way to consider the structure of your story was initially outlined by Kurt Vonnegut�supposedly in his discarded anthropology thesis at the University of Chicago. I have not had the privilege to read the paper, but he delivers an amusing overview in a video.taped lecture called The Shapes of Stories, which is available quite readily online. Vonnegut explains: �The fundamental idea is that stories have shapes which can be drawn on graph paper, and that the shape of a given society�s stories is at least as interesting as the shape of its pots or spearheads.� For instance, he sketches out the shape of a Boy-Meets-Girl romance like so:
Figure 2.26 Boy-Meets-Girl story graph (as described by Kurt Vonnegut)
Of course, not all stories revolve around the dichotomy of �Good Fortune vs. Ill Fortune.� The Y-axis could contain any sort of character polarity. I like to call this approach the Vonnegut Story Graph�to differentiate from Pope�s Story Shape�because we could both graph and draw the shape of a romance. Both would be helpful.
Figure 2.27 Story Shape vs. Story Graph comparison for a Romance
Regardless of which approach (or combination) you use, Story Shape is an idea that is so simple that I can understand why few people feel the need to write about it. Yet, it is so important to the process that it is hard to believe that more people don�t embrace it when they talk about screenwriting.

I�m sharing three of the thirteen stories (and scripts) with you to demonstrate how the ideas in this book can be used in the creative decision-making process. Personally I find that it is difficult to break, bend, or ignore what you don�t understand. So, to help gauge your proficiency in the basics, I�ll provide you with both the Underlying Work (a short story) and The Property (an adapted screenplay) in their entirety. I will also provide you with Screenwriting Scenarios�questions to consider as if you were the screenwriter involved in the adaptation process. There are no right or wrong answers to these questions. Simply by thinking about the answers, I hope that you can understand the decisions that I made, and consider the decisions that you might make in a similar scenario. Each scenario will build on the last.

�Karelia� Scenario A

Questions to consider:

straight vs. reimagined)

Shane sat next to the worms and set his rod down. He looked up and saw the green of the leaves standing out against the sky, the contrast making them look unreal and distant. He pulled his pocketknife out of the tackle box and stuck it in the sand between his feet. He looked over at Sam, and watched his head move slowly to the right as he followed his line downstream.
Shane thought of Christy McFaul hobbling down the hall, trying to ignore curious stares and hushed giggles, seeing her friends embarrassed by her presence. He stood up and moved his feet closer together.
�You�re a little pecker, Sam.� He peered down at the blade between his feet. �Sometimes I think it�s funny when they spit at you.� A breath of wind carried the smell of clover down the hill. He reached down to pull the knife out, and inched his feet together.
�Yeah. It�s really hilarious, isn�t it?� Sam jerked on his line like he had a strike, but it kept floating with the current. �You�re just mad because you know I�m right.�
Shane tried to focus on a thin strip between his feet, but it slipped away and he pictured Christy McFaul standing by herself�her shirt stretched tightly over her plastic shell, her head leaning off to one side. He held the knife between his thumb and forefinger, letting it sway as his hand shook.
�Knock it off, Shane. You�re not impressing anyone.� He heard Sam�s voice from the shore, and dropped the knife.
It just missed his left foot, and he felt the handle leaning against his skin.
�Well, maybe I don�t want to impress anyone,� he mocked, and moved his feet closer so the heels touched and formed a curved V.
�You�re always trying to impress someone, Shane, and it never works. You just look ridiculous.�
Shane picked up the knife again and bent his head closer; straight down at the little triangle of sand between his feet. He squinted one eye and held his breath. Then he felt the knife cut into his right foot.
For a moment it was clean. Then blood seeped up in a crooked line behind his big toe. �Jesus, Shane! Did you cut yourself?� he heard Sam say from somewhere far off.
Shane kept staring at his bloody foot as Sam ran over to him. He leaned back against Sam, who helped him to the ground. The blood tickled his foot as it ran down to his heel. �You got yourself pretty good, didn�t you?� Sam asked, wiping the blood away with his finger.
Shane leaned back on his elbows, feeling the rocks digging into him. Sam kneeled down at his feet and pressed one of the canvas high-tops around the cut. Shane winced and tried to jerk his foot away from the stiff material. �Sit still,� Sam said.
Shane looked up at the trees and cotton floating down with the breeze. He listened to the limbs creek and sigh. Watched the leaves shake against the sky. And felt Sam�s bony hand softly pressing against his foot. �Pretty impressive, huh?� Shane said.
�Pretty retarded,� Sam laughed. Shane watched him as he held his foot, his cheeks hollow and emaciated, his shirt hanging from his chicken bone shoulders. Shane�s eyes were distant and glassy, they didn�t move as shadows shifted and danced around him, and the creek flowed quickly around the bend.

�Karelia� Scenario B
At the meeting, you learn that the short film will actually be a pilot for a TV series. The producers are developing this as a series of thirty-minute �light dramas.� Their slogan is �Wholesome America: no drugs, no violence, no sex�just good stories.� They want to give you creative freedom to develop the script, but they have some requirements: they want the series to be about rural high school students, they want there to be an equal representation of male and female characters, and they want you to create an adult character (who will appear in each episode) to give adult viewers someone with whom they can identify. They expect the show to be watched by pre-teens and their parents.

Questions to consider:
� �  How will you structure your screenplay to ensure that it reaches 23� 24 pages? What thoughts do you have for characters? How will you handle the adult character?
� � � �  Is �light drama� the genre you were expecting? How does this help you design your story? Do you need additional information? How are you thinking about dialogue? Do you think you will use any sort of Three-Act Structure elements? Does the Hero�s Journey apply? Can you envision a Story Shape to your episode? What sort of archetypes jump out at you, if any? Will you need to create any, or are you going to sidestep archetypes at this point?

Section Two
Intermediate Practices

OceanofPDF.com

4

. . . . . . . .

Recognizing your Genre

You are original. I know. I get it. So am I. Everyone is.
Heaven forbid that anyone would suggest that you (or I, or anyone) should write something that was not completely original. No one wants to be derivative. And yet, how does one write a Romance without two people falling in love? Or a Crime Story without a criminal? Science Fiction without science? A War Film without some sort of combat? A Sports Movie without competition?
Or maybe I�m wrong. Maybe you aren�t writing a Crime Story, a War epic, a Sports Movie, Science Fiction, or a Romance. You�re staying away from �Genre.� I get it. You don�t want to be derivative. So, you are also staying away from Horror and Western too. I understand: you don�t want to be limited. So � no Action Films either. No Fantasy. No Thrillers. What about Slice-of-Life or Day-in-the-Life Films? You know, films like: The Wrestler, Boyhood, As Good As it Gets, Return of the Secaucus Seven, MASH, Lost in Translation, Sideways, Magnolia, Birdman, or Winter�s Bone. �Life� Films are Genres too�identified over time by enough films having enough similarities for the general public to recognize them as a Genre. If you are avoiding all of those kinds of films, then all I can say is �Wow. You�re �don�t limit me� approach is rather � um � limiting.�
But seriously, all sarcasm aside: allow me to take a moment to make an argument for Genre, and a few suggestions on how you could (or should) use it. This is part of a much larger discussion�one that I have written about at length in a different book called The Screenwriters Taxonomy. In that book, I have outlined a creative process for identifying and imagining nearly every kind of fictional narrative film possible. I will provide an overview for you at the end of this chapter, but the general idea stems from the idea of Super Genres.
I strongly believe that nearly all fictional narrative films can be divvied into one (or more) of eleven Super Genres�each with their own defining characteristics. These characteristics are not absolutes. They are not rules. They are suggestions based on what audiences over the years have come to expect. Stick with me for a moment; this is going to get a little complicated. To clarify, I�ll use a metaphor to illustrate my approach.
OceanofPDF.com

The Genre Roadmap Metaphor
Imagine Columbia, Missouri; a city roughly in the middle of the United States. Now imagine a road trip from Columbia, Missouri, to any other city in the Americas. That road trip is your movie script. Columbia, Missouri, to Toronto, Canada = movie script A. Columbia, Missouri, to Guadalajara, Mexico = movie script B; Columbia, Missouri, to Denver, Colorado = movie script C; Columbia, Missouri, to Indianapolis, Indiana = movie script D. And so on. There are hundreds of thousands of cities you could travel to (and scripts that you can write) and very few limiting factors on how your trip would go.
However, since we are talking about a driving trip, you will have to drive through Arkansas, Illinois, Iowa, Kansas, Kentucky, Nebraska, Oklahoma, or Tennessee on your trip. There is no way around it. You can�t leave Missouri by car without traveling through one of these eight states. Imagine those states as your Super Genres. You�re going to have to drive through one of them to tell your story. But I�m not trying to tell you how to drive, what kind of car (or truck, or motorcycle, or lawnmower) you should use, or whether or not to take the highway, byway, or dirt roads. It doesn�t matter to me. I also don�t care what direction you travel in to reach your destination (I mean � to write your script). If you want to drive to Canada by driving south out of Missouri into Arkansas�that�s great. Be original. Millions of people have driven through Arkansas. Just because you do too, does not make you derivative.
So that�s it. That�s my metaphor: if you are going to write a feature-length, fictional narrative film, then I guarantee that you are going to write in (or drive through) one of the eleven Super Genres. There are other �Genres� (which I call Macro Genres and Micro Genres), as well as movie Types, Voices, Pathways (and other categories) to choose from. But as a central consideration for screenplay adaptation, I suggest focusing in on Super Genre. If you find this approach useful, expand your understanding further into the Screenwriters Taxonomy from there.
OceanofPDF.com

Genre = Atmosphere + Character + Story
There�s no official Governing Board of Genre. Genres have been created using a crowd-sourced approach. They are built upon specific audience expectations that have evolved over the decades. For instance, if I say, �Let�s go see a Western,� then I recognize that you already have specific expectations for the characters you�re going to see in the film, the basic kinds of stories that might unfold, and the atmosphere within which one of those stories will be told.
Atmosphere, Character, and Story�these are the fundamentals of Genre.
OceanofPDF.com

Atmosphere
A film�s Atmosphere is affected by:
�
Location;

�
Costumes and Props;

�
Visceral Expectations for the Audience.

For instance, a film in the Western Genre often takes place in the American Southwest, with a large number of scenes filmed as exteriors so we can soak in nature�s beauty. We also expect to see saloons, a sheriff�s office, and maybe a brothel or a hideout in the cliffs.
The Costumes and Props focus on the people who live in these Locations (cowboys and Native Americans). Naturally there will be guns, horses, headdresses, and cowboy hats. We can also expect to see a sheriff�s star, cattle, rope, card games, and whiskey; and maybe a spittoon or a stagecoach.
The Visceral Expectations of a film are the elements that will excite the audience and get their hearts pumping or their minds racing. Each genre creates different Visceral Expectations. In a Western, the Visceral Expectations are created with fistfights, shoot-outs and exciting chases on horseback. There will also be spectacular images of the rugged countryside to feast our eyes upon.
Together, these elements (Location, Costumes/Props, and Visceral Expectations) define the Western Genre�s Atmosphere. If you went to see a Western, and you didn�t see those things, you would think you went into the wrong theater by mistake.
OceanofPDF.com

Character
A Genre�s Character also has three defining elements:
�
Character Types;

�
Character Goals (for each Character Type);

�
Stock Characters.

Horror Movies are typically stories about an innocent victim being confronted by some sort of overpowering, evil entity whose sole intention is to kill and destroy. This scenario provides two central Character Types: the Victim and the Killer, each with their own specific Character Goals: to survive or to kill.
The Stock Characters in a Horror Film are the other victims who are going to die off, one by one. Also there is usually an ineffective authority figure, there to demonstrate that �even the police can�t stop this monster,� and an archetypal herald character who knows the killer�s history and can explain the rumors of why the murders are happening.
OceanofPDF.com

Story
The third requisite of all genres is Story, which includes:
�
Theme;

�
Tent Pole Scenes;

�
Story Rhythm.

The Theme is the central recurring idea underlining the entire story. While each film may have its own specific story and theme, genres also have broad themes that are explored. For instance, Science Fiction Films typically critique modern societal problems by masking their critique in metaphor. A famous example would be Invasion of the Body Snatchers, which used a Science Fiction Story about �pod people� who were taking over the minds and bodies of Earthlings to comment on the �Red Scare� in the United States.
Tent Pole Scenes are the big scenes that hold up the film�just as a circus uses large tent poles to hold up the tent. In a Sports Movie, we expect to have the training scenes, the motivational speech, and the big game.
Story Shape, as explained in Chapter 2, illustrates the general pattern a story follows. For instance, the Story Shape for a Romance Film typically crafts a scene with the girl � then the boy � then the girl � then the boy. This approach creates a Rhythm. Each genre has its own general Story Rhythm.
Figure 4.1 Story Shape/Rhythm for a Romance
Atmosphere, Character, and Story are the building blocks of Genre, allowing screenwriters to understand the basic expectations of their audience. The writer needs to recognize that these expectations exist�not only within the audience, but within the minds of co-writers, producers, and within ourselves. Once recognized, the screenwriter can choose to work with (or against) these expectations to craft a unique script.
OceanofPDF.com

Super Genres
When strictly considering Story, Character, and Atmosphere as criteria, the choice of Genres whittles down to about sixty. But, in the spirit of creating a more specific vocabulary, I find that those sixty genres can fit beneath eleven genre umbrellas. I call these �Super Genres� because they actually define the Story, Character, and Atmosphere that all other genres subsequently use.
Table 4.1 The Eleven Super Genres
Screenwriters Taxonomy Super Genres
Action  Science Fiction
Crime  Sports
Fantasy  Thriller
Horror  War
Life  Western
Romance

To fully understand the nature of the Super Genres, let�s examine them one at a time.
OceanofPDF.com

The Eleven Super Genres

1 Action Films
Action Films are visceral, not intellectual. These films have great fight scenes and/or extended chase scenes, enhanced with heart-racing stunts and vivid special effects. This genre empowers the audience by placing them in the hero�s position. The hero is nearly always a Resourceful Hero; sharp-witted, quick on their feet and able to improvise both mentally and physically. Despite these strengths, the hero typically begins the film with an internal problem, quickly followed by an external problem (e.g., marital issues and hostage issues in Die Hard). By story�s end, our hero will resolve both by verbally explaining the internal problem while physically dealing with the external problem. These twin story threads create the rhythm of an Action Film. When one issue seems to be settled, the other issue falls apart.
Table 4.2 The Action Super Genre
ACTION SUPER GENRE
STORY
Themes Good over evil Logic over action Individual in a single-minded world
Expected Scenes Given the mission Personal issue Picks up a companion/duty that must be upheld Big chase/battle Victorious ending
Story Rhythm Internal vs. External problems Hero�s personal problem in the first act Hero vs. Villain in the second act Hero solving both problems in the third act
CHARACTER
Character Types Character Goals
A. Resourceful Hero A. Struggles against all odds to survive
B. Smart but Nasty Villain B. Single-minded evil objective
ACTION SUPER GENRE

STORY
C. Dame/Dude in Distress C. To be rescued
Stock Characters Sidekicks
Henchmen
The amazed locals
Hero�s advisor

ATMOSPHERE
Location
Exotic and/or Intense locations Clash between mundane and exotic locales

Costume and Props
Costumes that make the hero seem strong and will allow him/her to blend into a variety of locales They don�t change clothes much Props that allow our hero to be resourceful

Visceral Expectations
Fight scenes Chase scenes Stunts and special effects
The Action Film villain is usually single-minded with grand, two-dimensional plans like �taking over the world.� They are portrayed as highly intelligent with extreme character flaws, subsequently behaving in exceptionally nasty ways. This battle between the resourceful hero and the psychotic villain creates one of the main themes in Action Films: the battle between Good and Evil.
While we have every confidence in our hero, an important secondary character is typically put into harm�s way in an Action Film�often a Damsel (or Dude) in Distress that the hero must protect, rescue, or defend. Therefore, the climactic scenes at the end of the film are not only for the survival of the hero, but for the survival of others. Additional stock characters in an Action Film include the villain�s henchmen, a mentor who provides the hero with technological or logistical information, and unsuspecting individuals (politicians, merchants) representing the society that the villain is trying to destroy.
The locations in an Action Film create a clash between the mundane and the exotic. Because the hero stands in for the �every man� in the mind of the audience, the hero is often seen doing mundane things in typical locales. But then the hero is launched into some exotic location, or some exotic element within a mundane setting (even if the story takes place in a typical city, the hero will find himself on the roof of the highest sky scraper, the tomblike tunnels beneath the city, or the most regal museum).
The ability to blend into a variety of locations is important for the hero. Costumes are designed to make the hero seem both strong and suave, allowing him to wear an outfit to a dinner reception, but then also fight off various henchmen while wearing the same clothes. The hero�s clothes must play double or triple duty. Similarly, props must be multifaceted. Anything that the hero touches or sees can be used as a weapon or as an object that can help them get out of a bind.

2 Crime Films
Crime Films explore themes of truth, justice, and freedom, and contain the fundamental dichotomy of Criminal vs. Lawman. This relationship can be explored in a variety of multilayered and complex stories. For instance, Crime Films can employ the idea of an �anti-hero� where the audience roots for a criminal. They can embrace the emotions behind a crime, or the results of a poorly handled investigation or conviction. More traditionally, Crime Films have us rooting for the lawmen to win. We might follow the story of a lawyer, members of the jury, a valiant police officer, or victims of a crime.
Crime Films make the audience jump through a series of mental �hoops.� The audience is often trying to solve the crime along with the detective, figure out how the elaborate heist is going to work, how the convict is going to escape, or how the lawyer is going to corner the criminal at trial. Because the audience is already thinking on all cylinders, it is not uncommon for the Crime Film to use verbal gymnastics to keep the audience�and the protagonist�on their toes.
Table 4.3 The Crime Super Genre
CRIME SUPER GENRE
STORY
Themes Exploration of truth, justice, and/or freedom
Expected Scenes The criminal act (or its immediate aftermath) Clues and witnesses
CRIME SUPER GENRE

STORY
Red herring/double-cross/mistaken identity Final justice and/or �solving� the crime
Story Rhythm Us vs. Them (in a way similar to the Sports Genre)
CHARACTER
Character Types Character Goals
A. Criminal A. To stand up against a system/person/situation that they feel is unfair
B. Lawman/Detective B. To uphold the law and/or the status quo
Stock Characters Victim Witnesses Henchmen Boss Judge/Jury

ATMOSPHERE
Location
Someplace where a person can easily hide such as:
�
A big city

�
The wide open rural landscape

�
Repetitive camouflage of the suburbs

Costume and Props
Costumes where people can change �identities� and where clothes seem to represent a person Weapons and things that can be used as weapons Clues Tools to analyze clues

Visceral
Mental and verbal gymnastics

Expectations
Violence from a character who is cornered Unusual or unexpected crimes
The general rhythm of a Crime Story is Us vs. Them. The story typically jumps back and forth between the �Good� and the �Bad��and part of the fun for the audience is to decide who is on which side. Crime Stories are full of Trickster and Shape Shifter archetypes�forever challenging the protagonist to trust the right person. Inevitably, characters in a Crime Story will be double-crossed. In the end of a Crime Story, justice is usually served one way or another, and the initial crime is laid to rest.
Crime Stories take place in a wide variety of locations�but one constant element is that the location will usually allow characters to hide or blend into their surroundings. Crime Stories can also use costumes to hide people inside their uniform (in prison, the military, schools, or in a corporate setting). Costumes in this genre are also used to deceive the audience. Not everyone is who they appear to be. In addition, the audience for Crime Films will find excitement in the criminal acts and in the violence that surrounds them.
Typically, stories in the Crime Genre will involve the protagonist assessing the crime and then trying to mete out the appropriate justice. The lawman must find clues, assess weaponry, and gather information (often from witnesses) in order to assess how the criminal will be punished. Characters serve as judge, jury, victims, witnesses, organizational bosses, and henchmen. Props in the genre tend to be items that can be used as weapons, clues, and tools to perpetrate or solve a crime.

3 Fantasy Films
The hallmark of Fantasy Films is a sense of wonderment, typically played out in a visually intense world inhabited by mythic creatures, magic and/or superhuman characters. Props and costumes within these films often attempt to create a sense of mythology and folklore� whether ancient, futuristic, or otherworldly. The costumes, as well as the exotic world, reflect the personal, inner struggles that the hero faces in the story.
While a typical Fantasy Film follows the struggle of a single heroic protagonist, the hero is unknowingly standing in for �the many.� These metaphoric tales are enjoyable for the audience, providing a sense of wish fulfillment. Fantasy Films create fantastical representations of humans� subconscious fears and desires.
The Rhythm of a Fantasy Film usually follows the Hero�s Journey, wherein the hero begins the journey in a familiar world, then leaves for an unknown world, only to return to the first world with new knowledge. Along the way, the hero typically gathers a band of misfit companions who will discover the unknown world together, often becoming entangled in an epic battle as part of the final confrontation with an Evil Force.
The Mentor plays a key role in Fantasy Films, as does the hero�s Allies. The Mentor delivers wisdom from the past, or from another world, allowing our hero to better understand the newly discovered, unusual creatures. These different life forms metaphorically represent different decisions that humans have to make in life, often providing insight to the hero (and audience) as she witnesses sacrifice, beauty and love in ways that were unfamiliar at the beginning of the journey.
Whether the hero discovers a magical world that was once hidden, is introduced to the magic way of seeing things, escapes into a completely different reality, or simply lives inside a fantastical location, Fantasy Films encourage us to get lost in a new world, just as the hero does.

4 Horror Films
Structurally and rhythmically, Horror Films are very similar to War Films. The story typically starts with an ensemble group of characters who die, one by one, at the hand of �the enemy� until the lone survivor must confront a stronger enemy in order to survive.
Most Horror Films take place during modern day, in some remote location where a group of people can be isolated from the rest of society. The characters are often teenagers or people in their early twenties (the genre�s central audience). Characters wear typical, modern clothes�except for the Killer, who often wears a strange costume and/or mask, and utilizes a variety of props/weapons to kill the Victims.
Thematically, Horror Films often serve as a morality tale, with the Killer serving up violent penance for the Victims� past sins. Metaphorically, these become battles of Good vs. Evil or Purity vs. Sin. Characters who drink alcohol, take drugs, and have pre-marital sex tend to die, while the more pure characters tend to live.
Table 4.5 The Horror Super Genre
HORROR SUPER GENRE
STORY
Themes Good vs. Evil
Sin vs. Purity
Penance for past sins
Expected Scenes Arrival to isolation
Various deaths
Discovering the killer
Killer vs. Survivor final battle
Story Rhythm Typically a group whittled away by an unseen aggressor
CHARACTER
Character Types Character Goals
A. The pure A. To survive
B. The non-pure B. To live their life as they see fit
C. Evil Killer C. To kill
Stock Characters Ineffective authority figure
Characters very similar to the hero (who die)
Knowledgeable expert on Evil

ATMOSPHERE
Location Someplace isolated, to which most characters are new and unfamiliar
Costume and Props Typical modern-day clothes and household items
Visceral Expectations Bloody killings
Startle moments
Final confrontation with the killer
The Killer in Horror Films does not always have to be human, or even from this Earth. It could be the devil or some sort of possessed human, or even some sort of haunted location trying to kill the inhabitants. Of course, most films we consider to be Horror have a human-like being performing the killing�sometimes an adult, sometimes a child. Regardless, most Killers have some sort of superhuman ability, whether it is telekinetic powers, extreme strength, near invulnerability, stealth, or an above average intellect.
The Visceral Expectations in this genre come from the antagonist�s unique ability to kill, which inevitably reveals itself in violent deaths of the ensemble characters, a lot of �startle moments� as we wait to see who the Killer will strike next, and the final confrontation when the protagonist must battle the more powerful Killer to survive.

5 Life Films: Day-in-the-Life (or) Slice-of-Life Films
Day-in-the-Life and Slice-of-Life Films are subtly different from one another, but share enough similarities that they can generally be lumped together into one Genre.
Both Life Films take place in locations that are seemingly mundane to the protagonist(s). Costumes and props are comprised of modern-day clothes and items of daily life (again, to the protagonist, not necessarily for the audience). And while all of this may seem boring, the audience typically finds visceral pleasure from rooting for/against the day-to-day decisions that a character makes.
There is rarely an extreme antagonist in these films, other than devastating events like death or disease. Rather, the human antagonist is usually an adversary who is equally as mundane as the protagonist. Or the antagonist could be a collection of adversaries that our protagonist must face on a daily basis as s/he attempts to accomplish a series of personal goals. For instance, a character trying to find a job may encounter a variety of antagonists stopping him from achieving his goal. The other characters in this genre are family and friends, colleagues from work or school, and the people who populate the world in which the protagonist lives (shop owners, neighbors, police, etc.).
Common themes in this genre revolve around the idea that we all go through the same inherent struggles in our lives, the difference comes in the individual choices that we make. Typically, the audience can expect to see a variety of opinions on a single topic, forcing us to consider which choice will be �right� for the protagonist. Scenes that can be expected in Life Films are different characters contemplating the same topic, someone sitting down and having �the big talk� with the protagonist about what is �right,� and the Big Realization�either by the protagonist, changing their way and making the �right� choice, or the audience realizing that the protagonist made the �wrong� choice.
There are two general Rhythms of a Life Film. The film either focuses exclusively on the protagonist, only cutting away to other characters as they relate to the protagonist�s problems, or is designed as an ensemble piece, where multiple stories are told in parallel.
Day-in-the-Life
Most genres tell stories that involve the protagonist affecting the lives of other people. Whether it�s saving the planet, inventing something earth-shattering, robbing banks, killing the dragon, stopping a monster, ending the war, or winning the big game, the ramifications of the protagonist�s decisions affect people. However, the exploits of the protagonist in a Life Film will have little or no effect on a larger population, and their actions often have few ramifications on anyone else.
Table 4.6 The Life Super Genres
DAY-IN-THE-LIFE OR SLICE-OF-LIFE SUPER GENRE

Expected Scenes The big talk The big realization Many differing opinions about life from many different points of view
Story Rhythm Following the protagonist with limited cutaways to others in their life, but only as it relates to the protagonist
or:
An ensemble point of view where the group stands in for a single protagonist and the film jumps between characters equally
CHARACTER
Character Types Character Goals
A. Protagonist who is a A. A series of small goals to succeed in life, or the particular matter �typical person� at hand
B. The protagonist�s small B. Help the protagonist understand the world group of friends
C. The protagonist�s long C. Obstruct and challenge the protagonist�s sense of well-being in list of adversaries the world
Stock Characters Family Work colleagues
DAY-IN-THE-LIFE OR SLICE-OF-LIFE SUPER GENRE

STORY
People in this world (shop owners, civil servants, people around town)

ATMOSPHERE
Location Locations that either seem mundane to (or outside of the control of) the protagonist
Costume and Props Typical clothes and household items appropriate to the characters� time period
Visceral Expectations A series of key events or decisions that will change the character�s life
Rather, the Day-in-the-Life Film takes small events in a person�s life and raises their level of importance. The �small things in life� feel as important to the protagonist (and the audience) as the climactic battle in an Action Film, or the final shoot-out in a Western. In Day-in-the-Life Films, you often see protagonists struggle with their art, or with alcoholism or addiction; they deal with personal conflicts in their marriage, with illness, family issues, societal detachment, and insanity, just to name a few. Often, the protagonists deal with multiple, overlapping issues in the course of the film�just as we do in life. One of the main enjoyments of a Day-in-the-Life Film is for the audience to see how other people deal with the same sorts of problems that they are going through.
This is not to say that protagonists in other genres do not also deal with these issues. They do, but protagonists in other genres deal with these problems while they are taking corporations to trial (The Verdict/Crime Genre), discovering the theory of relativity (The Theory of Everything/Romance Genre), winning the championship (Hoosiers/Sports Genre), and fighting the Japanese (The Bridge on the River Kwai/War Genre). In a Day-in-the-Life Film, life�s struggles are the central conflict in the story.
Slice-of-Life
Similarly, Slice-of-Life Films use the same �small ball approach� to explore the stories of a community, or of a specific person in that community. They are often ensemble stories�each character having their own story, and the collective stories adding up to represent a particular cross-section of society.
If the story is not an ensemble, then the main character�s story tends to broadly reflect the lives of a particular community.
One of the main �enjoyments� of a Slice-of-Life Film is to spend time in a specific location observing how that community copes with everyday issues.

6 Romance Films
Similar in many ways to Life Films, Romance Films tend to take place in typical settings for the main characters: home, work, and the places in between. However, there are also �special places��locations that represent love and feeling wonderful. When connected to these locations, the characters dress differently and express a feeling of enchantment. These special places stand in stark contrast to the mundane world in which the characters find themselves for the majority of the film, reflecting an inner sense of happiness.
The Visceral Experiences in a Romance are emotional. The audience expects to see a First Kiss at the very least, and perhaps even a few hot and steamy sex scenes. However, these expectations are not always shown on screen and, especially in films from the 1940s and 1950s, the romantic interludes were often demonstrated through verbal double entendres and visual metaphors. Audiences also expect to see the �down side� of love, and are emotionally prepared for heartbreak and loneliness as part of the story.
The traditional characters (and Story Rhythm) in a Romance center around the �Boy� and the �Girl.� The old axiom to describe Romance Stories is: Boy meets Girl, Boy loses Girl, Boy gets Girl back. This is the general Rhythm, with the scenes split evenly between the Boy and the Girl �each wanting to �fall in love.� Of course we will also see characters such as The Best Friend whose sole purpose is to give the lovers guidance, and The Other (often an ex-lover, but also a job opportunity, illness, or family obligation) that stands in the way of love. We will watch how each side relates to falling into, and out of, love�hoping in the end that they find true love in each other. It should be noted that the lovers are not always boy and girl. Brokeback Mountain is a romance between two men. The idea of love does not always fit within our social norms either; Lolita is a romance between a pedophile and his teenage bride. In fact, it doesn�t even have to be a romance between two living things. For instance: Her.

B. The Boy
C. The Other
D. The best friend(s)

Visceral Expectations

A variety of themes that typically follow a single well-worn saying that everyone already knows, such as:

Love at first sight

�

�

B. To fall in love

Nevertheless, Romance Films can usually be identified by themes that reinforce our beliefs about love: Love at First Sight, Love Conquers All, All You Need is Love, and There is Someone Out there for Everyone, just to list a few examples.
The tent pole scenes in a Romance are the lovers meeting for the first time, their first date, their first �kiss,� the breakup, advice from their friends, and a final reuniting of the couple�which may end poorly or happily ever after. These scenes almost always include the two protagonists, who are surrounded with stock characters to populate their mundane world: service providers, friends, family, ex-lovers, and characters who represent other interests that take time away from the relationship, such as work, hobbies, habits, addictions, and travel opportunities.

7 Science Fiction Films
The Science Fiction Film is often the story of the protagonist (and her allies) facing something that is �unknown� or �incomprehensible� with the potential to change the future of the human race. In some films, the Unknown is represented by a villain with incomprehensible powers, a creature we don�t understand, or a scientific scenario that threatens to change the world. Regardless of who or what antagonizes the hero, we expect tent pole scenes where our protagonist faces the unknown head-on and learns an important truth about humanity before the final confrontation where she will risk �everything.� And, of course, there will be a scene where the �incomprehensible unknown� is explained by a scientist or a more intelligent being�allowing the storytellers to explore philosophical and moral issues about society.
Science Fiction Films frequently force the audience to consider the nature of human beings, the confines of time or space, and the concepts of human existence in general. The Anagnorisis of these stories often force the audience to reflect on thematic questions such as: Is there a right or wrong? Is there good and evil? Are we in control of our own destiny?
Like the Fantasy Genre, Science Fiction often takes the audience into an intensely visual world. The Visceral Expectations of this genre naturally tend to lean toward fantastic locations and special effects, battles with epic stakes and fun and unusual characters. However, if the story (or budget) doesn�t allow for these Visceral Expectations, then audiences are equally intrigued by mind-bending concepts and strong social commentary.
By looking at a list of Science Fiction Film examples, it may seem difficult to establish specific locations, costumes, and props. The key is not to concentrate on the specifics, but rather on the idea of a clash between the �alien� and the �familiar.� To accomplish this, the writer can employ a few techniques:
�
Making �the alien� seem familiar (e.g., put a saloon on another planet);

�
Making �the familiar� seem alien (e.g., show Manhattan completely abandoned).

Perhaps because the worlds and the concepts in Science Fiction Films are so fantastical, the characters are typically more simple (although usually a lot of fun). The hero usually has a group of Friends and together they battle the Villain (or complex Scenario). The hero and allies are simply focused on fixing the problem or stopping the villain, and navigating the new world(s) in which they find themselves. They typically do not have complex personal issues to worry about. Background characters include the standard scientists/experts, alien or unusual beings, and the unsuspecting masses that are at risk of being destroyed.

8 Sports Films
While the Sports Genre is perhaps the easiest to recognize, it is frequently misidentified. Of course, we know that in a Sports Movie people will be playing sports, and it will basically be the story of �Our Team� versus �Their Team.� Their team will always try to win, and our team will show the world that they deserve recognition or redemption. It doesn�t always have to be a team, though. The story could also be an individual athlete or the story could focus on an individual playing on a team.
Our tent pole scenes are going to be The Big Game, the Big Talk, or Motivational Speech, the Training Sessions (often � la montage) and the Loss of an Important Character Along the Way. These scenes all play into one or more of the following themes:
�
Hard work and dedication pays off;

�
There is no �I� in �Team�;

�
Sacrifices need to be made in order to win.

These themes (and others like them) are vital in an Underdog Story�which nearly every Sports Movie follows. We hardly ever root for the champion, although it is not impossible. In order to set up an Underdog Story, it is important to create a sense that The Other Team/Athlete is �the best.� Therefore, as a storyteller, you need to compare and contrast Our Team to Their Team. This creates the Story Rhythm of cutting back and forth between these two entities for a majority of the film�giving more scenes to the protagonist�s team�until the two finally meet for the Big Game.
Locations primarily consist of the playing field, the practice field, and the personal homes of the lead characters. Costumes and props center around uniforms and sports equipment�with personal (non-athletic) items representing the personality of each character. These characters, depending upon the number of characters, are often stock characters. For instance, in addition to coaches, refs and star players, we often see a character who:
�
believes that the underdog team can win;

�
believes that the champion can never lose;

�
holds secret knowledge about the game;

�
is physically weak, but morally or mentally strong;

�
sacrifices him-/herself for the team.

Together, these characters bond during the course of the film, allowing the underdog to win the final game�which is where the audience finds the greatest Visceral Experience. Other visceral moments come from watching the extraordinary athletic ability on both teams as they prepare for the final battle.

Story Rhythm
