Genre will give you�the screenwriter�a foundation upon which to tell a story to your audience. The motivation and emotion of your characters will pull that audience into your story, and the structure and dialogue will propel them through to its conclusion. Combined, these intermediate practices will help you write strong and unique screenplays.
Now that you have an understanding of these concepts, explore how Jo Weiss� short story �The Pool� was adapted into a half-hour television episode as part of a larger project.

�The Pool� Scenario A
The producers contact you for another assignment. Netflix has picked up the show as a six-episode experiment and they have made four creative decisions:
1.
They like the characters from your first script, but they want each show to highlight a different set of characters in the community. Characters that were highlighted in one episode will then be minor or secondary characters in the next episode. There will be thirteen main characters that will rotate through the season. The producers think this will broaden the fan base.

2.
The Ms. Kelly character was well received. They would like her to be the one central recurring character in every episode.

3.
Each episode will be developed as its own short film�individually written and produced. There will be no Writers Room where writers consult and share ideas. Each of the six episodes will be their own stand-alone project.

4.
Each episode will be based on a short story and progress through the year one month at a time. Your first episode was the �June� episode.

They want you to write Episode #3 (August). All that you know about Episode #2 is that it centers on three characters: a tomboy named JIK and her two male friends: TARHEAD and FRY. They are juniors in high school. You do not have to highlight them, but you must include them somewhere in the script. They are a tight-knit group of harmless troublemakers. You must also include�but not highlight�some of the characters from the first episode (including Ms. Kelly as a recurring character).

Questions to consider:
�
What is the spine of the story? What is the theme of the story?

�
Is there a genre to this story? If so, what is it?

�
What will your approach be to this material? (faithful vs. inventive, straight vs. reimagined)

�
How will you weave other characters into this story? Do any of the characters need to be eliminated?

�
Where is the central tension coming from in this story and how will you be able to illustrate that in a screenplay?

�
Knowing that you are trying to develop an ongoing series, how can you build depth to these characters that can extend out for many years?

�The Pool� by Jo Weiss
The house is filled with boxes.
We are supposed to be helping mom unpack. Instead, I am lying on the floor of the upstairs bedroom I will share with my sister and staring at the ceiling. It appears to have been painted in a matte white plaster and then decorated with the feet of two ostriches tied together and pressed against it.
Whenever someone wanders in, I make a point of telling them this. They do not come back.
Eventually I grow bored and turn my head to get a better look at the green, shag carpet. It is dark green but has light green highlights. I wonder how long it will be before this carpet looks familiar. I wonder what it will all be like in a year. I always do this �
I like to remind myself of the here and now by speculating on where I will be and what I will be doing in a year. Then I like to try and remember, when the year is up, all the different times I wondered where I would be, and compare my speculations with reality.
I don�t recommend it.
I never thought we would move again. This is the fourth time. A move for every child, so to speak. I always get the feeling that I�m in a really bad movie after we�ve moved. It�s just not real at first. So I decide who I want to pretend to be in this new and not-so-real world.
Usually I start out with good ideas �
Once I decided I was going to be more like David Cassidy and I was able to pull it off for about two days. I don�t know why I thought this would work to my advantage.
This time is more difficult. I�m not a kid anymore. I�m 16 years old, a small fish in a big pond.
In Indiana I was a huge fish in a puddle. I loved it. I actually got a standing ovation when I tried out for cheerleading because they had never seen anyone do a back hand spring before. At least not in person.
Now I�m in a big city. It even sounds cool �
Columbus.
People have heard of it before, unlike Kokomo or Burlington. I am excited. I am scared to death. I cannot settle on who I want to be like.
I�m obsessed with Nadia.
I liked that she never smiles and looks like an urchin boy. But she�s dark and Romanian, and I�ll never be that skinny or that perfect.
I�m also obsessed with Dorothy Hamill and scared of my new haircut. I had long hair and in a sudden moment of insight, realized I could cut it all off and no one would notice since they�d never seen me before.
At first, I thought it was really cool. I left the shop turning suddenly left, then right as if someone had called out my name, just so I could feel The Hamill Cut swing and move.
I was in heaven.
Now, it�s acting like my old hair again. I�m starting to think I look more like Carol Burnett than Dorothy Hamill. I love Carol, but I�m way too young to want to look like her.
I hate being the new girl. I pretend to like it to trick myself. I tell myself it�s a great time to experiment with my personality; an opportunity to drop bad habits or bad friends.
Actually, that is the best part.
Just before we move, I start feeling myself pull away from people. Preparation for separation. The more I withdraw, the more I am sought out. Every time this happens, I am in awe of the powers I have held for so long without knowing it. All I had to do was quit caring and I became a magnet.
I swear fervently, and often, that I won�t forget this next time. That I�ll quit worrying so much what other people think, and quit trying to please them and quit acting so needy with friends. But then I get somewhere new and all my natural instincts take over, which means �
Sit back and watch everyone. Figure out who is cool and who is not. Gauge their humor. Watch the teacher for the same reason. Keep mouth shut. Do not initiate conversation, yet be approachable and friendly.
Then, somehow, I lose my plan. I smile really big when anyone talks to me and look like a desperate loser. The only time this worked was in the fourth grade. I was in the same homeroom as Sheila Welch, and I was awestruck on sight.
She was blonde and willowy. She walked with a grace and ease (well beyond her years) up to the front of the class, to hand in her paper. Her knee socks were perfect. Her loafers had pennies in them. Her skin was olive and pale at the same time. And as she passed my desk, she smiled at me and I saw she had that rare combination of blonde hair with dark brown eyes.
Her teeth were perfect.
I smiled really big back, and became aware of who I wanted to be this time. I watched her sit or, rather, float down. She seemed to know every kid in the class and they all liked her best. Even the teacher liked her.
By contrast, no one knew me. My hair was not only a dull, mousy brown, but had been pulled back on top and on the sides (very tightly, I might add) and held in place with a rubber band from the newspaper. My eyes felt even paler than they already were. My skin was turning a blotchy purple and pink on my legs from nerves and cold. My dress felt stupid and my overbite asserted itself �
She ended up being one of my best friends for almost two years. Until we moved again. She even copied my Southern accent. I don�t think it was intentional, and it didn�t last long, but it impressed me immensely. Yet, it is always the first day of school when I remember her. A slow motion home movie of poetry in motion.
�Joanna, where are you?� my mother yells in a whiny, exasperated tone from the bottom of the stairs.
�I�m in my room,� I answer heavily, as if she has interrupted something important.
�What are you doing up there?� she asks, hopeful that I might actually be putting a bed together or unpacking my clothes.
�Looking at the rug,� I say, suddenly amused with myself.
I feel guilty for a second, but then she tells me I had better come downstairs and help or I will be looking at the back of her hand in a minute.
My brother Jeff is thirteen and wiry and still pretty like a girl. He doesn�t have that square-jawed manly look, yet. He gets away with murder because he�s the only boy. Once, when he was about nine, my mother told him to do something and he was a smart-ass back, and she got so mad she slapped him on the cheek. He rubbed his face, a slow smile revealing itself and said, �Thanks, I needed that.�
She collapsed laughing, and they have never been the same �
He had found his weapon.
She hasn�t touched him since. Whenever he is in trouble now, all he has to do is start snickering and she is gone again, laughing right along with him.
He will make friends easily because he�s a boy.
All they have to do is play sports together and they�re set. It makes me feel lonely and sorry for myself. I miss the days when he needed me to clear the path.
My sister Sharon is twelve. She is painfully shy and quiet in public and at school, but at home she is the meanest of the four of us. She has an ability to find the one button you thought no one knew about it and push it and push it and push it until you have to chase her upstairs so fast you know that if you catch her she will die. She is also devastatingly funny. When she knows she has pushed me too far, she pulls a Jeff and makes me laugh against my will�no matter how mad I am.
I worry about her. Junior High is tough. She is not. She and Jeff seem to be the same age, now that I have shot up a head taller. They find comfort in each other. I am jealous.
My baby sister, Kay, is only eight. She has no worries. She has no fear. She has already met most of the neighbors and has two friends who keep coming over for snacks. One of them is named Michael. He threw rocks at her the first day we arrived and now they�re best friends.
She brings treats from neighbors she has introduced herself to. This puts mom in the position of having to make something in return and carry it over and talk to people she would rather not know yet, thank you very much.
The best thing about this move is that it has happened in the summertime. I do not have to walk into another homeroom completely alone and self-conscious; or stare out at a sea of young judges, while a teacher introduces me�ending, as always, with the fatal advice that everyone should try and make me feel comfortable. A remark which, in my experience, only serves to make people want to make me feel uncomfortable. My debut will be made at the neighborhood swimming pool instead.
Mom has managed to find our bathing suits and some towels. Jeff has already rolled his up with goggles inside. The towels are stiff and tiny. I wonder how it will look. Over my suit, I pull on a t-shirt that reads �Bullshirt� (which for some reason my Southern Baptist grandmother finds amusing) and slip into cutoffs.
Sharon and Kay come downstairs. Kay has her goggles on like an aviator. I tell her to take them off until we get there.
She is wearing nothing but her suit; no towel in hand, no shoes on her feet, and I am suddenly and violently irritated. I do not want to walk in with my brother and sisters. I see everything as flawed and slightly magnified. I can tell that they will all be hanging around me making me look bad. We might as well be the Waltons. And that would make me John Boy on a very bad day.
Mom has been watching me furtively and casually tells me I look very pretty. She smiles into my eyes, then turns and tells the other three to do as I say and not to bother me too much. I feel better.
Jeff and Kay are already out the door. I follow Sharon.
The closer we get to the pool and the smell of chlorine and the shrieks and splashing and music, the more nervous I get.
As we cross the parking lot, I see teenagers everywhere. They are tan and laughing and flirting. Boys are pushing girls in and holding them under. And in a sudden panic, I desperately want to be the big sister again. �Hey! You guys wait up and walk with us or we�ll just go home,� I say in a tone that comes off as normal and bossy, but shakes underneath where only I can hear it �
Jeff is dancing around the entrance like Muhammad Ali. Kay is leaning her forehead against the wire mesh fence staring at people with her goggles on while they eat. Sharon is quiet and close, like an unexpected breeze. I am filled with gratitude and love for her. She is reading me.
Once inside, I am stunned.
We go through a dark tunnel of membership cards, wet concrete and shivering children with purple lips; out to what can only be described as the entrance to center stage.
The sun is blinding. The colors and sounds overwhelming. I�m aware of being watched, whether I am or not. Kay starts to take off for the edge of the pool to jump in, but I make them follow me to a grassy knoll at the far end, past the diving boards. I figure I�ll have a good view from there and it�s up on the hill so people won�t be able to watch me as much.
I pick a spot, towels are tossed and the next thing I know, I�m alone.
Kay left for the boards by herself and is standing in line at the low dive. I see the pale backs of Sharon and Jeff as they run for the pool, their shorts and t-shirts skimmed off in seconds �
All eyes are on me. I am sure of this now. And they are judging me.
I straighten the towels out. I use my feet to take off my tennis shoes and undo my shorts slowly. I stare into the distance as if I am being paid to keep an eye on someone. I make a great show of being bored and relaxed. I sit down alone, prop myself up on my elbows, and start to actually see �
Now and then I hear the lifeguard blow his whistle and realize I have been hearing it a lot and that they are blowing it at Kay, and some kid she has met, for doing cannonballs too close to his chair. The lifeguards all wear bright orange suits and white stuff on their noses. They swing their whistles. They look cool.
�Kay! Get over here!� I yell as if she were a dog. She runs over soaking wet and already tan. I let her know that if she embarrasses me again I�ll �
� I�ll tell on her.
I try and find Jeff and Sharon�s heads amidst the hundreds of heads in the water. Suddenly it seems like everyone in the pool has the same color hair.
Finally, I spot Sharon out of the water and heading towards me. I can tell she can�t remember where the towel is and I wave to her until she spots me. I am relieved to see her.
We lie back in the sun and talk about how cool it�s going to be to hang out here the rest of the summer.

We grow silent.
The sun feels incredible and I find I have quit thinking, which is so rare that it excites me right back into thinking again.
I prop myself back up and look over my sister at a blanket full of girls. They seem to be about my age. Of the four of them, three have the same haircut I do. After reading the Underlying Material, you meet with the producers who tell you that they would like to introduce some non-white characters into this small rural town�and would like you to initiate that in your script.
OceanofPDF.com

Questions to Consider:
�
Do you think you will use any sort of Three-Act Structure elements? Does the Hero�s Journey apply? Can you envision a Story Shape to your episode?

�
Will you focus on one person�s story, or tell more than one story?

�
Who are your main characters and what are their Motivations, Objectives, and Intentions? How will dialogue help illustrate these elements?

�
Where is your main tension coming from? How will you develop tension throughout the script?

�
Where is the emotional center of the screenplay going to lie? Which techniques will you use to craft your primary and secondary emotions?

�
What sort of archetypes jump out at you, if any? Will you need to create any, or are you going to sidestep archetypes at this point?

�The Pool� Discussion
Reflecting back on this adaptation, think about the assignment, the decisions made, and the outcome. Notice whether or not you are seeing the script as a whole or if you are identifying each element as a different lens that might be adjusted to improve the script.

Questions to consider:
�  What genre was this script written in?
�  Was an identifiable structure and/or story shape used?
�  Was this a faithful adaptation? Why or why not?
�  What decisions were made regarding character and dialogue?
�  Were archetypes used or avoided?
�  How was tension developed?
�  What was the spine and what was the theme of the screenplay? Were
they different from in the short story?
�  Were Objectives and Intentions used to create tension? To develop
theme? How did dialogue (and/or action) illustrate these elements?
�  What were some key decisions made by the main characters? How
were  these  decisions  informed  by  characters�  Motivations,
Objectives, and Intentions?
�  When (if anywhere) did primary and secondary emotions differ? Why
was this technique used (or not used)? What other techniques elicited
emotion?
�  How would you have adapted the story differently?

Thematic Approaches

In 2008�9, I had the pleasure of mentoring Michael Kortlander through his Honors tutorial thesis entitled Introducing the Literary Film: A Study of �Non-Recurring Phenomenon��Unconventional Screenplays That Should Have Failed, but Succeeded�a copy of which can be obtained through the Ohio University library system. I highly recommend the read. Michael analyzed many of the common ideas taught in screenwriting books, determined to prove that there are many other ways to write screenplays than to simply follow the guidelines of McKee, Seger, and Field. We spent the entire year debating, hypothesizing, challenging, and arguing about the screenwriting process�how it should be taught, and how it should be practiced. It was a fantastic year.
OceanofPDF.com

The Kortlander-Williams Matrix
Of all the ideas we discussed, the one that intrigued and surprised me the most was our conversation on theme. I consider theme to be a key to adaptation and to writing in general. Therefore, I was surprised when Michael wrote, �I looked at over forty of the best-selling screenplay books on the market today and found no consistently agreed-upon definition for theme. For his part, screenwriting guru Syd Field ignores theme almost entirely.�
Over the course of many months, we discussed Theme. Michael was inspired by the likes of David Mamet and Flannery O�Connor, who tend to believe that the role of a writer is to tell stories that underscore the complexities of the human experience�not to educate an audience. Therefore, the theme of the story should not be concrete or easily explained. Mamet claims that stories demonstrating a moral certainty reward the audience with a superficial feeling of moral or ethical superiority, and believes that stories should not be designed to change an audience. If they do, that�s fine�but that should not be the intent of the writer. For O�Connor, stories that have only one clearly articulated meaning are flat. She believes that theme is so complex that it cannot be summed up in a single statement, but only through the reading of the story as a whole. She wrote:
When you can state the theme of a story, when you can separate it from the story itself, then you can be sure the story is not a very good one�. When anybody asks what a story is about, the only proper thing is to tell them to read the story.
Linda Seger falls on the opposite side of the fence, arguing �The great writer is part philosopher, part psychologist, part theologian.� Michael referred to Seger and McKee (and others) as �Traditional screenwriters,� defined as those who believe that the screenwriter is an educator, and who typically crafts themes that are clearly articulated and understood. Through the audience�s (and characters�) understanding of the theme, there should be a change in attitude by the end of the story. In order for this to occur, Traditional stories typically focus on a single theme or on complementary themes that are often connected to a character flaw that a protagonist must overcome by story�s end.
Writers like O�Connor and Mamet stood at the other end of the spectrum. Non-traditional screenwriters work with messy and complex themes�often multiple, contradictory themes rooted within various characters�instead of prescribing a clear lesson or moral to the story. The storyteller is not designing the story to create a change in the audience. They do not see themselves as an educator, philosopher, or theologian.
Michael created a continuum with Traditional Scripts on one end, and Non-traditional Scripts on the other. Writers do not choose one camp or the other. They typically find themselves sliding across the continuum.
Figure 8.1  Kortlander Continuum of Theme
Table 8.1  Different approaches to theme�Traditional vs. Non-traditional
Traditional Scripts  Non-Traditional Scripts

Writer is part educator, philosopher, and/or Rather than moralizing, writer explores theologian complexities of the human experience Clean/clear themes Messy/complex themes
Single or complementary themes Multiple, often contradictory, themes Designed to elicit a change in character or Not designed to elicit a change in character or audience audience
In the end, we came to realize that all good stories contain ideas that intrigue the audience. It is not a resolution or a synthesis of these ideas that attract us�but the emotions and thoughts they elicit. �Theme� is amorphous, taking on many shapes. Michael defined theme as �the abstract idea or ideas made concrete by the telling of the story� and identified five central ways to conceptualize theme within a story:
1.
A moral to the story: If you work hard, you will eventually be rewarded.

2.
A descriptive statement: Love conquers all.

3.
A question: Is it just to harm one for the greater good?

4.
A binary: Love versus Hate, Order versus Chaos

5.
Individual concepts: Betrayal, Vanity, Madness.

To me, the idea of Traditional or Non-traditional theme is not enough. The screenwriter must also consider how this theme will be infused into the story. Visually, the storyteller can craft metaphors and motifs to illustrate and punctuate ideas. However, the strongest delivery method will usually be dialogue. Most films are rich with dialogue. And so, either by direct inclusion or intentional exclusion, dialogue will inform the story�s theme. It is the screenwriter�s job, therefore, to decide what dialogue goes into the story and what dialogue should be left out. This is especially important for adaptations where much of the story (and theme) exists in the underlying material.
The first impulse of many writers adapting material is to didactically present both story and theme. I do not use �didactic� in a patronizing way. I intend for it to mean �explanatory� in that the dialogue provides information in a straightforward manner. The audience does not need to interpret the meaning of the dialogue. There is an objective understanding of what the words mean. The opposite would be an �Active� approach to dialogue, where the meaning of the words is unclear, and the audience must actively interpret what is being said. The conclusions are subjective. This approach takes Michael�s continuum, and turns it into a matrix.
Figure 8.2 Kortlander-Williams Theme Matrix

Spider-Man: Traditional/Didactic
A Traditional/Didactic approach is one where the theme is clearly articulated in dialogue, the theme is simple and easy to understand, and it is a central, unifying theme that remains consistent through the story and teaches the protagonist (and audience) a lesson. A perfect example can be found in David Koepp�s script for Spider-Man. On page 36
Koepp writes a scene where Peter Parker (aka Spider-Man) hides his superpowers from his uncle.
INT. OLDSMOBILE DELTA 88 � DUSK
Uncle Ben is at the wheel, Peter beside him, moves to get out.
PETER:  Thanks for the ride.
UNCLE BEN:  Hold on a minute� We need to talk.
PETER:  Talk about what?
UNCLE BEN:  You�re not the same guy lately. Fights in school, shirking your chores,
you barely say a word to me or your aunt � what�s the story?

Peter unconsciously pulls his sleeves down over his wrists. He just can�t tell him.
PETER: There�s no story.
UNCLE BEN: You�re changing, and that�s normal. This is the age when a man becomes the man he�s going to be for the rest of his life. Just be careful who you change into. Okay?
PETER: I feel all this, all this power, but I don�t know what it means, or how to control it, or what I�m supposed to do with it, even.
UNCLE BEN: You�ll figure it out. You�re one smart cookie, always have been. But knowledge is power. And with great power comes great responsibility. Don�t ever forget that.
PETER: Yeah, yeah, I know all that, it�s not what I�m talking about. You wouldn�t understand.
UNCLE BEN: Wanna bet? When I was your age I went through exactly the same things.
PETER: No you didn�t, that�s my whole point. It�s just possible that something unique is happening to me, isn�t it? UNCLE BEN: Believe it or not, it passes. You grow out of it. PETER: I�m not gonna grow� just drop it, I�ll figure it out myself. UNCLE BEN: Look, if you won�t give me so much as a clue, I can�t help you. PETER: I didn�t ask for help. UNCLE BEN: I know I�m not your father, Peter�
PETER: Then stop pretending to be. There are some things you say that you wish you could pluck right back out of the air. This is one of them. And it�s too late. Uncle Ben�s feelings are deeply hurt. He looks away.
UNCLE BEN: I�ll pick you up on this corner at nine o�clock. Peter wants to say something. But can�t.
Tragically, this is the last time that Peter will see his uncle. Later, Peter refuses to use his powers to help stop a criminal. This criminal kills Peter�s uncle�thereby searing Uncle Ben�s didactic message into Peter�s brain: �With great power comes great responsibility.� This becomes the central theme of the story, with Koepp reinforcing it about twenty pages later, after Uncle Ben has died.
INT. PETER�S BEDROOM � NIGHT
Peter sits on the side of his bed. His door left ajar. In a moment Aunt May appears at the door, knocks. Enters. She looks at him, then puts his diploma on his desk and his science prize on a shelf along with a few other awards.
She moves to the bed and sits beside him. Finally: PETER: I missed him a lot today. AUNT MAY: I know. I miss him too. (takes his hand) But he was there. PETER: I just wish I hadn�t � AUNT MAY: Peter, don�t start that again. PETER: I can�t help thinking about the last thing I said to him. AUNT MAY: Stop it.
PETER: He tried to tell me something important and I threw it in his face. AUNT MAY: You loved him. And he loved you. He never doubted the man you would grow into. How you were meant for great things. You won�t
disappoint him. Or me. She waits another moment, then squeezes his hand and gets up. Moves to the door. She leaves quietly closing the door all the way. Peter gets up, opens a dresser drawer,
moves some sweaters out of the way, digs all the way to the bottom of the drawer�
� where his Spider-Man costume is crumpled, the red sweatshirt lying on top of it. The spider outline, sketched on its front. UNCLE BEN: (V.O.) Remember� with great power comes great responsibility.
Koepp�s didactic method turns Uncle Ben into a mentor for Peter, guiding him to embrace his Spider-Man identity and to use his powers wisely. It is a lesson for both Peter and the audience�one so important that Koepp states it again during the denouement. Spider-Man has defeated the Green Goblin and saved Mary Jane�who finds Peter at Uncle Ben�s gravesite. We join the scene as they break from a kiss, Peter trying to choose between love and being a superhero:
CLOSE ON M.J. in love.
BACK TO PETER, he forces himself to turn. WE STAY CLOSE ON HIM as he walks away. M.J. receding in the BG. SPIDER-MAN: (V.O.) (Sadder, wiser) Can�t say I didn�t warn you. The story of my life
is not for the faint of heart. A wind kicks up. Peter pulls his coat tighter. SPIDER-MAN: (V.O.) Like the man said, it�s hard to be a saint in the city. But
whatever life holds in store for me, I now accept. I will never forget these words again: Peter looks over his shoulder at M.J., still standing by the gravesite. SPIDER-MAN: (V.O.) �With great power comes great responsibility.� He continues on. We move in closer to his face. SPIDER-MAN: (V.O.) This is my gift. It is my curse. Who am I? Peter cracks a little smile. SPIDER-MAN: (V.O.) Baby, I�m spider-man.
Didactic themes are common in superhero stories, which are often morality tales for teenagers and young adults. Koepp chose a traditional approach consistent with the Spider-Man comics�garnering a People�s Choice nomination for Best Motion Picture, an MTV Movie nomination for Best Movie, and grossing more than $800 million worldwide.

The Dark Knight: Traditional/Active
Not all superhero films need to be didactic. In The Dark Knight Jonathan and Christopher Nolan took a traditional approach with a Good vs. Evil/Right vs. Wrong theme, but introduced it in a more complex and active way. At first, it appeared as if the Nolans were going to take a didactic approach, clearly explaining the backstory of Batman�s antagonist (The Joker). On page 40, the Joker threatens a mobster named Gambol:

The Joker gets a switchblade in Gambol�s mouth � sharp metal pulling the cheek taught. The bounty hunters subdue the remaining bodyguards.
THE JOKER: Wanna know how I got these scars? My father was a drinker and a fiend. He�d beat mommy right in front of me. One night he goes off crazier than usual, mommy gets the kitchen knife to defend herself. He doesn�t like that. Not. One. Bit.
The Joker TUGS Gambol�s cheek with the blade.
THE JOKER: So, me watching, he takes the knife to her, laughing while he does it. Turns to me and says �why so serious?� Comes at me with the knife � �why so serious?� Sticks the blade in my mouth � �let�s put a smile on that face� and�
The Joker looks up at the ashen faces of the remaining bodyguards. Smiles.
THE JOKER: Why so serious?
The Nolans soon reveal that they are more interested in engaging the audience actively than in spoon-feeding them information. About twenty pages later, the Joker has broken into a party, threatening guests to find District Attorney Harvey Dent. It appears as if the Joker is going to revisit the story about his father, but then he reverses course:
A distinguished Gentleman steps into the Joker�s path.
GENTLEMAN: We�re not intimidated by thugs.
The Joker stops. Stares at the man. SMILES AFFECTIONATELY.
THE JOKER: You know, you remind me of my father. (GRABS him) I hated my father.
The Joker has his blade in the Gentleman�s mouth.
RACHEL: (OS) Stop!
The Joker drops the Gentleman. Turns to Rachel.
THE JOKER: Hello, beautiful. You must be Harvey�s squeeze. (runs the knife across her cheek) And you are beautiful. You look nervous � it�s the scars isn�t it? Wanna know how I got them? I had a wife, beautiful like you. Who tells me I worry too much. Who says I need to smile more. Who gambles. And gets in deep with the sharks. One day they carve her face, and we�ve got no money for surgeries. She can�t take it. (presses knife into her cheek) I just want to see her smile again. I just want her to know I don�t care about the scars. So I put a razor in my mouth and do this to myself� And you know what? (starts laughing) She can�t stand the sight of me� (or crying) She leaves! See, now I see the funny side. Now I�m always smiling.
Now the audience is in a quandary. They thought the didactic information from the previous scene was true. But now they have been told a contradictory story by the same person. This creates an active response in the audience. They must choose one story or the other, or choose to discount The Joker altogether. This is exactly the Nolans� point�and the theme of the film.
The Nolans appear to be introducing a complex set of themes into the story (Harvey Dent choosing a life of crime as Two-Face; Rachel Dawes choosing between Dent and Bruce Wayne; and Joker enticing Batman to kill him). They seem to be taking a non-traditional approach, exploring multiple themes and ideas. But they are not. The Nolans are exploring only one concept: Choice. More specifically: the choices that define us as individuals and as a society.
The Joker forces everyone to make a choice. Just as he forces the audience to choose which story to believe, he forces Batman to choose to save Rachel or Harvey, and he forces the citizens of Gotham City to choose which boat to blow up. Harvey Dent gets the joke. When he becomes the villain Two-Face, every decision he makes is based on the removal of choice. He simply flips a coin. In the third act, the Nolans reveal the theme to Batman and the audience.
THE JOKER: Killing is making a choice�
Batman PUNCHES the Joker across the face. HARD.
BATMAN: WHERE ARE THEY?!
The Joker FEEDS off Batman�s anger. Loving it.
THE JOKER: � you choose one life over the other. Your friend, the district attorney. Or his blushing bride-to-be.
Batman PUNCHES the Joker again. The Joker LAUGHS.
THE JOKER: You have nothing. Nothing to threaten me with. Nothing to do with all your strength� (spits a tooth) But don�t worry, I�m going to tell you where they are. Both of them, and that�s the point � you�ll have to choose.
The Nolans chose to Actively deliver their Traditional theme of Choice, engaging the audience and forcing them to participate in the story. This is a very effective way to tell stories to an audience that is willing to jump through such mental hoops.

Glengarry Glen Ross: Non-Traditional/Didactic
David Mamet uses didactic delivery to tell a Non-traditional story in Glengarry Glen Ross. As a playwright, Mamet revels in dialogue. This screenplay was adapted from his own play. There are only a handful of locations and characters. Dialogue�beautiful, dynamic, and complex� dominates the film. Take this example of a real estate salesman Ricky Roma trying to engage an unsuspecting bar patron to buy some land.
INT. THE RESTAURANT � NIGHT
Roma at a booth with James Lingk
ROMA: You know something, you know something? All train compartments smelled vaguely of shit. You take a train, you�re paying for a compartment, luxury, all this, all of the time, the thing smells like, it vaguely smells, and you ignore it. (beat) That�s the worst thing I can confess. You know how long it took me to get there, a long time. When you die, you�re going to regret the things that you don�t do. You think you�re queer? I�m going to tell you something: we�re all queer. You think that you�re a thief? You get befuddled by a middle-class morality? Get shut of it. Shut it out. You fuck little girls? So be it. There�s an absolute morality? Maybe. And then what? If you think there is, then be that thing. Bad people go to hell? I don�t think so. If you think that, act that way. A hell exists on earth? Yes. I won�t live in it. That�s me�
The character explains his ideas didactically, but the ideas are oblique. They don�t square with any sort of concrete theme. Yes, the film is about a crime so there is a sense of morality, but this is only connected tangentially to what Roma is saying. Compare Roma�s dialogue to a conversation between two other salesmen discussing morals:
MOSS: I know it�s� what, hey, what did I learn as a kid on Western? You don�t sell a guy one car� Eh? You sell him five cars, over fifteen years.
AARONOW: That�s right.
MOSS: Eh? You�re god damn right that�s right. Guys come in, oh, the, blah, blah, blah, I know what I�ll do: I�ll go out and rob everybody blind and go to Argentina �cause nobody ever thought of this before�
AARONOW: Huh�
MOSS: � and so they kill the goose. And a fuckin� man, worked all his life, has got to�
AARONOW: � that�s right�
MOSS: Cower in his boots.
AARONOW: (simultaneously with �boots�) Shoes, boots, yes.
MOSS: For some fuckin� �sell ten thousand, and you win the steak knives.�
AARONOW: Whatdaya, whatdaya do?
MOSS: What can you do?
Mamet, as storyteller, doesn�t have a point to make. He is not interested in making a moral stand. He is more interested in portraying the lives of these Chicago salesmen. The tension comes from our interest in the characters, not in determining their moral direction. By the end of the story, the mystery is solved and the guilty are imprisoned�but the story doesn�t change any of the characters. They go on with their lives, selling bogus real estate investments, and Mamet leaves it to the audience to decide if there is a moral to the story. The characters are fascinating, and the story grabs our attention. And that is enough.

The Revenant: Non-Traditional/Active
When dialogue is minimized, the film becomes more active. In the movie theater, audiences sit in darkness trying to make sense of the images they see. They are forced to interpret the images and their interpretation is subjective. Theme, imagery, and metaphor intertwine. But the screenwriter must put these images into words�they must write the script. By examining these written words, we are able to more clearly understand the themes in a seemingly ambiguous story.
Michael�s Punke�s script for The Revenant tells the story of a man (Hugh Glass) left for dead after being mauled by a bear. The year is 1823, and Glass struggles to avenge the death of his son by killing the man who abandoned him in the woods. It is a story of revenge, but Punke�s approach to theme is much more complex. Richard Lawson�s review in Vanity Fair concludes by stating:
Much hay will be made about The Revenant�s white-knuckle gruesomeness, and I suspect many viewers will take pleasure in feeling ragged but a little tougher for having sat through this slow, torturous adventure. Which, I think, is the intended effect. (Imagine how tough everyone feels for making it.) I don�t think anyone will leave the theater feeling terribly enlightened�not about man�s inhumanity to man, not about the dark mechanics of Western expansion, not about an incremental genocide that blights any rational view of American history. But they will feel tougher! What with the arrows and the claws and the horse guts and all. It really is quite something to watch The Revenant, a punishing and exhausting experience. Whether or not it�s worth it, though, is something every man�and, oh yeah, woman�must decide for himself.
Lawson�s criticism seems to fit nicely with Kortlander�s Non-traditional approach to theme. I believe Kortlander would argue that screenwriters Mark L. Smith and Alejandro G. I��rritu (who also directed the film) were not trying to �enlighten� the audience about �man�s inhumanity to man� or �the dark mechanics of Western expansion,� or even the �incremental genocide that blights any rational view of American history��as Lawson seems to require. Instead, Smith and I��rritu were trying to tell a harrowing and complex story about the human condition. Personally, I believe the screenwriters were actively engaging the audience on a metaphorical level �a level where one of the many themes of the story lies. This is only my subjective opinion, but this is how I interpret the screenplay.
To begin, it is important to know that a �revenant� is a person who has returned from the dead (according to the Collins English Dictionary). Specifically, a revenant is thought of as an animated corpse who returns to terrorize the living�as defined by historians such as Robert Bartlett. The character of Hugh Glass does exactly that: he returns from the dead to terrorize the living. Seen this way, The Revenant is more than just a survival story. It is more than just a revenge story. It is the story of a man�s actions (John Fitzgerald�s actions, the man who left Glass to die) coming back to haunt him. When reading the August 9, 2010 draft of the screenplay, it seems obvious to me that the screenwriters did not intend for Glass to seem human. He is much more of a wraith.
Until page 20, Glass is a mortal man. But when his companions discover Glass after he was mauled by a grizzly bear, the script describes him as a corpse:
� his throat is torn wide open� stomach and chest a gruesome design of gashes and cuts. His right leg is twisted in a horrible angle.
Bridger�s legs give out� he drops to a knee and vomits.
MURPHY: Oh, Jesus.
STUBBY BILL: He�s tore to pieces.
The men stare down at Glass� corpse.
As the men fight about whether they are in danger of being discovered by the local �Ree tribe, this corpse comes back to life for the first time and one of the men utters just two words: �Jesus Christ��evoking another famous person who rose from the dead. This is important, and will come into play, later.
The next twenty pages are dedicated to the men keeping Glass alive. They slowly nurture him back from the brink of death, but eventually this team of trappers and hunters needs to move on. They leave three men� including Fitzgerald�behind to care for Glass.
By page 40, Fitzgerald decides to kill Glass in order to save his own skin. Glass� friend�Pig�tries to stop Fitzgerald. Fitzgerald kills Pig, scalps him, and tells the third man (Bridger) that Pig�s scalped body is proof that they are about to be attacked by �Ree Indians. Bridger is terrified, allowing Fitzgerald to abandon Glass, leaving him to die.
Bridger�s frozen� doesn�t know what to do. So Fitzgerald suddenly GRABS GLASS BY THE ANKLES� starts dragging him across the ground. Glass GROANS IN PAIN.
BRIDGER: Wait!
But Fitzgerald isn�t waiting� he pulls Glass to that grave he dug, and ROLLS GLASS� BODY INSIDE.
Glass hits the bottom with a painful THUD.
FITZGERALD: Now we done what was asked of us. (hard to Bridger) Move.
Bridger stares at the open grave a moment, then fear sends him running after Fitzgerald�
� as Glass lies there INSIDE HIS OWN GRAVE.
After following Fitzgerald and Bridger for a while, we return to the camp where Glass was left. Notice the death-like descriptions:
EXT. CAMP � MORNING
Empty and quiet� no sign of life� until GLASS� HAND RISES BETWEEN THE LOOSE BRANCHES. His fingers dig into the earth, pulling himself up from the hole� a dead man climbing out of his own grave.
The screenwriters want to make clear that Glass has returned from the dead (yet again). There is no dialogue to didactically inform the audience, but the screenwriters are communicating this idea to the filmmakers in very clear language. This pattern continues.
About ten pages later, Glass succumbs to exhaustion and passes out. He wakes again as vultures peck at his skin�thinking that he is dead. He fights them off, and searches for shelter:
Glass crumbles back to the ground� squints up into the sun� the SILHOUETTES OF THE VULTURES CIRCLING ABOVE HIM� waiting for him to die.
Glass glances to a ridge just a few hundred yards away.
At the base of the ridge, a GIANT BOULDER has broken free, creating a partial cave. Glass starts crawling toward it.
And if it�s possible, he looks even closer to death now than when he started this journey.
EXT. CAVE � DAY
No more than ten feet deep, but enough to hide from predators. Glass slides as far back in the recess as he can� collapses against the rock wall.
Another Christ reference with the boulder moved from in front of the cave. Glass retreats into the cave. It appears as if he is going to die, but the voice of his son brings him back to life one more time.
INT. CAVE � DAY
TIGHT ON GLASS� FACE� eyes closed. He looks dead. BOY�S VOICE: (V.O.) Not yet. Not yet. Glass� eyes suddenly spring open.
EXT. CAVE � DAY
Glass crawls out of the crevice� shields his eyes from the sun, as he takes in the scene.
Twenty-five pages later, catastrophe strikes Glass and his horse. Chased by Elk�s Tongue and his warrior tribe, Glass and his horse plummet off a cliff, to certain death. Notice in this passage all of the different references and images relate to the thematic ideas of death, rebirth, and ghostly terror.
EXT. FOREST � CONTINUOUS
And the horse comes crashing through the branches� the massive limbs slowing her fall� snapping off as her body hits and twists� tosses Glass away.
Glass sails through the air� hits a slope, and tumbles down into a stream of icy water�
� sinks below the surface a moment� long enough that we�re sure he has to be dead� until he rises from the water� drags himself up to the snowy ground� collapses� shivering� staring up through the trees�
� to Elk�s Tongue and his Warriors peering down from the top of the cliff� with no way to get to Glass. Finally, they turn� disappear.
EXT. WOODS � NIGHT
Glass moves shivering through the trees� his body soaked� clothes beginning to ice over� body convulsing from the cold�
� when he finds the DEAD PAINT MARE twisted on the forest floor.
Glass grabs the loose blanket from the snow� shakily wraps it around himself. But it�s worthless in this frigid night air. Glass is going to freeze to death.
He stares at the dead horse a beat, then pulls the knife back, and SLAMS IT INTO THE HORSE�S STOMACH� begins slicing the mare�s belly open. His hands shake so much he can barely control the knife.
Blood and organs spill out, staining the snow. Steam rises from the remains. Glass keeps on cutting� turning his head from the smell, as he pulls out whatever doesn�t ooze out on its own� emptying the carcass.
Glass peels off his wet furs, then does what we didn�t think was possible� he begins crawling feet-first into the horse�s hollow belly� holding its ribs up so he can slip inside� curling up� SQUISHING IN� deeper� deeper� until only his head remains outside of the horse.
Glass wraps the blanket around his face and head� doing everything he can to survive.
DISSOLVE TO:
EXT. WILDERNESS � MORNING
The dead horse� its fur white and icy� Glass� blanket-wrapped head still protruding from the seam in its stomach. And nothing�s moving� the whole world looks frozen stiff�
� until Glass� head shifts� the CRACKING OF FROZEN FLESH, as he lifts the upper half of the mare�s belly. It�s like a cocoon tearing open. He rolls out of the carcass, hitting the ground, and squinting up into the warmth of the sun.
The horse�s blood covers his clothes. He slides the frozen fur back over himself, then gazes up at the sun for direction� starts walking. Alone� in the middle of nowhere� again.
Glass returns from the dead to terrorize the living. This theme is certainly not spoken.
Yet, oddly, this idea of our past returning to haunt us may have been the subconscious influence for Vanity Fair critic Richard Lawson to reflect upon topics such as �man�s inhumanity to man� or �the dark mechanics of Western expansion,� or even the �incremental genocide that blights any rational view of American history.� Lawson seems to want a didactic message, but Smith and I��rritu obviously did not. They seem content with infusing the idea of a Revenant, and allowing the audience to reflect upon the larger ideas themselves. All the screenwriters needed to do was provide subconscious visual imagery to stir those thoughts. Flannery O�Connor would be proud.
Figure 8.3 Kortlander-Williams Theme Matrix with examples
When placed on our Thematic Dialogue Axis, each of these examples falls squarely in a different quadrant. One quadrant is not �better� than another. They are different approaches. Screenwriters should consider the source material, the audience, and their own inclination as storytellers when deciding on an approach�active or didactic, traditional or non-traditional. This is an important conversation to have before starting any adaptation.

Reshaping Structure

A number of years ago, I was hired to rework a script and I started noodling around with different configurations. I was exploring the Hero�s Journey and simultaneously trying to figure out whether the original screenwriter had used a Three-Act Structure, when I discovered something: The Three-Act Structure and the Hero�s Journey were actually very similar. In fact, they can be laid atop one another as a creative and analytic tool. To demonstrate, let�s examine the Academy Award-winning script Argo written by Chris Terrio. In brief, Argo is about a CIA exfiltration expert�Tony Mendez�who is sent to Iran in 1979 to rescue six American embassy employees who narrowly escaped being taken hostage when Iranian students attacked the embassy and captured fifty of their colleagues. Mendez�with the help of two Hollywood insiders�devises a plan to disguise the diplomats as a Canadian film crew scouting Tehran for their upcoming science fiction film Argo.
Argo won an Academy Award for Best Adapted Screenplay in 2013, and similar awards that year from the Writers Guild of America, the Los Angeles Film Critics Association, and the Alliance of Women Film Journalists. The underlying properties for the Argo screenplay are a nine-page article from Wired magazine called �The Great Escape: How the CIA used a Fake Science Fiction Film to Sneak Six Americans Out of Revolutionary Iran� by Josh Bearman, and a fifty-one-page chapter titled �RAPTOR in the Dark� taken from Antonio Mendez�s book The Master of Disguise: My Secret Life in the CIA.
So, which structure did Terrio use when writing Argo? It is a question many writers ask themselves. Chris Terrio is a graduate from the USC film school, and certainly has an understanding of the Three-Act Structure and the Hero�s Journey. In my experience, I have found that studio heads often want stories to follow a Three-Act Structure. Yet, filmmakers like George Lucas (who also went to USC) seem to choose the Hero�s Journey as a template. Online, you can find David Poland�s DP/30 video series, where he interviews Terrio on his process of writing Argo. While Terrio does not directly mention a specific structural approach to the film, by analyzing the source material, the completed script, and Terrio�s interview, we can unpack some of the key decisions made to (consciously or subconsciously) fit one or both of these paradigms.
The Hero�s Journey is typically drawn as a circle, and the Three-Act Structure is typically drawn linearly. But, look closely for similarities. Notice that both structural representations divide the story into four equal segments.
Figure 9.1 Three-Act Structure
Figure 9.2 The Hero�s Journey

CALL TO ADVENTURE  The State Department calls CIA exfiltration expert Tony Mendez in for consultation. Mendez�s boss, Jack O�Donnell, tells him the State Department wants to do this on their own, without CIA help

Figure 9.3 Act I of The Hero�s Journey and Three-Act combined Table 9.1 Argo comparison with the Hero�s Journey and the Three-Act Structure�first 25%

Notice that if you put the first quarter of the Hero�s Journey on top of the first act of the Three-Act Structure, the Set Up simply encapsulates the first four steps of the Hero�s Journey (Ordinary World, Call to Adventure, Refusal of the Call and Mentor/Supernatural Event), followed by an event that incites the hero to leave. The only difference between these two models is that the Hero�s Journey describes this action (the Hero Must Leave), while the Three-Act Structure explains that this is the reason for our hero�s departure (the Inciting Incident). In Mendez�s case, both moments involve going to Hollywood to see if his plan is possible. Argo holds up to this dual model precisely.
This demonstrates to me that Terrio�consciously or subconsciously� used both models for the first act of Argo. I believe that we all do. I believe that if we use one model, we are, in fact, using both.
OceanofPDF.com

Case Study: Argo
Of course, some may say that Terrio was simply telling Mendez�s story, and that the story just happened to take Mendez to Hollywood�the fact that these events mirror some sort of model is pure happenstance. However, when examined more closely, we can see how Terrio�s adaptation of the underlying work actually manipulated events into these models, rather than taking them at face value. For instance, here is how Mendez himself described these events in The Master of Disguise:
I sent Headquarters a cable outlining our progress with the Canadians and presenting the movie-team option, as well as two other options: The six could pose as a group of Canadian nutritionists conducting a survey in the third world, or a group of unemployed teachers seeking jobs at international schools in the region. I was half expecting a flaming rocket in response to my suggestions, but Headquarters remained silent, always a good sign.
Over the next ten days, I shuttled between Washington and Ottawa, struggling to flesh out the complex logistical details of all three exfiltration cover options�. The sense of being engaged in a wartime effort intensified daily. Headquarters recognized that the Hollywood scenario had potential beyond the possibility of rescuing the six diplomats. [�] Between trips to Ottawa and intense planning sessions with the Near East Division, I made a quick trip to consult Jerome Calloway in California. I brought $10,000 in cash with me, the first of several �black bag� money deliveries to set up our cover motion picture company. Normally, such expenditures would be considered wasteful, since the Carter administration had not yet approved the movie option. But Admiral Turner and the Deputy Director for Operations realized that we had to be flexible, and that preparing such an elaborate facade took time and money.
So, in reality, Mendez never Refused the Call. In fact, he was developing three exfiltration options simultaneously. He also wasn�t struck with the movie-team idea out of the blue. He had worked with John Chambers in the past (called Jerome Calloway in the 1999 printing of the book to protect Chambers� identity). And, he was never sent to Hollywood to see if this was a reasonable plan. Headquarters liked the project and backed it financially from the start. When Mendez finally did travel to California, he brought money with him to set up the fake film company. Chambers mentored Mendez, by phone, but Mendez knew what he was doing�there was no need for Mendez to travel into an �unknown world� on an adventure. But it makes for a good movie. Terrio adapted the facts into an award-winning story by molding them into a structure.
But Terrio did more than just follow a step-by-step model. He added layers of complexity to this story. We�ll return to the Hero�s Journey in a moment, but to really understand what Terrio did, we need to simultaneously understand Argo�s Story Shape. Terrio was telling the story of four different groups:
1.
The Six escaped diplomats and their Canadian hosts

2.
The Iranian Revolutionaries and Iranian Guard searching for The Six

3.
The CIA and State Department officials trying to rescue The Six

4.
The Hollywood team pretending to make the fake Sci-Fi film Argo.

Oddly, these four groups never meet. If you sketched out the Story Shape, it might look like this:
Figure 9.4 Incomplete Story Shape for Argo
Needless to say, a movie about four entities that never meet makes for challenging storytelling. Terrio meets this challenge by having two specific �characters� that traverse the boundaries between these groups. The first is our hero�Tony Mendez. He starts as a part of the CIA group but quickly leaves this world, venturing to Hollywood then back to DC, then off to Iran where he meets with Iranian officials and with the diplomats and Canadians �all the while staying in touch with his teams in DC and LA. None of the other groups meet each other, but Mendez meets them all.
Figure 9.5 Story Shape for Argo with Mendez as binding agent
It is a clever way to tell the story, and creates an exciting rhythm by constantly cutting back and forth between the various story threads. Mendez, as a character, does not need to be written into every scene. The audience can watch the Iranian Revolutionaries piece together the shredded photographs, or the CIA infighting with the State Department, or the diplomats choosing their fate�all without Mendez physically present� because he is the glue holding each of these four threads together. Mendez becomes omnipresent.
This structure also provides Terrio with some interesting choices. Mendez actually enters two new worlds: first Hollywood, and then Tehran. As mentioned earlier, Terrio could have minimized Mendez�s trip to Hollywood and spent more time in Tehran, but instead he went the other way. He built upon the Hollywood story because Terrio recognized an opportunity to say something important through his use of story structure. In his interview with David Poland, Terrio explains:
The film is about storytelling and who hijacks the story. You know: the Iranian students understand this. In that scene with the montage and the reading of the screenplay, there�s a very deliberate juxtaposition of the cameras watching Tehran Mary talk about colonialism and America�s place in the world, and then the cameras of the reading of the screenplay, because the student hostage-takers knew very well what it meant to have these images beamed around the world. And so then, in that [earlier] scene, when [John Chambers] is trying to coach [Lester Siegel] to take on the movie, he says, �You ever think about how it�s just for the cameras?� and that, for me, became part of the core message: that in order to understand the modern world and the 24-hour news cycle and the way that stories sort of snowball, and how story defines policy (even our Middle Eastern policy) that [the Iranian Hostage Crisis] was the embryo of a lot of that. I think that to even understand the modern Middle East, you have to go back to the gates of the embassy.
Terrio had two key ideas in mind to build out this idea of people hijacking stories for their own purposes:
1.
Tehran Mary and the Iranian students taking the world stage and hijacking an international political story for their own aims

2.
Mendez, Chambers, and Siegel hijacking the truth about making the faux film Argo to support their own agenda

Terrio juxtaposes both of these ideas off of each other in this montage in the middle of the film. It is worth noting that Terrio identifies this scene as the first scene he envisioned of the film and a key part of the pitch he gave to executives at Smokehouse Pictures that landed him the screenwriting job. The scene begins with actors gathering in Hollywood for an orchestrated media event surrounding a script reading of the faux Argo. Mendez is acting the part of the associate producer on the film. We pick up the scene on page
52 of Terrio�s March 25, 2010 draft:

over the battlefield and hold there�

As pointed out earlier, Terrio developed two �characters� to tie the four story threads together. One is: Mendez. The other is: Media (specifically television and print). Characters throughout the film are forever watching the news, and referring to magazines and newspapers. A second Story Shape could be drawn like this:
Figure 9.6 Story Shape for Argo with Media as binding agent
Conceptually then, Terrio knew that by the middle of the film he wanted to give equal weight to both the Hollywood story and the Tehranian story. He devised a dual Story Structure to omnisciently move between these two worlds. The Media Story Structure allowed the audience to intellectually connect with what was going on in both places, but in order for the audience to become emotionally invested in both threads Terrio needed to adjust the Three-Act Structure (and the Hero�s Journey).
Figure 9.7 Second quarter of The Hero�s Journey
Traditionally, after the hero moves across the first threshold, he is confronted by a number of Tests before Approaching the Cave and eventually confronting a Supreme Ordeal. When laid upon the Three-Act Structure, the combined model looks like this:
Figure 9.8 First half of Act II: The Hero�s Journey and Three-Act Structure combined
Terrio uses this model when Mendez ventures to Hollywood and is greeted by his Mentor and Threshold Guardian, John Chambers. Together, they proceed through a series of tests:
�
Convincing Lester Siegel to join their team;

�
Finding an appropriate script;

�
Battling a snaky agent to purchase the script at a reasonable price.

Traditional expectations lead us to believe that our hero is now ready for an important confrontation. But instead, Terrio does something very interesting. He tosses the Hero�s Journey aside momentarily and doubles back the way he came. He forces Mendez to cross yet another Threshold� this one into the Middle East.
Again, Mendez is greeted by a Threshold Guardian�now in the shape of a CIA agent who informs him of the changing routine of getting into (and out of) Iran. Terrio also gives Mendez a whole new set of tests:
�
Convince the Iranian Film Commission that he is a filmmaker.

�
Convince each of The Six diplomats that his plan is going to work.

By taking this approach, both Mendez and the audience have now traveled emotionally through both worlds, and are collectively invested in both threads. Terrio�s new design looks something like Figure 9.8, and now we are prepared for his montage scene.
Figure 9.9 First half of Terrio�s Act II: The Hero�s Journey and Three-Act Structure combined
From this point forward, Terrio returns to the Hero�s Journey/Three-Act Structure. When fully synthesized, the advanced model that Terrio�s story theoretically used looks like this:
Figure 9.10 Argo: The Hero�s Journey and Three-Act Structure combined
Or, seen as a table, represented like this:

. . . . . . . .
Crafting and Honing Tension

Syd Field contends that Tension needs to continuously rise through the second act until it reaches its climax in the third.
Figure 10.1 The Hero�s Journey��Tension� as Y-axis
I realize that many writers feel like this model suggests a �paint by numbers� approach. I suggest looking at it differently: You should come up with your story however you want to come up with it. Write it off the top of your head, noodle it out in outline form, meticulously plan it out in long hand. Whatever. The creative process you use to create the first draft of your story doesn�t matter. What matters most is whether or not you (and your collaborators) like it. Can it be improved? If it doesn�t need improvement: Congratulations. You�re a genius. If it does need improvement, welcome to the club. Personally, when my script needs improvement, I want to use a variety of tools to find and fix the problem. The more tools in your toolbox, the easier it is to do the job right. Of course, you need to understand how the tools work, first.
Case in point: the entire premise of the Three-Act Structure has �Tension� as the Y-axis (and �Time� as the X-axis). The Inciting Incident begins the rising tension for the story, and the Climax is the peak of that tension. And fundamentally, within the script itself, many screenwriting books suggest that each scene should contain an element of Tension. Tension, tension, tension.
The funny thing is: most screenwriting books don�t tell you what Tension is. Or they describe it very simply as �conflict.� But personally (and professionally) that doesn�t seem to make much sense. Every scene in a movie doesn�t have to contain conflict. Tension? Yes. But conflict? I don�t think so.
OceanofPDF.com

Building Tension
From my perspective, Tension doesn�t necessarily involve the characters in the story. Tension involves the audience. Period. If the audience is bored, then there is no tension. Even if the characters are screaming at each other, if the audience leaves the theater or the reader puts down the script then that is a clear indication that Tension has been lost.
So, if that is the true measure of Tension�and I would argue that it is� then the most simple definition of creating Tension in a screenplay is: to write the story in a way that will keep the reader turning the pages (or keep the audience glued to their seats).
This is true for directors as well, but they have different tools. A boring scene can be made more interesting with music. A camera move will make a lifeless shot more dynamic. And a great actor can keep the audience�s interest piqued with charisma.
Screenwriters don�t have music, famous actors, special effects, or camera moves at their disposal. Instead, we work with more nuanced methods of captivating our audience. Editing, imagery, dialogue, and actions must be manipulated and woven together into complex patterns to draw our readers in. Broadly speaking, there are five different approaches to tension.
Five Ways to Build Tension
1.
Tension through Conflict

2.
Tension through Understanding

3.
Tension through Entertainment

4.
Tension through Engagement

5.
Tension through Anticipation

OceanofPDF.com

Tension through Conflict
The �go to� way to create tension is through conflict. When two characters are at odds, there will almost certainly be some sort of dramatic tension. Knowing that each character has a set of Objectives and Intentions, the conflict can be created in a number of ways.
Two characters can have the same Intention but have opposite Objectives. The film Moneyball is about a Major League team trying to rebuild its squad with a limited budget after three star players leave. The General Manager (Billy Beane) wants to use a new statistical approach to recruit players and to determine how/when to play them, while Team Manager (Art Howe) wants to use traditional scouting methods and seasoned intuition to guide recruiting and coaching. This creates a natural tension because the audience begins rooting for one character over the other �not because of their personalities, but simply because their Objectives are at odds.
Table 10.1 Moneyball example of similar Intentions but different Objectives
Beane�s Intention Beane�s Objective
In order to rebuild the team Beane wants to use a statistical matrix to recruit new players �
Howe�s Intention Howe�s Objective
In order to rebuild the team Howe wants to use time-tested scouts (and instinct) to recruit new � players
Two characters can also have the same Objective but have opposite Intentions. For instance, Beane hires economist Peter Brand to be his assistant GM. Beane is focused on the human aspect of the matrix. This model is helping him understand his team better, and to understand himself and his own career. Brand is more of a numbers wonk. His economic theory is in play, and he is more focused on whether or not the numbers work than interacting with human beings. Naturally, this causes tension as Bean coaxes Brand to interact more with the players.
Table 10.2 Moneyball example of different Intentions but similar Objectives
Beane�s Intention  Beane�s Objective
In order to rebuild the team  Beane wants to use a statistical matrix to recruit new players
�
Brand�s Intention  Brand�s Objective
In order to test his model �  Brand wants to use a statistical matrix to recruit new players

Conflict can also be created through the use of contrast. This works especially well at the scene level. A smart character paired with a dumb character in a scene creates contrast. Comedic scripts do this especially well. Think of how combinations like smart/dumb, strong/weak, fat/skinny, scared/brave keep the audience interested in what will happen next. We know that there will be more tension if a strong prisoner escapes with a weak prisoner tagging along, than if two strong prisoners escape together.

Tension through Understanding
In contrast to the more visceral tension created through conflict, tension can also be created on the more intellectual side of an audience. In a film, we can listen in on conversations that we wouldn�t normally hear. We can watch people do things that we wouldn�t normally be allowed to watch them do.
There is something mesmerizing about watching a junkie prepare his fix, a prisoner digging a tunnel, and a beauty applying makeup. People like to watch a process�especially if it is something unique to them (fixing a shot of heroine), done under unique circumstances (digging an escape tunnel), or highly personal (applying makeup).
The same can be said for new ideas, or a new way of explaining old ideas. Consider this monologue from Edward R. Murrow from the first two pages of George Clooney and Grant Heslov�s screenplay Good Night, and Good Luck.
INT. CHICAGO THEATER
October 15, 1958. The Radio and Television News Directors Association Annual meeting. We�re in the wings of the theater. Standing there alone is Edward R. Murrow. He looks slightly ill at ease. He lights a cigarette� he looks at some notes in his hand as we overhear his glowing introduction by the MC. Cheers and applause as we walk with him to the podium. A long awkward pause.
MURROW: This might just do nobody any good. At the end of this discourse a few people may accuse this reporter of fouling his own comfortable nest, and your organization may be accused of having given hospitality to heretical and even dangerous thoughts.
CUT TO: Close-ups of people listening. Smiling. Not aware that this is not to be the comfortable acceptance speech you might hear of a retiring employee getting his gold watch. This will become what will later be called �The Box of Lights and Wires Speech�. One of THE MOST IMPORTANT broadcast journalism speeches EVER. It is an attack on everyone in this room� an attack on himself. And, at this point, it has just begun.
MURROW: But the elaborate structure of Networks, Advertising Agencies and Sponsors will not be shaken or altered. It is my desire, if not my duty, to try to talk to you journeymen with some candor about what is happening to radio and television. If what I have to say is responsible, then I alone am responsible for saying it. Our history will be what we make of it. And if there are any historians about fifty or a hundred years from now, and there should be preserved the Kinescopes for one week of all three networks, they will there find recorded in black-and-white, or color, evidence of decadence� escapism, and insulation from the realities of the world in which we live. We are currently wealthy, fat, comfortable and complacent. We have a built-in allergy to unpleasant or disturbing information. Our mass media reflect this. But unless we get up off our fat surpluses and recognize that television in the main is being used to distract, delude, amuse and insulate us, then television and those who financed it, those who look at it and those who work at it, may see a totally different picture too late.
There is no inherent conflict in these words (or actions), but the audience hangs on each sentence because they are trying to untangle what Murrow is talking about. That�s tension.
And other times, the audience tries to understand the film�s environment, not for themselves, but for the sake and well-being of the character in the film. If our protagonist goes off to prison, we listen very carefully to the rules being set by the warden. Why? We are not trying to understand the prison bylaws because it affects us personally. We are not listening to make sure that they got it right. We listen on behalf of our character. We are worried about him. We want to make sure that he knows what the rules are because we want him to survive. We want him to be okay, and we are trying to learn with him � or for him. We are trying to understand how he is going to survive, and in order to do that, we need to pay keen attention to the entire speech.
Here is an example from Oliver Stone�s June, 1977 draft of Midnight Express. By page 28 our protagonist, Billy, has been arrested in Turkey and severely beaten on his first night. Midway through the long courtyard scene, two other prisoners�Bell and Erich�tell Billy the reality of his situation. Even though we know most of the story from seeing it in the first act, we are still just as riveted as Billy is.

BELL:  (shaking his head) History, man, history. How much?

BILLY:  The airport. Trying to get on the plane for the States.

BILLY:  (tensing) Twenty months?
BELL:  Twenty fucking YEARS, man � YEARS! I figger ten at the least.

BILLY stunned.

Tension through Entertainment
Of course, we all like to be entertained. It is one of the main reasons why we go to the movies in the first place. But, does entertainment in and of itself create tension? I would say: yes. If you are drawn into a performance, then by our definition it is creating tension. This is especially true when there is entertainment embedded within the film itself. Case in point: In the Coen brothers� Inside Llewyn Davis the character Llewyn often sings entire songs up on stage, uninterrupted with shots of anything other than him singing. But the audience is captivated. The songs are beautiful. The character is tragic. And the tragedy is intensified by the beauty of the songs. People demonstrating their craft at a high level is entertaining, and audiences will watch this as long as it continues to move the story forward, creates emotion, or builds theme.
The same is true for sex. Not that sex is a craft (although, ahem � it may be one of the oldest). It is certainly something that people rarely find boring. People like to watch love scenes. Now, granted: different strokes for different folks but, in general, love scenes engage an audience. So does nudity�or even hints of nudity. Perhaps it is the voyeur in all of us, but people rarely look away when there is a half-naked person on the screen.
In fact, the television show Game of Thrones routinely creates scenes with half-dressed characters (often engaged in pre-or post-coitus) when they need to deliver a lot of exposition. Typically, exposition can bore the audience, but with naked people on screen, the writers knew that no one would be getting up to make a sandwich during those scenes. The show employed this technique so often that television critic Myles McNutt coined the phrase sexposition (instead of exposition). And it works. In Michael Tolkin�s script for The Player, a Hollywood executive (Griffin) is being stalked by a disgruntled writer. He is worried that the man wants to kill him, but doesn�t want anyone to know about the situation. In this scene, there is only one plot point: Griffin wants to find out how long his girlfriend, Bonnie, thinks the stalker would take to try to kill him. This is pure exposition that the audience needs to know about Griffin�s state of mind. First, read the scene from Tolkin�s novel of the same name:
Griffin told Jan to get Mary Netter. After a moment she told him that Mary was in a meeting. Griffin said it was important, Jan put him on hold, and in a few seconds Mary got on the line.

�Yes.�

�Someone sends a gift, she doesn�t get a thank you note and she gets really steamed?�
�He. And he gets mad enough to pick a fight.�
�A man�s getting this angry? This is a comedy, no?�
�It�s just an idea right now.�
�If this isn�t a comedy, a man shouldn�t get that angry. You�re describing indignation, which is not for heroes of movies. Whose idea is this?�
�Forget the film for a minute. How long would you wait before you waited long enough?�
�When I give a gift to someone and the gift and the person are special to me, I get very excited every time I imagine the person unwrapping the box. If it�s for a girl, I think about her saving the ribbon, because I save the ribbon. So, if you�ve sent the gift by mail, or even if you had the store deliver it, how long does that take? Three days local, a week to New York? If you send something nice and you don�t hear back within two weeks, you have a right to be really angry�if they got the gift, of course� unless they got run over by a truck. You imagine your friend with this precious thing and he�s enjoying it, but he�s not responding. Maybe he hated it. He doesn�t know what to say about it. Is that possible?�
�Very possible.�
�Well, you think that he hates it, which is just as bad. So you start to resent him, and then you wish you hadn�t sent the gift. Maybe it was too extravagant and the person who got it doesn�t know what to say, maybe you overestimated the friendship, maybe it�s only in your mind. You�ve sent something valuable and the person who got it didn�t really like you before, and now he�s even a little scared of you because only a lunatic would be so generous to a casual acquaintance. Anyway, if the person who sent the gift is really quite proper, then he�d be angry in two weeks. If he brooded a lot, he�d be ready to kill in four or five weeks.�
�Who said anything about killing?� �It�s funnier if he wants to kill. This is a comedy, no?�

INT. GRIFFIN�S BEDROOM � NIGHT
Griffin and Bonnie are making love. He comes. She doesn�t. Stay separate. He starts to stroke her with his hand. She shakes her head No.
BONNIE: It�s okay.
They lie together, quietly.

Comedy is another form of entertainment that draws a crowd. Once a joke is started [Knock, knock.] the audience is naturally drawn in. [Who�s there?] It has something to do with the pattern. [Hatch.] The audience knows that you are setting them up and they are waiting for the pay off. [Oh, really? Hatch who?] If you, as a screenwriter, can write funny jokes [Gesundheit.], then the audience will stick with you through thick and thin.
The same goes for the creation of moments that are awkward�which may be funny for the audience, even if it is not so funny for the characters. A prime example would be the use of bad language or dirty dancing in an inappropriate place. In The Blues Brothers Jake and Elwood Blues captivate the audience when they visit the nun who raised them, but can�t stop swearing in front of her. There is no inherent tension in the scene, but it�s funny as hell and I�m not going for more popcorn when that scene is playing.
