Tension through Engagement
Creating tension through Conflict, Understanding, and
Entertainment are the most basic ways. These techniques immediately get
people interested in your story and in your characters. But you can�t just
have people fighting in every scene, or telling jokes, or digging tunnels (so
to speak). Good screenwriting needs to involve the audience on a personal
level too. We want the audience to invest themselves in the story.
Tension through Engagement is a more sophisticated approach�one
that engages the audience mentally and emotionally. Personally, I think that
the best stories are when the audience feels as if they are a part of the story;
when the audience wants to yell to the character because they are invested
in their decision-making process; when they are responding to the story
without a second thought, almost instinctively, subconsciously. That�s when
you know you really have them engaged.
The most direct way to engage your audience is to give your characters
a choice. And then � don�t answer it. At least right away. At the beginning
of the Science Fiction Film The Hunger Games, a young girl named
Primrose is chosen for The Reaping (an almost certain death for such a
young girl). Katniss�her older sister�must decide whether she should try
to save her. For at least a minute, the audience doesn�t know what Katniss is
going to do. We have ideas about what she should do � what she could do
� but we are waiting to see what she will choose to do. We are engaged.
This scene is full of tension.
Another subconscious way to engage the audience is through the use of
patterns. Each individual scene may not in and of itself have tension, but the
pattern does. For instance, in a Sports Story, we may have an athlete who is
training to throw a curve ball. It is not necessarily interesting to see the
pitcher throw these practice pitches�but image this pattern:
Scene 1: The mentor shows the pitcher how to throw �the dirtiest curveball around,�
something that no one can hit. But the pitcher can�t figure it out�the catcher
can�t even catch his pitch because it�s so crazy.
Scene 2: The pitcher tries again � and fails. Another crazy pitch.
Scene 3: The pitcher tries again � and fails. Another crazy pitch.
When you consider each scene on its own, there is not a lot of tension. They are just basic actions. But taken together, they tell a story that engages the audience. You could even spread these scenes out between other scenes in the movie. They don�t have to be back to back�just keep coming back to them as if the pitcher is practicing all week. It is the pattern that creates expectations. Three terrible pitches in a row lead the audience to believe that this was a bad idea. Now the audience believes something, and so �
Scene 4: The pitcher wants to quit, but the catcher�his ally�convinces him to slow it down and concentrate more. The pitcher does � and throws one over the plate. Pitcher and Catcher smile: this could be a game changer.
In scene four, the pattern is broken: the pitcher throws one right across the plate. Now your pattern�combined with a break in that pattern�has given the audience something to think about: will the next pitch go over the plate (starting a new pattern) or will the next throw be wild again (the old pattern)? Instead of observing and believing character behavior, the audience is now anticipating and predicting character behavior. This is active engagement and a higher level of tension.
Of course, you�as the storyteller�control when and where �the next pitch is thrown.� Imagine if the next pitch was thrown during The Big Game. Act III, bases loaded. Two balls, two strikes. The pitcher needs to retire this last hitter to push the game into extra innings�their only hope to win the game.
Scene 5: The catcher signals for the New Pitch, but the pitcher shakes him off. The catcher signals again for the New Pitch, but the pitcher shakes him off. The catcher calls for it one more time. The pitcher winds up and throws the New Pitch into the dirt. Ball three. The catcher carries the ball back to the mound with a full count.
Now you have the audience fully engaged. Regardless of their prediction, you have now given them the answer: the old pattern is back in play. But this isn�t practice any more. This is The Big Game. And there is only one pitch left.
Scene 6: On the mound, the catcher asks what happened. The pitcher explains that he can only throw the New Pitch well if he slows it down�but if he slows it down, the batter is going to kill it. The catcher says, �Have faith,� but the pitcher wants to throw his old curve ball. They can�t agree but time is up, and the catcher returns to the plate.
Now you have created a pattern that engages the audience and perhaps addresses the theme of your entire film: faith in a new talent vs. staying in your comfort zone. Not only have you engaged the audience through a series of relatively mundane pitches, but you have also left the audience with a cliffhanger and a thematic question about life. Which pitch will he choose to throw? I have no idea. But your audience will be watching the next scene to see what happens!
Akiva Goldsman and Mark Protosevich use this technique in I Am Legend (2007). In their July 11, 2006 draft, we watch Neville�the seemingly lone survivor of an apocalypse�and his dog, Sam, live another solitary day in Manhattan. The environment is not new, nor is Neville�s predicament. On page 25, we learn that Neville is making radio broadcasts:

NEVILLE�S VOICE: (OVER) This message for the week beginning September 5, 2012� EXT. SOUTH STREET SEAPORT � HIGH ANGLE � DAY
Neville�s SUV pulls up to the bottom of the long wooden pier that juts out into the sunny East River.
NEVILLE�S VOICE: (OVER) It will repeat for seven days, twenty-four hours a day on this radio station until September 12th when I will record again.
EXT. SOUTH STREET SEAPORT � PIER � DAY
Neville, Sam following, walks the pier. He carries a bunch of fresh picked daisies in his hand.
NEVILLE�S VOICE: If that date has passed, and you are hearing this broadcast, it means that I am dead and you should disregard the following� Neville stops at the end of the peer.
NEVILLE�S VOICE: My name is Robert Neville. I am a survivor living in New York City. He sets the flowers down amidst older bouquets that have not yet been blown away by rain, or wind, or time. OVER HIS SHOULDER � CONTINUOUS WIDE SHOT The Brooklyn Bridge is broken in the center. Both halves of stone and metal spill into the river.
NEVILLE�S VOICE: I will be at the South Street Seaport. Every day at midday, when the sun is highest in the sky. Neville stands, puts his hand gently on Sam�s scruff.
NEVILLE�S VOICE: If you are out there� If anyone is out there� Stares out at motionless Brooklyn.
NEVILLE�S VOICE: I can provide food, I can provide shelter, I can provide security. A man and his dog waiting.
NEVILLE�S VOICE: If there�s anybody out there� Anybody. Please. You are not alone. For no one.
It is a nice scene, imparting a feeling of loneliness and loss. But there is not much tension. It is repeated�like our pitcher pitching pitches� fourteen pages later. Again, as Neville goes about his daily routine, we now see how and where he is broadcasting.
INT. RADIO STATION � SIXTH AVENUE � DAY
Neville sits in a recording booth, ten car batteries hooked directly into the main recording control. Radio City Music hall promises the Rockettes out a sunny window behind him. Neville flips the main toggle from broadcast to record.
NEVILLE: This message is for the week beginning September 12th, 2012. It will repeat for seven days, twenty-four hours a day, until September 19th when I will record again.
The scene continues, as before, with Neville and Sam gathering flowers, waiting at the pier. Nothing happening. Now we have a pattern. The audience suspects that the virus has killed everyone. Both pitches have gone into the dirt. By page 64, however, Neville has lost hope. He exhibits dangerous behavior: drug use and a lack of personal safety from the zombies that live in the shadows. It is then, at midday, when a stranger� soon to be ally�finds him. Neville, mind clouded with drugs, can�t believe that another person is standing before him. Neville opens his mouth to answer. Truth is, he can�t.
ANNA: You�re Robert Neville, right? We waited all day, man. What happened? He�s still not getting it.
ANNA: From the radio.

Something has changed in Neville�s eyes.
NEVILLE: Anna.
ANNA: Won�t it be wonderful?

ANNA: One of the kids on the boat. He was ARMY. He knew the evacuation plans. He was going to come with us�
Her voice trails off. Nothing good in the memory it finds.
NEVILLE:  Anna, there are no safety zones. There were no final evacuations. Everything
just fell apart.

Then Anna broke the pattern, but only once. No one else has surfaced. There is no evidence that the next pitch will be a strike. In fact, this might be the end of the game. Tension through Anticipation
The most complex approach to creating tension is the use of Anticipation because in order to anticipate, the audience first needs to be engaged. From there, they need to be able to draw conclusions about the story that may or may not ever be witnessed on the screen. You are basically forcing them to create scenes in their mind while they are watching the movie or reading the script. It is quite a trick, but an essential one for writing award-winning screenplays.
Foreshadowing is when a writer prepares the audience for what will happen. �Pre-shadowing� is a term I use to suggest to the audience what might happen. A good time to use pre-shadowing is after a character has made a choice, but before the choice is acted upon. You build anticipation by having the character explain what it is they plan to do, leaving the audience to wonder if the character will be successful. This provides the audience with anticipation. A classic example of anticipation can be seen in Richard Tuggle�s screenplay for Escape from Alcatraz. In this scene, Frank Morris has recently arrived to Alcatraz and is standing in the prison yard talking to one of the inmates, English. Notice how Tuggle lays out (visually and verbally) all of the various obstacles that our �hero� will have to overcome if he is to escape from this prison.
English falls silent and leans against the cellhouse wall. Morris does the same. From this top step they can see over the walls. Ocean liners and sailboats are only a couple hundred yards away. To the West, spanning the entrance to the bay, is the Golden Gate Bridge. Details of San Francisco, such as cable cars clattering up the hills, are clearly visible. They watch an airplane fly overhead, its wings glistening in the sun. The outside world is very close.
MORRIS:  No one�s ever bust out?
ENGLISH:  No, and they been trying for years. Every con here�s looked for a hole, a way
out, a breakdown in the system. But there ain�t any. See those hacks?

ANGLE ON GUARDS
ENGLISH: (v.o.) Most joints have one hack for every seven cons. There�s one hack for every three cons here. That way they know every con�s name and every con�s habits. You suddenly start ordering a lot of books, they want to know why. You start shittin� at night instead of in the morning, they want to know why.
ENGLISH
ENGLISH: But say you�re going to try it anyway. First you�ve got to get out of your cell. The bars on all the cells are toolproof. Each bar has six hard smaller bars set in a steel tube, with extra steel poured in. No way to tunnel out either. Shit, this island is solid rock. But suppose you get out of the cell. Then you�ve got to get out of the cellhouse. Every way out is locked and guarded. You saw what it was like when they brought you in. But, o.k., say you make it out of the cellhouse. See that tower?
ANGLE ON THE GUARD TOWER
ENGLISH: (v.o.) There�s one on the powerhouse, on the factory, on the hill, on the road and on the dock. They can see the whole island and they�ve all got searchlights. But maybe you�re lucky and you make it to the shore. See the water?
ANGLE ON BAY
ENGLISH: (v.o.) It�s only a mile swim to land, but the current makes it seem like ten. And the water is cold enough to numb your arms after a couple of minutes. ENGLISH
ENGLISH: And even if you�re a good swimmer you won�t have time to make it. There�s 12 counts every day. They�ll know you�re gone at the next count and send the launch around to pick you out of the water. Sometimes I think that�s all this shit hole is � one long count. We count the hours, the bulls count us and the king bull counts the counts.
By providing all of this information to the audience early on, Tuggle has put the audience into Morris� shoes. They know that the movie is called Escape from Alcatraz. They bought their ticket to see how someone escapes from Alcatraz, and so they will pay careful attention to the details of how this is accomplished. Morris (and the audience) knows that someone will have to �first get out of your cell,� swim a mile of freezing water, and avoid twelve prisoner counts each day. So, when Morris later says to the prison librarian, �I�m looking for information on concrete and rubber,� we know what he is thinking. We are thinking it too: �He needs information on concrete to get out of his cell, and he needs information on rubber to make a raft.� This keeps the audience much more engaged than if they were in the dark about the details of what Morris needs to escape.
But this is only part of the technique. In order to get the audience to engage in the characters� decisions or plans, they first need to be invested in the characters themselves. We need to �like� the characters. You don�t have to trust Alcatraz convict Frank Morris in order to �like� him. He was a career criminal, after all. But as a screenwriter you do have to get your audience to root for them. So, how do you do that? Three fundamental ways to get your audience to like your characters are:
�
Give your character an attribute that we can respect.

�
Make your character vulnerable.

�
Give your character someone or something else that they care for.

Typically, screenwriters will try to accomplish these things throughout the first act, but Tuggle managed it in one scene on page 15. He starts by stripping his character naked. How much more vulnerable can a guy be?
BASEMENT
An armed guard looks down from an elevated walkway. The inmates hand their bags to an orderly in a large wire cage. The men file over to an area where they disrobe. A guard turns a handle that turns on all the showers at once. Litmus sticks his hand in his bathroom pocket and withdraws his pet mouse. He holds it in his closed hand to hide it from the guards. He heads for the showers and gets under the nozzle, wetting himself all over. He keeps the mouse out of the direct spray. He opens his hand a little and drips water from the other hand onto the mouse. Litmus then takes a little soap and washes the mouse. Morris and Wolf are undressing for the showers. Wolf watches Morris undress. Morris hangs his bathrobe on a wall hook and heads for the showers. Wolf, now naked, walks over to Morris� robe. He ignores several other empty hooks and places his robe on top of Morris� robe so that they hang from the same hook. Morris gets under a nozzle next to Litmus. Morris notices the mouse.
MORRIS: You go everywhere together?
LITMUS: Even to bed. And when I wash, he wash. Easier to sleep together that way. Morris watches Litmus wash the mouse. The hint of a smile appears at Morris�s mouth. Wolf, looking for a shower, starts to pass Morris. Wolf stops, turns around and walks back to the shower next to Morris. An inmate is already in that shower. Wolf stands silently in front of him. The inmate nervously leaves the shower to Wolf. He turns toward Morris.
WOLF: I�m Wolf. Morris doesn�t reply or look at him. Wolf slowly looks up and down Morris� body. Wolf leans back, letting the water flow over his head, down his face and into his mouth. His tongue hangs on his lower lip. Wolf spits the water out.
WOLF: Where they transfer you from?
MORRIS: Atlanta.
WOLF: Nice town, Atlanta.
MORRIS: I never saw it. Wolf looks down toward Morris� prick. The water beats into Wolf�s back.
WOLF: I�m looking for a new punk.
MORRIS: Good luck. Wolf takes the soap and squeezes it from one hand to another. The soap bar slides easily back and forth, foaming Wolf�s hands. Wolf looks up and down Morris� body.
WOLF: You don�t understand. I just found her. The showers hiss, the steam rises. The guards are talking among themselves. Morris finally turns toward Wolf.
MORRIS: (inviting) Then why don�t you show her what you can do? An arrogant triumphant smile crosses Wolf�s face. The Smile fades and Wolf licks his lips. Wolf takes two short steps toward Morris. Morris drives his right hand into Wolf�s stomach, doubling him over, the soap dropping to the floor. Morris puts his left hand under Wolf�s chin and forces Wolf�s face up. Morris picks the soap up with his right hand and stuffs it into Wolf�s mouth. Morris straightens Wolf up and pushes him back against the wall under the shower. The action has taken place so fast that the guards have missed it. The sound of the shower has hidden the noise of the fight. Wolf is propped up against the wall, the water going over his head, the soap sticking out of his mouth like an apple in a pig�s mouth. The soap drops from his mouth and he groans. A guard hears Wolf and comes to the edge of the shower area.
In just three minutes, Tuggle was able to (1) make Morris vulnerable;
(2) show his soft side by having him befriend Litmus and his pet mouse; and (3) demonstrate his grit and strength by standing up to the rapist, Wolf. Tuggle also illustrated how clever and thoughtful Morris is�all attributes that gain the audience�s trust and make us care for our �hero.� So, even though Morris is an Alcatraz convict, when he decides that he is going to escape, we are rooting for him�which creates tension because we want to see if and how he succeeds.
An audience will also anticipate thematic messages in a story. For instance, as the audience begins to realize that the film Crash is a critique of race relations in America, every scene with racial diversity takes on new meaning. If a black character walks into a scene with a white character, it doesn�t matter how boring the action (ordering lunch, asking for directions, sitting side by side on a bus, reading), the audience starts thinking about race relations, and therefore anticipating actions based upon their personal perceptions of that theme. It doesn�t matter if anything at all happens on screen. It is the anticipation of what might happen that is important.
As a purely made-up example, imagine that we are writing a screenplay about race relations in this country and also about police brutality as it relates to race. In our story, we have two characters: a black teenager and a white cop. Somewhere in the middle of our story, we have the two of them get onto a subway car. There they are, sitting on the subway�just the two of them, not saying a word. As the audience watches this scene, each person will be thinking about race, about police behavior, about teenage behavior, or whatever. It doesn�t matter what they are thinking about � the point is
that they are thinking. They are engaged, and want to see what happens next. As an example, read this scene about two police officers meeting in a parking lot. Without any context, try to identify the source of tension:
EXT. POLICE STATION � PARKING LOT � THE VALLEY � AFTERNOON
The back door opens; officers come out in pairs and head to their assigned squad cars. Hansen walks alone toward his. He sees Ryan leaning up against it, waiting. Hansen is confused and apprehensive. Ryan nods to him as he approaches.
RYAN: Hey.
HANSEN: Hey. (an awkward beat) Maybe they didn�t tell you? I�ve been reassigned.
RYAN: Yeah, they told me. I just wanted to say good luck and good riding with you. Ryan offers his hand. Hansen takes it, shakes. Ryan doesn�t let go.
RYAN: Wait �till you been on the job a few more years.
HANSEN: Yeah.
RYAN: Look at me. Wait �till you�ve been doing it a few more years. You think you know who you are? You have no idea.
Ryan releases his hand, pats him on the shoulder and walks off towards his new partner, Gomez, who waits by his car.
RYAN: Yo, Carlos. We rolling?
The scene is actually from the final draft of Paul Haggis and Bobby Moresco�s script for Crash. Ryan and Hansen are both white. By this point in the story, we have learned that Ryan is racist, and that his racism bothers Hansen. Hansen has asked to be transferred to avoid working with Ryan. He would rather work alone. Now reread the scene and notice the thematic anticipation of tension. The words haven�t changed, but the subtext has.
Even though both characters are white, there is racial tension. Notice that all of the other officers are �in pairs.� When Haggis directed the film, he made sure that the cops were a mixture of races. No one is solo, except these two�who are divided by their opinions on race. To add to the tension, Hansen doesn�t know if Ryan knows why he is being reassigned. Nor do we. So we don�t know how Ryan is going to react. Is he waiting for him for a reason, or just waiting for him? But by the end of the scene, we understand that Ryan does know. His last words to Hansen are a warning (or a challenge) of sorts. And only one person is left alone: Hansen. Ryan has paired up with Gomez. Interesting choice. What does it mean? Why is Hansen the only officer working alone? What are Haggis and Moresco trying to say here? There are many ways to interpret this scene�this scene with very little tension�but the answers are not as important as the fact that every person in the audience is engaged in these relatively mundane lines and actions.
A more simple way to create tension through anticipation is to design the story so characters must race against �The Ticking Clock.� This is what�s called building a �Sense of Urgency.� The lawyer must reach the governor before the convict is electrocuted. The team must score the winning point before the end of the game. The family has to drive across the country before the beauty pageant starts. If the audience knows that there is a ticking clock, then every action suddenly has more import because we anticipate both good and bad endings based on the clock. Thus � tension is created.

The Triangle of Knowledge

A number of years ago, I discovered�or identified�a technique for creating and amplifying tension in a screenplay. I call it The Triangle of Knowledge. I did not �invent� this technique, by any means. It has been used by storytellers for centuries. But, as an educator, I find it useful to label ideas so they can be discussed and remembered more easily.
OceanofPDF.com

The Triangle of Knowledge
The Triangle of Knowledge is simple to create, yet sometimes difficult to understand. It can be used to increase tension to an already tense section of the script, or to add tension to a scene when it is missing. The Triangle of Knowledge works like this: In any given scene, there are three �minds� that contain knowledge:
1.
The Protagonist (or the main character in the scene) can know something.

2.
Another character (Character �X�) can know something.

3.
The Audience can know something.

Together, this creates a triangle � of knowledge.
Figure 11.1 The Triangle of Knowledge
Once you conceptualize this triangle, you can create tension by blocking knowledge to one corner of the triangle�keeping that corner in the dark, so to speak:
A. Keep the audience in the dark.
B. Keep the protagonist in the dark.
C. Keep Character �X� in the dark.
By manipulating who-knows-what, a screenwriter can craft tension in new and interesting ways. To illustrate, consider these scenes from three different films:
1.
An alcoholic wants to get drunk. He asks an acquaintance to lend him some money. The second person gives him the money and tells him to have a good time.

2.
Two bank robbers plan their latest escape. They are crack shots, fully armed, know what they are doing and have never been caught. They bicker about their plans but finally decide to go to Australia.

3.
Two gangsters discuss the history of falcons in New York City. Then one tells the other to shave his moustache because gangsters don�t wear moustaches.

Inherently, there is very little tension in any one of these three scenes. Let�s consider how the Triangle of Knowledge can be used hypothetically, and then use real examples to illustrate how this approach creates tension in these scenes.
TRIANGLE OF KNOWLEDGE
By withholding knowledge from one of three entities (audience, main character, secondary character) you can create or add tension in a scene or sequence.
OceanofPDF.com

Option A�Audience in the Dark
Figure 11.2 The Triangle of Knowledge�Option A
Keeping the audience in the dark is a ploy frequently used at the beginning of a film. The audience is already actively engaged because they want to understand the story. Rather than spoon-feeding them information, if you withhold knowledge you can actually increase the audience�s desire to know more. For instance:
EXT. CITY PARK � DAY
A MAN in a dark suit, with A BLACK BRIEFCASE, sits on a park bench.
ANOTHER MAN in a dark suit and carrying AN IDENTICAL BRIEFCASE sits next to him on the park bench.
They do not talk.
The first man takes the second man�s briefcase and walks away.
The second man ignores this, then grabs the first man�s briefcase and walks away in the opposite direction.
In this example, the audience has no idea what these two men are up to �but both characters certainly know what is going on. At the start of the film, the audience may actually be more interested if they don�t know what�s happening. They become engaged because they have to create the story in their minds: Are these characters spies? Are they criminals? Are they FBI agents? And what�s in the briefcases?
Screenwriter/director Mike Figgis used this approach in the first scene of Leaving Las Vegas�a story about an alcoholic whose life falls apart, leading him to Las Vegas to drink himself to death. But the audience doesn�t know any of this yet. In fact, they don�t even know that the protagonist (Ben) is an alcoholic. This is the opening scene from Figgis� 1994 draft:
INT. SMART BAR IN BEVERLY HILLS � NIGHT
It is the kind of bar where the well-to-do folks in LA go to pick up or be picked up. Lesser known actors, agents and executives of all ages. Into this bar comes BEN. BEN is in his 30s. He is wearing an Armani suit that could use a visit to the dry-cleaners. He hasn�t shaved in the last 24 hours but neither have any of the actors in the bar. He is a good-looking man but clearly he is in trouble of some kind. Although still in control of his faculties it becomes clear in the following scene that he is much the worse for wear with drink. He looks around the room until he sees someone that he recognizes and then walks over to the table where two couples are seated. The men are young execs, the girls, both blonde and busty, have very white teeth and smile all of the time. The camera follows BEN over to the table. One of the execs looks up as BEN gets close. He recognizes him but delays his recognition until the last moment in the hope that BEN is not looking at him.
BEN: Peter!
PETER: Ben� how are you man?
They shake hands. BEN is not invited to take a seat and PETER waits for a while before being forced to introduce him to the table.
PETER:  Ben Sanderson, Mark Nussbaum, Sheila and Debbie.
MARC:  Nice to meet you. I think I spoke to you on the phone a couple of years ago,
weren�t you both at MGM with Laddie?
BEN:  That�s right. Are you still at I.C.M?
MARC:  No I�m at Tri-Star now.
BEN:  That�s great, say hello to Mike for me. That�s a beautiful dress Debbie, and
those are fabulous earrings Sheila.

There is an awkward silence. BEN does not make a move and is not invited to join them. The girls smile. MARC: I got to tell you, I�m a big fan of your writing. I loved �Bay of Pigs�. BEN: Thanks a lot. I didn�t actually write it, I just got the credit. I was fired. (to PETER) Can I talk to you for a moment? PETER gets up and he and BEN walk to the door together. PETER: Listen, Ben, I can�t help you any more, do you understand?
BEN: This is the last time. Promise. I just need some cash tonight. I lost my credit cards. The money will be on your desk first thing tomorrow morning, scout�s honour. How�s the new one coming along? I hear you got Richard Gere.
The two men look at each other for a while. PETER�S friends are looking at them as are other people in the room. PETER takes out his wallet and extracts some notes. PETER: This is all I have in cash. Please don�t drink it here. BEN: Yes that�s fine. I�ll messenger it over to you tomorrow. PETER: I don�t want it. Ben� I think it would be best if you didn�t contact me again. And he turns and walks away, back to his table. Notice how Figgis keeps the audience in the dark about Ben�s alcoholism. We know that something is going on, but we don�t know what it is exactly. Figgis lets the reader in on �the secret� in the opening paragraph so they�ll know how to read the scene. On screen, however, withholding this knowledge keeps the audience curious and engaged in what would otherwise be a mundane scene.
OceanofPDF.com

Option B�Protagonist in the Dark
Figure 11.3 The Triangle of Knowledge�Option B
It is difficult to keep the protagonist in the dark for too long because they are the center of the story. But, through editing and parallel story structures, certain moments can be used to ratchet up the tension. A common way to do this is to threaten the protagonist�s safety without the protagonist knowing they are in danger.
For instance, imagine a thriller where our hero believes that a victim is being held hostage at an abandoned warehouse. She calls for backup and enters the building alone. She calls out for the victim. There is no response. She makes her way through the building and the audience sees that The Killer is waiting to spring a horrible trap. The Killer screams, �Help me! Down here!� We rejoin our hero as she makes her way toward these fake cries for help.
By letting the audience in on The Killer�s plan, the audience wants to warn their hero not to open the door at the end of the hall. Had the audience not been privy to The Killer�s plan, the scene would still have tension, but the tension would not be as high.
In Butch Cassidy and the Sundance Kid (1969) William Goldman used this approach to excellent effect in the final scene. Butch and Sundance are two bank robbers on the run from a posse lead by lawman Joe Lefors. Butch and Sundance have eluded Lefors every step of the way. Butch is an expert sharpshooter, and both are tough as nails. In the final scene, our heroes have been identified in an open-air market in Colombia, South America. After a brief shoot-out, Butch and Sundance prepare to fight their way to freedom, as they have done throughout the film. The duo has every expectation that they will live to see another day. We would feel the same, except we learn something that Butch and Sundance don�t know: there is an entire army waiting for them!
THE SUN Crimson red and falling. There�s a sharp metallic sound and � ONE OF THE FOUR SIDES OF THE PATIO The troops are sharply slipping their bayonets onto their rifles. The Captain moves quickly
along his men, making sure that everyone and everything is ready, and as he turns the corner � ANOTHER WALL LINED WITH TROOPS As the Captain approaches, this Group snaps on its bayonets, and again there is this sharp
metallic sound. As the captain continues his efficient military way � BUTCH AND SUNDANCE Crouched close together by a window, peering out toward the setting sun. SUNDANCE: It was your great idea got us here. BUTCH: Forget about it� SUNDANCE: I never want to hear another of your great ideas, all right?
BUTCH: All right. SUNDANCE: Good. BUTCH: Australia. SUNDANCE He just looks at Butch.
BUTCH: SUNDANCE: BUTCH: SUNDANCE: BUTCH: SUNDANCE: BUTCH: SUNDANCE: BUTCH:

SUNDANCE

That�s all you know. Name me one thing. They speak English in Australia. They do? That�s right, smart guy, so we wouldn�t be foreigners. And they ride horses.
And they got thousands of miles to hide out in � beaches, you could learn to swim � Swimming�s not important, what about the banks? Easy, ripe and luscious. The banks or the women? Once we get one we�ll get the other. It�s a long way, though, isn�t it?
and a good climate, nice
(shouting it out) Everything always gotta be perfect with you! I just don�t wanna get there and find out it stinks, that�s all. Will you at least think about it?
He considers this a moment. SUNDANCE: All right, I�ll think about it. EXTREME CLOSE-UP � BUTCH AND SUNDANCE
BUTCH: Now after we � (and suddenly he stops) � wait a minute � SUNDANCE: What? BUTCH: You didn�t see Lefors out there? SUNDANCE: Lefors? No. BUTCH: Good. For a minute I thought we were in trouble. THE CAPTAIN He nods and as he does, one group of men begins to load their weapons, the bullets slipping into
the chambers and � THE DYING SUN There is the continuing sound of rifles loading and�
Since Butch and Sundance are ignorant of the impending slaughter� one that the audience and soldiers know all about�a sense of tension is created. We are jumping out of our seats because Butch and Sundance are not planning their escape properly. They should be thinking about how to outgun an army, not how to pick up women in Australia.
OceanofPDF.com

Option C�Character �X� in the Dark
Figure 11.4 The Triangle of Knowledge�Option C
When the audience knows what other characters do not, it is like being part of an inside joke�even if the story is dramatic in nature. We snicker (or cry) because the other characters are acting upon incomplete information, often making decisions that we know they will regret. We, the audience, see the car crash coming and we can�t look away.
Imagine a movie where we know that a young man off at college in 1985 has contracted a disease that gives him less than a month to live. We also know that he has decided that it is best if he dies alone. He calls his parents on the phone to tell them that he is staying at school for the summer to earn some extra money. His parents think this is a great idea and talk about how fun it will be for him to have a summer away from the family. The boy pretends that he is excited about this new adventure and invites them down at the end of the summer to see his new life.
The audience and the young man know what is really going on, but the parents are in the dark. Strong emotional impact can be created with this technique. Compare the tension in this scene with a different approach, one where the boy calls his parents to tell them that he is dying. In this scenario, no one is in the dark and the only place to find tension is through a melodramatic argument.
The film Donnie Brasco capitalizes on this approach, telling the story of an undercover cop (Donnie) infiltrating the mob through his friendship with gangster Lefty Ruggiero. Through the entire film, the audience knows that Donnie is a cop, but Lefty is completely unaware. This makes even the most mundane scene interesting because underneath the routine conversation lies our deep-seated interest in watching Donnie make his way into the mafia�s inner sanctum. In his 1992 draft, Paul Attanasio writes a scene where Donnie talks about birds, when really all we can think about is whether Lefty is really going to let Donnie become a Wiseguy.
EXT. SUNSET. ROOF
Lefty tends to the PIGEON COOPS on his roof, Donnie alongside him. LOUISE, early 30s, a good-looking woman in stretch pants, brings Lefty a SPRITZER. LOUISE Here you go, Bennie. hundred on the outside� LEFTY: Don�t be carrying your money in a wallet no more. Wiseguy got his money in
a roll, like this. Beaner on the outside.

LEFTY: I�m not the boss, Donnie. The boss ends up dead or in jail. Why the fuck

The Triangle of Knowledge is a useful tool when writing an adaptation.
It can help you to enhance tension that already exists in a scene, or to add
tension when other techniques are not working.
OceanofPDF.com

The Storyteller�s Parallax
A parallax is created when the location (or the direction) of an object appears to be different based upon your point of view. For instance, imagine that you are sitting on a train watching a child throw a ball up in the air. To you, the ball is moving vertically right in front of you �slowly enough that a child can catch it. But what if you are watching that same action as you stand beside the tracks as the train whizzes by. Now the balloon seems to be moving horizontally at more than 100 miles an hour. It is no longer in front of you. In fact, once you blink, the ball is disappearing in the distance. The same event observed from two different vantage points can create a parallax. Each perception is different, although there is only one event.
The Storyteller�s Parallax is an additional layer to the Triangle of Knowledge, created by the Storyteller manipulating important information within a scene or sequence. Imagine taking the Triangle of Knowledge and creating a pyramid by adding �The Storyteller� as a fourth entity who has knowledge within a scene. This third dimension will allow you to visualize the Storyteller�s Parallax.
Figure 11.5 The Triangle of Knowledge
Figure 11.6 Isometric view of the Storyteller�s Parallax
By creating a pyramid, we now have four triangles. The original Triangle of Knowledge and three different parallaxes:
� Audience�Protagonist�Character �X�  [The Triangle of Knowledge]
� Audience�Protagonist�Storyteller  [Parallax A]
� Audience�Character �X��Storyteller  [Parallax B]
� Storyteller�Protagonist�Character �X�  [Parallax C]

Although not an adaptation, the film Panic Room provides an excellent example of how this technique can work. It�s the story of a mother (Meg) and daughter (Sarah) who just moved into a new house�one with a �panic room� where people can safely hide in case of emergency. This psychological thriller plays out as three men (Junior, Raoul, and Burnham) break into the house and try to get into the panic room where the two women are hiding.
Let�s start by examining Koepp�s February 23, 2000 draft. Act I ends with the men breaking into the house and the women running for the panic room.
INT. MASTER BEDROOM � NIGHT
Meg and Sarah run through the master bedroom. As she passes the night table, Sarah swings an open hand to grab her cell phone from its cradle. But in her haste she starts to pull it out at an angle and the prongs at the bottom stick in the charger.
The phone slips from her fingers, bounces off the corner of the table, caroms off her foot, and skitters under the bed.
Sarah turns, looks to the head of the stairs just as Junior turns the corner and Raoul thunders down the steps from four.
She abandons the phone, grabs Sarah, and they lunge through the open doorway to the panic room.
Junior and Raoul burst through the doorway to the bedroom, just a few steps away.
Meg was unable to grab the phone, and so the women cannot call for help. (Note: Most panic rooms have a telephone inside, but the phone has not been hooked up because Meg and Sarah just moved in.) Therefore, Meg and Sarah need the cell phone. There is some good tension in this scenario (our protagonist is being threatened) and Koepp is using the Triangle of Knowledge (Meg and the audience both know that the cell phone is an option for rescue, but the antagonists do not know this). Obtaining this cell phone becomes one of Meg�s primary objectives for Act II.
IN THE PANIC ROOM,
Meg is watching the monitors, thinking. She notices something, sits forward abruptly, looking at the monitor that covers the master bedroom. She squeezes right up close, staring at the screen.
ON THE MONITOR,
we peer closely at the box next to her bed, the one she was using for a night table. On top of the box, she sees the charger for her cellular phone, sitting empty.
IN THE PANIC ROOM,
Meg sits back, eyes alive with an idea. If only she could get to that phone, which must be still under the bed.
By page 60, Meg and Sarah devise a plan to grab the phone while the men are downstairs.
INT. MASTER BEDROOM � NIGHT
The door begins to crank open, Meg slips through the gap sideways before it�s even all the way open. She darts across the room �
� slides to the floor next to the bed �
� and flattens herself, reaching underneath it to get the phone. Damn, that phone slid far, it�s right in the middle, wouldn�t you know it.
The men hear Meg in the bedroom searching for the phone and run back upstairs�but not before Meg grabs the phone and returns to the panic room.
These last two scenes have far less tension than the first. Personally, I don�t think the scene where Meg realizes that she needs the phone has any tension at all. She needs the phone. We know that. She knows that. All she is doing is looking at the empty charger � and �thinking,� which is something that you can tell the reader, but not the audience. This scene has problems. Similarly, the scene where she grabs the phone is also a little weak. Yes, there is tension when the men run back upstairs to grab her, but the essence of the scene has little tension: Meg has a plan (grab the phone from under the bed while the men are downstairs). The plan works (the men go downstairs, and Meg grabs the phone from under the bed). Because her plan works, the scene ends up feeling contrived�as if the storyteller knew he had to add tension, so he had the men race back up the stairs.
Instead, however, let�s look at these scenes from a different perspective �through a different parallax, if you will. In the final film, a small change was made in this sequence of events which significantly increases the tension and improves the story.
In the film, the phone doesn�t slip from Meg�s hand. She just doesn�t grab it because she doesn�t have time. Meg chooses safety in the panic room over grabbing her cell phone. When the antagonists burst into the bedroom and find that Meg and Sarah have locked themselves inside the panic room, one of them flips over the mattress in a fit of rage, doing two things:
1.
Knocking the phone under the bedframe; and

2.
Blocking Meg�s view of the nightstand through the security monitor.

Director David Fincher is known for using close-ups sparingly in his films. However, in this rare instance, he shows the audience (in close-up) that the phone slid under the bed. This creates the parallax:
�
From Meg�s perspective, the phone is still in its charger beside the bed; but

�
The storyteller and the audience both know that the phone is somewhere else.

This parallax doesn�t change the tension in the first scene, but it affects the second and third scenes dramatically. From here on out, whenever Meg is contemplating a grab for the phone, we know something that nobody else does: her plan may not work because the phone isn�t where she thinks it is. If Meg only has 30 seconds to grab the phone, she may not be able to find the phone and get back to safety in that amount of time. Now there is tension in the second scene because we are watching our hero making an unwise decision. The tension is only amplified in the third scene�when Meg actually attempts her ill-conceived plan. In the film, she goes to grab the phone and it�s not there. Now she has to improvise, and now we are riveted. When the men hear her this time, our hearts race. She doesn�t have time. She�ll never find the phone. She searches. She looks under the bed. She finds it. But she�s running out of time. The men are almost there! Tension!
By creating a private line of information between the storyteller(s) and the audience, Fincher and Koepp created a parallax: two opposing points of view based upon a singular truth of where the phone was. Only the audience and the storyteller knew the truth. The protagonist and the other characters were completely in the dark. This parallax created more tension and elevated these scenes to new heights.
Figure 11.7 Two sides of the Storyteller�s Parallax
Two powerful examples of the Storyteller�s Parallax in use can be found in The Usual Suspects and No Country for Old Men. (Warning: spoiler alert!) In both examples, it is the audience who is kept in the dark by the storyteller.
Figure 11.8 Translucent side view of the Storyteller�s Parallax
Figure 11.9 One side of Storyteller�s Parallax
In The Usual Suspects, US Customs Agent Kujan questions small-time crook Verbal Kint about a criminal mastermind named Keyser S�ze. As Kint tells his story, the storytellers show it to us on screen. In the film, we see how Kint and his companions were coerced by S�ze to participate in a variety of criminal acts. By the end of the film, Kujan takes pity on Kint and releases him from custody. In the last sequence of the film, Kujan (and the audience) realize that Kint�s story has been a lie. Almost the entire film has been a lie�a lie told by Verbal Kint who is, in fact, Keyser S�ze. Not only has Verbal Kint been lying to Kujan, but the storyteller has been lying to us by presenting Kint�s lies as if they were true. We�ve been hoodwinked and need to re-examine the whole story through a different parallax if we want to really understand what happened.
The Coen brothers use a similar technique in No Country for Old Men. However, their approach was more philosophical, and has left people pondering its meaning since the film won the Academy Award for Best Adapted Screenplay in 2008.
To fully understand what the Coen brothers did in the film, we must examine the film�s story shape. No Country tells two different stories about three central characters. The first story is about Llewelyn Moss, a man who steals drug money from a crime scene and is then chased by a sociopath (Anton Chigurh) who tries to get the money back.
Figure 11.10 Story Shape #1 for No Country for Old Men
The second story follows Sheriff Bell, who is chasing Anton Chigurh because he escaped from jail, and tracking down Moss to try to save his life.
Figure 11.11 Story Shape #2 for No Country for Old Men
Combined, these two stories create a story shape where all three men are on a collision course to meet up in the third act for a climactic confrontation.
Figure 11.12 Act I and II Story Shape for No Country for Old Men
However, the Coens subvert our expectations in three unusual ways:

1.
Chigurh never catches Moss, yet Moss doesn�t escape either. Instead, Moss dies off-screen and we never find out who kills him.

2.
Bell later goes to investigate Moss� motel room. As Bell stands outside, the audience sees that Chigurh is inside the motel room waiting to kill Bell. Yet, when Bell enters the room�Chigurh is gone and all possible exits are locked.

3.
Bell never catches Chigurh (or even sees him).

Because of these decisions, the story shape unexpectedly ends up looking like this:
Figure 11.13 Final Story Shape for No Country for Old Men
People have speculated for years about this third act because the Coen brothers have yet to explain their creative reasons behind these choices. However, I believe that there is a simple yet elegant explanation. As with The Usual Suspects, No Country is a story being told to us by one of the characters. The entire film is a story being told by Sheriff Bell, and the story is told in retrospect. Now, image that you are a sheriff who chased a criminal but never caught him�and you�re asked to tell the story about that chase. Naturally, all you would know is what you were able to deduct by examining each crime scene. You have clues that allow you to speculate what happened, but you don�t have the whole story. This, I believe, is how No Country for Old Men is being told. Sheriff Bell is telling us the story of three men�but only from Sheriff Bell�s perspective. The details of Moss� or Chigurh�s stories are pure speculation based on clues and witness testimony that Bell collected during his investigation. This is one of the reasons why the character of Anton Chigurh is so evil and mysterious: Bell doesn�t understand Chigurh, and so he interprets him as an emotionless monster. This approach has a number of ramifications, each of which explains the Coen brothers�Act III decisions.
The reason why Moss dies off-screen is because Bell is never able to figure out what happened to him. Therefore, the storytellers exclude this information from the film as well. Again, imagine telling the story from Bell�s point of view. He might say something like this: �By the time I got to the motel, Moss was dead. I never found out who killed him or why. I just knew that if I could find the money, I�d be able to track down Chigurh.�
Of course, this is pure speculation, but I believe that the motel scene with Bell and Chigurh standing on opposite sides of the door provides confirmation of this theory.
To me, the pivotal scene of the film is when Sheriff Bell goes to Moss� motel room and the Coen brothers show us that Chigurh is standing behind the door waiting for Bell. Yet, when Bell opens the door�Chigurh has mysteriously disappeared. How can that be? Why would the Coen brothers do that? The answer lies in The Storyteller�s Parallax. This is the scene where the storytellers (the Coen brothers) reveal to the audience that this is not an objective, omniscient story. Instead, the entire film (just like The Usual Suspects) is being told from Bell�s subjective, and incomplete, perspective.
Once again, imagine Bell telling the story in hindsight: �When I got to the motel, I knew that I might find Chigurh searching for the money. I was ready because, if he was still there, he could very well be hiding in the room waiting to kill me. When I opened the door, I realized that I was too late. The room was locked tight. Chigurh wasn�t there. He had found the money hidden in an air duct and escaped.�
Generally speaking, the scene is insignificant. Bell goes to an empty motel room and discovers what we already suspected: that the money and Chigurh are gone. Yet, the Coens give the scene significant weight by adding the phantom Chigurh behind the door. The only reason to do this (and to only do it once, at the very climax of the film) is to drastically shift the film and create an Anagnorisis for the audience.
Without this scene, the point of view for the film seems Omniscient. But with this scene, we realize that the film is not Omniscient. After careful consideration, the audience should be able to deduct that the film is being told from the point of view of an old man�Bell, the old sheriff who no longer understands the criminals he is chasing. The criminals have escaped. The hero from yesterday can no longer solve today�s crimes or understand today�s criminals. From Bell�s perspective, this is no longer a country for old men.
In order for the Coens to bring us to this realization, these storytellers had to create a parallax. We thought we were watching an omniscient and objective story about three men when, in fact, we were watching one man�s limited and subjective point of view of those events. This third act reversal makes us reconsider the entire film, its story, and its Active/Non-Traditional approach to theme.

Adapting Damion
Armentrout�s �So the Young
Enter the World�

The advanced techniques from Section Three
may take some time to think through and understand. Some you may like. Others, you may not. But what I think you�ll find is that these four central ideas (Theme, Tension, the Triangle of Knowledge, and Reimagining Structure) are ideas that you have considered � but never really had the words to describe.
I don�t claim to have invented these techniques and ideas; I have simply recognized them and given them names. By naming something, it can be discussed. By discussing something, realizations can be made. By realizing an idea, that idea can be strengthened, transformed, and reimagined. My hope is that you can use these advanced ideas to strengthen, transform, and reimagine your own ideas about storytelling and how to write screenplays.

�So the Young Enter the World� Scenario A

1.

They want you to write Episode #7 (December) and revisit Sam and Christy�s relationship from Episode #1. They also want you to develop a small side story with Blu and Jane from Episode #3.

2.
The Ms. Kelly character will continue in every episode. They want the size of her role to expand in this episode.

3.
In the second half of the season�starting with Episode #7�each episode should be more �genre-specific� (whatever that means!).

After reading the short script, they want to meet to discuss the episode�s genre.

Questions to consider:

spine?

straight vs. reimagined)
�  What is the emotional center of the story? How will you convey this
to the audience in the screenplay�or will you?

tension the same way in the screenplay?
�  Can you identify the character�s Motivations, Objectives, and
Intentions in the short story? Will you use these in the screenplay?
Why or why not?
�  Knowing that you are trying to develop a story that can be revisited
in future episodes, how can you build out this story so that it has that
sort of trajectory?
�So the Young Enter the World� by Damion
Armentrout

A blindness will creep around the eyes of youth just as vitality and vigor attach themselves to their frame. The young ones are foolish, but not fools. They simply lack the attribute of experience in their revelries and downfalls. It is this blindness that sets the stage for their dramas. This blindness that makes their faces up to be so young, so innocent of the struggle that abounds in the world beyond their limited scope of experience, but also makes them ignorant of the true joys of life and the methods that they are come by. No, the young are not fools, just foolish, and their dramas are superficial and sometimes petty, yet sometimes as grand as anything that a person could have in a lifetime. So the young enter the world to be awakened by hardship and rewards so thankfully received.
On a certain cold day outside of class there was a chill unrivalled by previous lows that was sulking about the window, making attempts to breach the shoddy old caulk that sealed out the cold and harbored the warmth indoors. It was February, a month of mourning for the Earth to air its grievances at the arrival of winter by crying out in gusts of wind and frost.
Tyler was in Room 213 that day, looking down at the frozen ground and wondering how long it would be until the sad Earth was reunited with life once again. He was looking down at the frozen ground instead of the board, where a lesson in chemical agitations was being conducted. Teachers everywhere delight in bringing the listless and distracted to justice and so Tyler was called on to expound upon the conclusion to a particular reaction. He reacted in kind to this maturity and made a cheap reference to intercourse that was greeted with a brief fanfare of laughter from classmates. The teacher went on with the lesson and Tyler returned to the frozen ground.
Downstairs in an English class, one�if not the only one�of Tyler�s true friends was inking her way around an empty sheet of notebook paper. Seeing as the visual arts were more her suit, Samantha was listlessly gamboling her way around some intricate weaving of random lines reminiscent of ancient Celtic artistry. As she was doing this, the ink in the pen she had found earlier that morning ran out and she was left with no alternative but to pay attention to the teacher in her windowless, interior classroom. She had no alternative because she had no one to talk to.
Samantha was a unique girl. One could tell just from the way she dressed that she could maintain a dialogue with members of the elite society of Preps and Cheerleaders just as easily as she could explore the darkness with the Freaks while everyone in-between just looked kindly on her. Despite her social graces, she was surrounded by people making up tests for others who just seemed to be too busy to disturb. As she looked up at the clock, the bell rang and she escaped to the front steps leading up to the main entrance of the school that Tyler and Samantha attended.
Tyler sat at these old stone steps every day at lunch to get away from people and collect his thoughts. Samantha came outside and sat down with him. �What�s going on?� she asked.
�Oh, nothin�. Just the same usual thing. Why?�
�No reason. Just trying to get into your head is all. It�s cold out here, how do you stand it?� She wrapped her arms around herself and shivered with a sudden chill.
�I don�t know.� He looked out on the frostbitten trees occupying the perimeter of the grounds. �It�s hard. I mean, I try to go on with things but I keep coming back to that emptiness. Like, all I have is friends, nothing deeper.�
�Well, you have me.�
�Yeah, but it�s not like we can hold each other on blustery nights and not talk, you know? It�s more like, I�ll be there for you when no one else will. I mean, we�re not in love or anything.� He said it with detached conviction, but he didn�t mean a word.
At night, before sleep would claim him, he would involuntarily envision a loving embrace with Samantha. He planned whole scenarios in his head about the way they would kiss, how they would get there and what to do after. Later, in the sobering rays of morning, he would disregard his dreamings as nonsense that would destroy their friendship.
Yet, he would sometimes catch himself gazing intently at her profile as she leaned toward her desk in study hall. Her brown, shoulder-length hair would be drawn by gravity to be a backdrop for the beautiful performance that was her face, the wisps nearest him tucked behind her ear like a curtain eagerly drawn in anticipation of her entrance. He would tear his eyes away from her and shake his head abruptly, then turn back to more pressing business. He would always look back for an encore glance though.
The cold wind whipped Samantha�s hair about her face distractedly. �I guess,� she said blandly, �You�ll find someone though. And even if you don�t, you�ll always be my sex symbol.� Grinning, she put her arm around him and leaned her head against his shoulder.
�Hmph.� Tyler had an obvious distaste for his friends� game of calling him a sex symbol or similar epithets. It seemed to him a bitter irony that was best avoided. Sam emitted a small laugh of satisfaction at his annoyance.
When her head was against his shoulder she started to look out at the sky, admiring the patterns of winter�s ceaseless cloud cover. She started to imagine what it would be like to actually be able to sustain her leaning and not have to get up when the joke was over. Much like Tyler, Samantha had noticed that she was beginning to see her friend in a different light. She noticed more and more that she would just be sitting somewhere and thoughtlessly thinking about what it would be like to live with him. She would think about kissing him sometimes, or just think odd things. Once, just after she had awakened on Saturday, she was laying in her warm bed while it snowed outside and she thought how nice it would be if Tyler were the blanket on her bed. How he would be the thing that kept her warm. She thought it was an odd way of looking at things, but she enjoyed it.
His voice startled her, and brought her back down from her thoughts, �You�re going to John�s tonight, aren�t you?�
�I don�t know,� she said with a slight roll of her eyes.
�Come on. Who else am I going to talk to?�
She shrugged. �All right. You�ll pick me up?�
�Can�t. My mom�s using the car. Can you drive?�
�Yeah. All right,� she concurred. Then, after a second, she continued, �You want to go get some food? I�m starving.�
He shook his head No and with a concurrent gust of wind, she leapt from her step and turned for the cafeteria at a half run. �Okay. Six-thirty,� she yelled, and was gone.
Tyler watched her until she disappeared in the crowd of students. He sighed and looked back out on the bleak landscape. There is a clarity in the crispness of winter air that sharpens the eye. You can see farther in the cold than at any other time of the year.
Samantha came home from school at five because of all the people she dropped off at various houses around the vicinity of the school. Samantha never just dropped people off, though. They would talk or stop by some little doughnut shop for a little coffee.
At home she grabbed something to eat and jumped into the bathroom for a quick shower. She only rinsed her hair because she knew that once her routine was done, she�d already be fifteen minutes late. When she got out, she patted her face with a towel and let the rest of her body drip dry while applying a subtle amount of eye make-up.
Eye liner and mascara were the only types of cosmetics she used; she hated girls who obsessed over the way they looked. She hated the way that they always took two hours to make themselves look like either a clown or an untouchable porcelain doll. She decided that her eyes looked smoky enough and took fifteen minutes to search for another top to wear. She wasn�t going to change outfits, but she refused to go on a social outing with the same old paint-stained clothes on. It took twenty-five minutes to drive from Samantha�s house to Tyler�s. She was out the door at six twenty.
After dinner at around six, Tyler retreated to his room. He made sure that he hadn�t dropped any food on his clothes that would leave an identifying mark, and after he had passed inspection, he searched through his cd collection for something to listen to. Something sad and depressed. He knew that later he would begin to notice his friend and make himself miserable. This is the way it worked for him. He would begin to notice her and position himself closer to her, but would do nothing for fear that it would be awkward or ill-received. He laid on his bed and helplessly thought about what it would be like to actually act on his desires. To actually kiss her, to hold her, to have her hold him back. He continued listening and imagining, with occasional glances out the window.
When Sam arrived at six forty-three, Tyler had listened to about half of the cd. Its melancholy melodies played his frustrations and he was feeling more helpless and more frustrated than before. So he was when he saw that she had pulled up. He turned off his stereo and called to his mom to notify her of his departure. The car�s door handle was cold and took some working to achieve admittance. Her heater didn�t work as well as it should have so her windshield was always a bit clouded over with breath�s condensed vapors. They took off for John�s and turned on the radio.
He avoided her profile by looking out the window, looking at all the houses that looked the same. He was disgusted that people lived like that. They didn�t care about anything but their plastic little lives. Samantha kept her eyes on the road. She would make a right turn and glance over at his face. She could only see a little because his head was turned so far towards the window, away from her. She would return to the car ahead of her and wonder what was going to happen that night. Probably the same thing that always happened. She would start to talk to some people and notice him near her but not turn and talk to him. She didn�t want to seem rude to those she was conversing with, or rude to Tyler for interrupting. She stopped at a red light and thought about driving.
The sudden stop jolted Tyler�s eyes forward. She had almost missed it. While she was watching the lights and the cars making left turns, he broke against his will and looked at her. He looked at her like it was a hard gaze. Like his stare was touching her face. A song came on the radio and as he said this he turned his eyes to the now-green traffic light. �You know, I really hate what they�ve been playing on the radio recently. It just doesn�t have the sophistication the usual stuff has.�
�Yeah, I�ve noticed it, too.� She risked drifting left of center to make eye contact with him as she spoke.
When they arrived at John�s, they split up immediately and afforded themselves the company of two discussions already in progress. Tyler would drift away for periods at a time, thinking of Sam and her beauty that seemed so earthy she may have just grown out of the ground. He would shake his head and scramble to catch up with the progress of the conversation. His attempts were useless, he kept floating away, and the conversation wasn�t anything worth his time anyway so he left as politely as possible and sought refreshment in a beverage.
While he was talking to his friends, she watched him. He was so cool when he spoke. Especially when he was really interested in or knowledgeable on a subject. He would start moving his hands and his face would be really intense, especially his eyes. He would move in his chair or if he was standing he would change feet and shift his weight. She watched as he did all this while he made his way around the room; joining, then drifting. It was hard for her to find an opportunity to join in with him and still be attentive to the people she was talking with. She made polite noises when someone would say something to her and then watch Tyler. When he left the room, she followed.
She found him in the kitchen, leaning on the counter and staring at a red plastic cup in his hand, with some sort of anonymous cola sloshing around at the bottom. �Having fun?� she asked.
�It�s not as good as the last one,� he answered without looking up from his cup.
�Yeah. John�s parties seem to go back and forth like that, don�t they?� She carefully put her hands on the cup and took it from him. She drank the cola and handed it back. �Thanks,� she said, moving her hair back behind her ear.
�Sure,� was all he could say.
�You don�t feel like talking?�
�No. Yeah. I don�t know. I just, I didn�t know what anyone in there was talking about. It seemed stupid anyway, so I left. It�s no big deal.� He shrugged his shoulders and looked back down into his empty cup.
�I don�t think there�s anyone in his dad�s study,� he heard her say. He looked up and she smiled. �You want to talk in there?�
�Okay.�
So they went into the absent father�s study to talk. Tyler was feeling very lonely that night and was relieving his tired soul by handing Sam the typical rhetoric he always did when he was down.
�I�ll never find anyone. There is no one that could possibly ever fall in love with me. Not in high school. Not ever. People grow up to be just like they are when they�re in high school and if nobody likes me now, they won�t ever. And anyways, I already know pretty much everybody in school, and they don�t interest me at all.�
�Yeah, well �� She wanted to prove him wrong. She wanted to say everything she had ever thought about him. She wanted to tell him about his gestures, his writing, his eyes. She wanted to tell him everything and make him feel about himself what she felt. Samantha thought about making her move. She could see so clearly in her mind the events unfolding into a long, beautiful kiss but she couldn�t bring herself to do it. She couldn�t move. She looked away and away again. She put the tip of her finger in her mouth and bit down. It didn�t free her from her frustration.
�The only interesting people are at the coffee shop. Everybody at school is just some Jock or superficial Something-or-Other who doesn�t give a damn about anything important.� He had a habit of peppering his rants with mature obscenities which he hoped gave his speech a bit of sophistication. Tyler looked up to see if his grave air had allowed Sam to grant his views some kind of legitimacy.
�Come here,� was all she said and held out her arms to him. He stepped forward and she held her friend as she had so many times before when his feelings got too much for him to bear and she could find nothing else to say.
He was sitting in a big chair, the kind in law offices, and it was awkward to stand and lean over and hold him. She fell into his lap and met his eyes with grim accuracy. She could not turn away, even for a second. Her arms were still around his neck and her face was only inches from his. She could feel him breathing lightly from his mouth. She could feel his chest moving with his breath. She had lost every idea that had ever come to her except the thought of the warm blanket.
Tyler�s fascination with Sam seemed to bubble to the surface of his consciousness as she held him. He thought of his scenarios and felt his emotion toward her progress past his control. It was the smell of her that overwhelmed him; she didn�t smell like shampoo or perfume as others did, but earthy and valid. She had an aroma of ancient symbolism and the smell of gardening in the warmth of summer.
He touched his lips to the side of her neck. For some reason this didn�t surprise or shock her at all. There was a kind of comfort in it all as he pressed his face closer and closer to her lips. Every tension she felt in the moments between when she fell and now were dismissed. She had also forgotten about the blanket.
It seemed that the moment their eyes shut, the rest of the world just fell away from being. They existed alone and in love, like they had been that way for so long they couldn�t remember how it all started.
They went on that way for some time, and were oddly quiet when they finally emerged from the study and made their way for the door without saying good-bye to anyone, and exited into the chill of night in February.
They got into Samantha�s car and started off. They said nothing to each other. They were silent. No radio. No talking. They weren�t even thinking actively, except ideas moving in and out of their heads as they drove to her house. Fantasies, remembrances, brief inquiries as to what might happen once they got where they were going. The February sky was black, starless. The only light issued from high-pressure sodium lamps above the road. The contrast bore a similarity to the way they were thinking. The car would pass under a lamp and light would enter the windshield and then cause a glare, and then the car would pass into darkness. So much was going on in their minds so quickly.
In silence they rode the rest of the way to Samantha�s house, and in silence they descended the stairs to her basement room. Filled with the feeling of brimming frustration and unfulfilled emotion, they started where they left off. They would stop occasionally and hold each other. They would look into the other�s face. Neither expressed the confusion behind it. They wondered what exactly it was they were doing, why, and what would happen the next day. As Tyler was still thinking this, Samantha kissed him again and he lost his thoughts. When they were kissing, it was deathly quiet, and their hearts were beating so that they could hear nothing but their own deafening pulses in their ears. It didn�t cross either mind that they could talk about what was going on. It would have seemed taboo. They just continued to kiss and touch and stare until someone�they never remembered who�turned off the light in her basement room.
When the night�s passion had finally been appeased, they sat in bare skin, blankly wondering what would be done now.
They dressed in silence. She drove him home in silence. Before he left her alone in her frosted car, he gave her a kiss and said nothing with his voice, but gave her a look she would recall with perfect clarity even into her old age. Loss, appeasement, confusion, an awkward understanding of the significance of what had just happened between them. He shut her door, and after waiting for him to get into his house, she drove off.
The next day they made their way as best they could, not by ignoring each other, but by just trying to have things be the same. Tyler sat alone on his steps that day and Sam didn�t come to visit or see how he was. They were both trying to sort out what it all meant, what would happen, how they would get by without unraveling the bond which previously existed between them. They had nowhere to turn, not to anyone else, certainly not to each other. They could only turn inwards on themselves.
There is a blindness found among those of the young. It obstructs their vision very carefully and sometimes obscures their view so much that maturity isn�t arrived at, but stumbled upon. They wrap their wounds from the fall as best they can with their limited sight and carry on in as able a manner as they can manage. The young are not fools, just foolish. They find their drama and play their parts and in the end�past the make.up, past the blocking, past the wardrobe and the set dressing�perhaps they find what really matters. So the young enter the world.

�So the Young Enter the World� Scenario B
After reading the Underlying Material, you meet with the producers who tell you that they would like each episode to have at least two storylines (an A story and a B story) running in parallel.
OceanofPDF.com

Questions to consider:
�
Which genre did you choose for this script, and how do you plan to weave other characters and additional storylines into your script?

�

that sort of trajectory?

�So the Young Enter the World� Discussion
Reflecting back on this particular adaptation, think about the assignment, the decisions made, and the outcome. Think about how many different aspects of the short story, your adaptation, and the producers� requirements you had to consider while adapting this story.
OceanofPDF.com

Questions to consider:
� � �  What genre was this script written in? What was the spine of each story? What was the theme of each story? Did the genre, spine, and theme work in unison in the script? Why or why not? Where would you place this script on the Kortlander-Williams Matrix for theme?
� � � �  What purpose did symbolism and metaphor play in the script? Was this a faithful adaptation? Why or why not? What sort of literary techniques were adapted into the screenplay? Were archetypes used or avoided? Did characters play dual roles across stories?
� � � � � �  How was dialogue used to develop the characters and illustrate change or theme? Were identifiable structure(s) and/or story shape(s) used? What decisions were made regarding character and dialogue? What role did character Motivations, Objectives, and Intentions play? Where is the emotional center of the screenplay? How were primary and secondary emotions crafted? Can you identify at least five ways that tension was developed in the script? Was the Triangle of Knowledge used? The Storyteller�s Parallax?
�  How would you have adapted the story differently?
OceanofPDF.com

Conclusion
While these scenarios are fiction, they are based on processes I have personally gone through many times. If you were looking for the answers to the questions somewhere�there aren�t any. There shouldn�t be. There are no right or wrong answers to the questions. There may be �better� answers, or �worse� answers, but really just asking these sorts of questions is part of the creative process. I find that asking them before, during, and after the drafts are written helps to define the work and to focus my creative attack. It also provides discussion points for the producers. When these questions are used, the notes are more focused and the session is more productive.
I hope that walking through this process helped spur some of your own creative insights into an adaptation you might be considering�just as I hope the rest of this book provided you with new tools for writing award-winning screenplays.
Best of luck! Eric R. Williams Athens, Ohio summer, 2017

Bio

. . . . . . . .
Eric R. Williams has written professionally for American Movie Classics, Workshop Productions, Universal Studios, Grateful Films, and Fox Interactive. His screenplays have been workshopped at the Film Independent Producers Lab in Los Angeles, and have won three prestigious awards: Best New Work from the Writers Guild of America, Award for Individual Excellence in Screenwriting from the Ohio Arts Council, and an Emmy Award for Interactive Media. Eric is the author of the Routledge Focus book The Screenwriters Taxonomy. He is also an award-winning educator and the inaugural director of the MFA in Communication Media Arts program at Ohio University where he teaches screenwriting and virtual reality production. Eric earned his undergraduate degree from Northwestern University in Chicago, and his MFA from Columbia University in New York City. You can learn more by visiting his website: www.WilliamsOnStory.com.
